# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum II

## Funding proposal package for FP183

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGFGREEN II): Green Climate Fund (GCF)-Supported Projects in Bangladesh, India, Nepal, and Bhutan, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Great Green Wall (GGW) Countries - Phase I";

b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;

d) Secretariat's assessment;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Report from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

g) Gender documentation.

# A.20 Executing Entity Information

1. In this proposal, "Executing Entity" (EE) refers to any entity that will sign a Subsidiary Agreement with IFAD or execute, carry-out or implement an activity funded by the GCF in accordance with the terms and conditions of such agreement, including policies and procedures (see Section B.4 for information on such policies and procedures and the GCF).

2. IFAD acts as EE and will sub-contract/sub-lease the activities related to the program coordination, supervision and reporting to the GCF for IREGENFIN through a Regional Coordination Unit (RCU) in Burkina Faso. The RCU will also be responsible for the programme's coordination. IFAD will also carry out all activities related to the GEF GW Regional Programme.

3. National Executing Entities (NEEs): The Republic of Burkina Faso, the Republic of Côte d'Ivoire and Senegal are NEEs. These countries, represented by their respective ministries of economy and finance, are the NEEs. The relevant legal agreements will be signed with each of these NEEs in accordance with requirements established in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) and Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). The NEEs will sign on behalf of the local National Agricultural Banks (NABs). The NEEs will also sign the GCF loan agreement and guarantee the repayment of the GCF loan portion. Each NEE will sign on a lending agreement with the IFAD to implement the GEF grant component 2 and the GCF loan proceeds. The NEEs will sign the relevant legal agreements with each of the five LNABs that are responsible for implementing components 1, 1.1, 1.1.1, 1.1.3 and 1.1.5.

4. For the implementation of the GEF grant proceeds (Component 2), the NEEs will make arrangements with their respective local agricultural banks to implement the project in the baseline investments programme management units (PMUs). These ministries are: the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Food Security of Burkina Faso; the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Food Security of Côte d'Ivoire; the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Food Security of Senegal. They will be responsible for the implementation of the achievements under components 10 and 12 (under Component 2).

5. The NEEs will make the decisions on the use of the loan and grant proceeds with no objection from the AE.

The eleven countries of the Great Green Wall (GGW): Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana are among the world’s poorest and most vulnerable countries to climate change. They ranked at the bottom of the Human Development Index in 2019. The GGW is a region characterized by its high vulnerability to climate change, due to its dependence on natural resources, which contributes to food insecurity, youth migration and conflict! Agriculture, livestock and forestry are the main economic activities in this region. These activities are directly or indirectly dependent on rainfed agriculture (crops, livestock, fishery and forestry)! The region’s agriculture: forestry and other land uses (AFOLU) sector is responsible for 70% of GHG emissions. The majority of these greenhouse gases (GHGs) emissions generated mainly by deforestation (wood and clearing land for agriculture), livestock rearing, soil erosion, burning of biomass and the use of fossil fuels. These activities are also directly or indirectly affected by climate change and climate variability due to these countries’ geographic location and their socioeconomic and technological characteristics. The region is characterized by high vulnerability to climate change because of its duration of droughts and floods, temperatures, desertification, water stress and soil erosion. All of which reduce agricultural productivity and food security in the region. This has a direct impact on the livelihoods of people in the loss of assets, crops and livestock, disruptions in value chains and soaring food prices — and COVID-19 is also an additional factor.

7. Climate projections indicate that precipitation levels will continue to decrease, while temperatures are expected to increase by between 2.5°C and 4.0°C of the 2030–2050 period compared to the pre-industrial era. This will result in a reduction in agricultural productivity by up to 20 per cent in the region, which will reduce regional food security and lead to increased migration and displacement, especially of youth! The climate models producing using the IFAD Climate Adaptation in Rural Africa (CERA) project indicate that the number of people living below the poverty line is expected to increase severely affected by future climate change: average milllet production is predicted to decrease by 10 per cent, groundnuts by 25%, and sorghum by 30%. The region’s population is also projected to increase from its current “population” in the region, including smallholders who already experience high levels of food insecurity and poverty. This will place a further strain on resources such as water, land and other natural resources over the next three decades, placing a heavy burden on government resources. According to the Ecological Threat Report 2021, climate change is expected to increase competition for resources in the region, leading to increased conflict and security in the GGW area, which will increasingly transcend the capacity of each country to manage these issues. This will also lead to an increase in migration from the region, especially among young people. All stakeholders, including governments, private investors, public and private banks, insurance companies, project developers, cooperatives, producer organizations, civil society organizations and international and regional organizations.

8. Political instability, together with other non-climate stresses such as environmental and land degradation caused by deforestation, desertification, soil erosion and water scarcity, are placed on biodiversity loss, food security, conflict over land tenure, the destruction of biodiversity and the spread of climate-induced pests are all potential drivers of migration in the region. The GGW countries have a high vulnerability to climate change because of their low adaptive capacity and limited financial resources to respond to these challenges.

9. Climate change also destabilizes local markets, curtails economic growth and investment, and as a result, job opportunities for young people. This is especially true in rural areas where youth are more likely to migrate limiting the growth of rural small farmers’ organizations, cooperatives and micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs). The GGW countries have a high vulnerability to climate change because of their low adaptive capacity and limited financial resources to respond to these challenges. Improving on-farm productivity by providing working capital allowing for investment in post-harvest value chains is one way to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers, especially young people. Providing technical assistance strengthening capacity and awareness on climate risks and their impacts on the production systems.

10. To address these increasingly complex and interrelated challenges and support the transition towards climate resilient, low emission agriculture, the Great Green Wall Initiative (GGWI) was launched in 2007 by the African Union (AU), the European Union (EU), the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the World Bank and the Global Environment Facility (GEF). As Sahelian countries are struggling with limited budgets and funding in a context of COVID-19, USAID is proposing to support the Government of Niger (GON) in its efforts to achieve its climate change mitigation goals with an estimated budget target of US$3 billion from private, national and international sources. The GCF Acceleration Facility (GCF AF) will provide technical assistance.

Pillar 1: Investment in small and medium-sized farms and strengthening of value chains, local markets, organization of exports.

Pillar 2: Restoration and sustainable management of ecosystems

Pillar 3: Climate resilient infrastructures and access to renewable energy

Pillar 4: Capacity-building.

11. Prior to this, in the 2018, the Government of Niger informed IFAD's support to develop the GCF "Inclusive Green Growth" (IGG) National Programme (NPN) for its country. In November 2019, the Simplified Approval Process (SAP) at GCF-NGREENF Nigeria (SGA/PJ) was approved by the GCF Board. The main objective of IGREENFN is to build and up-scale the resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers in Niger to climate change impacts through the adoption and implementation of climate adaptation and mitigation practices and solutions, it will contribute to reducing GHG emissions from agriculture and land use sectors, while enhancing food security and livelihoods. The project will focus on value chains, as well as renewable energy technologies (RETs) for power efficient processing, storage and packaging equipment and irrigation systems.

12. In 2019, the GCF approved the IGREENFN (Dikoro, Fada N'Gourma, Hadejia, Jalingo, Kebbi, Kogi, Lafia, Lokoja, Makurdi, Mubi, Nasarawa, Niger, Sokoto, Taraba) and IGREENF Nigeria with similar development challenges requested IFAD's support to replicate the IGREENFN in Niger project in their countries. In response to this request, IFAD developed a Feasibility Study (FS) for the GCF-NGREENF Nigeria (GCF-NPN) note (CN) and annexes (Feasibility Studies, Gender Assessment, Preliminary EIS), which were submitted in July 2020. The IGREENFN project was presented to the IFAD Board of Directors (BoD) for approval on a virtual meeting held on 7 September 2020. It was suggested that the project's geographical scope be expanded to all counties of Niger, and that the project should be aligned with the GCF-NGREENF Nigeria (GCF-NPN) note (CN). I approach since the beginning of 2020, in coordination with UNCCD and the GCF. 13. The GCF-NGREENF Nigeria (GCF-NPN) note (CN) was approved by the GCF Board on 20 September 2020, with a total budget of US$45 million. The project will be implemented in 7 counties of Niger: Agadez, Dosso, Maradi, Niamey, Tillaberi, Zinder and Yagha. The project is expected to reduce GHG emissions from agriculture and land use sectors by 10% over the period 2020-2030.

13. To roll out this regional approach, IGREENFIN and the Regional Support Programme will be implemented using a phased approach:

   (Phase 0: The GCF Board approved the SAP012 (IGREENFIN in Niger) in November 2019. This pilot project was designed to test the feasibility of the investment model for IGREENFIN and for utilizing this to expand to Phase 1 and Phase 2 countries.)

   (Phase 1 will be implemented in the first set of five GGW countries (Burkina Faso, Mali, and Senegal) plus Chad)

   (Phase 2 will be implemented in the second set of seven GGW countries (Chad, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia, Uganda))

   The Regional Support Programme (RSP) will provide support (on enhancing knowledge management and exchanges, reporting and monitoring; piloting digital transactional technologies and promoting private sector engagement) to IGREENFIN in the first five countries and in the future for all Accredited Entities, including projects developed under IGREENFIN phases 0, I and II.

### Figure 1: Diagram Illustrating the phased approach for IGREENFIN and the GGW Regional Support Program

|The Great Green Wall Regional Support Program (GGWP)|


14. IFAD's long-term vision is to implement and expand IGREENFIN (IFAD's climate finance brand) and the Regional Support Programme in African countries outside the GGW area in order to tackle climate change and support sustainable development. The GGWP will be implemented as a separate project from IGREENFIN, but it complements IGREENFIN 1 and the Regional Support Programmes. For IGREENFIN 1, a separate funding proposal will be prepared for each country, with the GGWP being included in the overall portfolio of projects.

The proposed IGREENFIN programme has three components (described in detail in Section B.3.) and is structured as follows:

* **Component 1: Green Business Financing Facility** - will be established to provide concessional loans to foster investment and mitigation practices for green businesses within the selected agricultural value chains in each target country (refer to Section B.5., Table). Operated by both national agricultural banks (NABs) – the Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso, the National Bank of Agriculture of Mali, and the Agricultural Bank of Senegal – and the Agricultural Bank of Chad and the Agricultural Bank of Djibouti, the facility will offer special lines of financing for smallholder farmers, agribusinesses, and micro-, small- and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) (including agribusiness dealers and solar operators). The proposed investment criteria and list of specific activities are described in Section B.5.

* **Component 2: Climate Resilient Infrastructure** - will be established to provide concessional loans for the construction of climate-resilient infrastructure, including irrigation systems, water harvesting structures, and other infrastructure that contributes simultaneously to pillar 1 (investment in small and medium-sized farms and strengthening of value chains), pillar 3 (climate resilient infrastructures and access to renewable energy) and pillar 4 (diverse economic and social activities). The proposed investment criteria are described in Section B.5.

* **Component 3: Climate Accelerator** - will be established to provide concessional loans for the development of climate-resilient value chains, including agribusinesses and MSMEs in line with the GGWP objectives and the target countries' climate change adaptation and mitigation agenda. In this funding proposal, the term 'green business' refers to investments in the selected agricultural value chains that are consistent with the GGWP objectives and in line with the GGWP objectives and the target countries' climate change adaptation and mitigation agenda.

17 Component 2 Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) - will be created to address capacity, knowledge and policy bottlenecks limiting the uptake of green business projects in the selected countries; it will target Utility, Central banks and clients (FOs, MSMEs and cooperatives), with a special emphasis on women and youth. The TAF will provide support for: i) AttilaBank (an existing GCF accredited pilot NEE) to perform activities under the umbrella of the GCF’s Technical Assistance Facility (TAF), which is to serve as a GCF accredited pilot NEE for the other targeted agricultural banks; ii) AttitlaBank (an existing GCF accredited pilot NEE) to perform activities under the umbrella of the GCF’s Technical Assistance Facility (TAF), which is to serve as a GCF accredited pilot NEE for the other targeted agricultural banks; ## B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

**Programme region:** The Sahel, the Great Green Wall and the targeted countries

21. This section begins with a geographic representation of the 13 targeted countries of the interventions of IGREENFIN I and the Regional Support Programme (Figure 2), followed by the climate analysis for the entire region and a detailed analysis of the IGREENFIN I targeted countries.

**Figure 2:** The 13 targeted countries for IGREENFIN I and the Regional Support Programme

| Country | Description |
| --- | --- |
| MALI | Upper Volta, Niger, Chad, Sudan, Ethiopia, Eritrea, Djibouti, Somalia, Kenya, Uganda, Rwanda, Burundi, Tanzania |
| NIGERIA | Nigeria, Benin, Togo, Ghana, Côte d'Ivoire, Burkina Faso, Mali, Mauritania, Senegal, Gambia, Guinea-Bissau |

Source: Elaborated by IFAD (2021)

22. The Sahel stretches from the Atlantic Ocean to the Horn of Africa across eleven countries of the Great Green Wall and the northern part of coastal countries such as Benin, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Guinea and Senegal, which are among the poorest in the world. It is wedged between the Sudanian Savannah to the south and the Sahara Desert to the north, which makes it one of the most fragile ecosystems on earth. The Sahel is also one of the driest regions with some of the lowest rainfall and most environmentally degraded regions in the world and therefore, one of the most vulnerable to climate change, as the increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are expected to have a significant impact on food security, water availability, living and growing conditions. According to the IPCC 6th Assessment Report[1] monsoon precipitation is projected to increase over the central Sahel and decrease over the far western Sahel, and the monsoon season is expected to have a

[1] https://www.ipcc.ch/srcm/fortifying-the-sahel-and-western-africa/


---
**Page 12**
---

delayed onset of rainfall. The IPCC also reported an observed increase in river flooding, drying and agricultural and ecological droughts, and projected increases in meteorological drought at GWL 4° (mostly in seasonal intensities), as well as more intense tropical cyclones.

The Sahel is home to over 500 million people and its population is expected to double by 2060[1]. The region has a range of ecosystems and agricultural zones, such as the savannah, semi-arid regions, sub-humid Guinea and extensive coastal forests. It also includes the arid Saharan desert in the north and the tropical rainforests of the southern Congo Basin, the Guinean Savannah, rice and cassava in humid and sub-humid zones; and tree crops (coconuts, coffee, cashews, mango and oil palms) in the south. The region is home to a rich biodiversity, including many species that are unique to this part of Africa and livestock[2]. The region is endowed with great potential for renewable energy sources, primarily solar, which can be used to power irrigation systems, water pumps, and other agricultural activities. The Sahel has one of the youngest populations in the world and a large, vibrant and creative youth population. The countries of the GGGI are committed to achieving the goals of the Paris Agreement on climate change (both nationally determined contributions (NDCs) conditional and unconditional), by strengthening the mitigation and adaptation capacity of the agricultural sector.

Climate Change in the Sahel: Observed trends and projected impacts

4. Rising temperatures: As GHG warming the Earth’s surface around the world, the impacts of this human-influenced climate change are already being felt in Africa. Annual average temperature has increased nearly 1°C since 1970[3], at a rate twice the global average[4]. In West Africa and the Sahel, 2019 and 2020 were the seventh warmest year on record[5].

### Shift in precipitation patterns
In the 1970s and 1980s, the region experienced one of the most severe multi-year droughts ever recorded. Since then, rainfall has become more erratic, with frequent dry spells interspersed with heavy downpours. The lengthening of the dry season and more frequent dry spells combined with increased temperatures have affected the balance of the water cycle, resulting in a greater frequency of extreme rainfall events and severe floods.

## Projections for the five target countries

* Projections indicate drying and increased GHG emissions across all five target countries, the countries that will be most affected by climate change.

31: Shift of agro-climatic zones due to climate change: The combined effect of changes in precipitation and temperature will strongly affect the state of agro-climatic zones. Prevailing warmer and drier conditions in the projected future climate will lead to a significant shift towards more arid conditions, which could have major implications for the native vegetation and farming. A recent study (SEFAM, 2017) found that one in six trees in the region are likely to be lost by 2050 due to climate change. The study also found that rainfall linked to climate change. It also found that in some sites in the Sahel, average temperatures rose by 0.8°C and rainfall decreased by about 17%. These changes could lead to a shift southwest towards wetter areas, which could affect rural communities engaged in the extraction of NFTPs®.

**B**

### 32. Desertification

Desertification is a process by which productive land becomes progressively drier and its soil nutrients increasingly depleted, leading to the loss of vegetation cover and increased susceptibility to wind erosion.

Some scientists argue that as increasing global temperatures and altering rainfall patterns, climate change is likely to accelerate desertification in many parts of Africa. In some areas, such as the Sahel, this process has already completely unsuitable for human or domesticated animals. While climate change may be partly to blame for this, human activities have also contributed significantly.

### 33. Extreme weather events

The three selected GCF countries under IGREEMFIN (Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal) plus Côte d'Ivoire are considered highly vulnerable to extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, sandstorms and extreme heat. The main climate-related natural hazards observed in the targeted countries between 1970 and 2015 include flooding, landslides, extreme heat, drought, and wildfires.

### Table: Risks for climate hazards for IGREEMFIN

| Country | River flood | Landslide | Extreme heat | Wildfire | Water scarcity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Burkina Faso | Very Low | High | High | High | High |
| Mali | Very Low | High | High | High | High |
| Senegal | Very Low | High | High | High | High |
| Côte d'Ivoire | Very Low | High | High | High | High |
| Ghana | Medium | High | High | High | High |

**Source:** GDFRR, n.d. https://www.hazarddata.org

### 34. In the GCF countries, Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana, the consequences of warming temperatures are already manifesting themselves in extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, sandstorms, wildfires, and desertification. These, together with soil erosion and increases in water stress, diseases and local outbreaks, have a direct impact on food security.

The African population has been affected at least once every two years by this type of hazard. Four major drought-related environmental disasters occurred between 1970 and 2015, resulting in an average annual loss of $3 billion or about 4% of GDP, with an increase in staple crop prices of up to 50 per cent. Furthermore, the cost of responding to the severe droughts has been estimated at around $6 million per year.

Prolonged droughts have forced many pastoral communities to abandon their traditional grazing areas and migrate to other regions, increasing the intensity of extreme weather phenomena is projected to increase, causing negative impacts on agricultural production.

Programme targets for iGREENFIN1

35. Selected countries: The GREENFIN I (phase 1) programme focuses on the five selected IFAD developing member states (Bolivia, Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana and Mali). As an IFAD member state with resource allocations under ID AD 2019-2021:

* High level of poverty, food insecurity and malnutrition
* Vulnerability to climate change
* Vulnerability of the agricultural sector (including livestock and forestry) and key agricultural value chains to climate change
* Country's needs and request for IFAD support to mobilize GCF resources through the National Designated Authorities Processes (NDAPs), as well as the development of a comprehensive, integrated and coherent set of IFAD GCF Country Opportunity Strategic Plans (COSOPs) and country national development plans and adaptation plans.

Table 3: Selected countries for innovative investment projects

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 16 OF 116

## Climate change vulnerability and impacts

The countries included in IGRENEFIN I (Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali, and Senegal) are among the world's least resilient countries to climate change and the least ready to mobilize investments and contribute to adaptation. The populations in these countries are among the poorest and most vulnerable to climate variability and land degradation; and poverty is often linked with environmental degradation. Agriculture and related sectors such as livestock production, fisheries, and forestry are the primary source of employment in the region and generate at least 40 per cent of GDP in most of the countries. The rural poor depend on natural resources for their livelihoods, including forests, woodlots, fuelwood, bushmeat, non-timber forest products (NTFPs) and fresh water, and perform essential services such as waste treatment and recycling. These activities are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts.

Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana put even greater pressure on natural resources, as rural communities struggle to obtain food, water, fuelwood, and other basic necessities in the face of increasing competition for these resources from urban areas. The region is also characterized by high levels of deforestation and forest degradation, which further reduces vegetation cover. Climate variability along with frequent droughts, floods, heatwaves, wildfires and rising mean temperatures are expected to have a significant impact on agricultural productivity.

## Climate change impact on key sectors

### Agriculture and food security: Agricultural production and productivity levels in the region are already low by global standards and they are expected to decline even further due to the impacts of climate change and COVID-19 because of reduced rainfall, increased temperatures, and more frequent extreme weather events. These factors increase risks (climate, biological, price, labour, health, policy, and political). Agriculture in the West Sahel is almost entirely rainfed and relies on seasonal rains that are becoming increasingly unreliable due to climate change. The region has a mean annual precipitation of 500-1200 mm), making it highly vulnerable to increasing climate variability and putting a large percentage of the region’s people at risk of food insecurity. In addition, the West Sahel is one of the most drought-prone regions in Africa. Droughts associated with weather phenomena like El Niño or La Niña can lead to significant agricultural production losses as well as reduced livestock productivity.

### Water resources: The West Sahel has a very low water resource base and is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts, particularly those related to increased temperatures and more frequent extreme weather events. In the Sudan, dry spells and droughts lead to increased evaporation, which can reduce water resources and diminish soil moisture. This leads to reduced crop yields and livestock productivity as well as increased pressure on groundwater resources.

### Livestock: The generally human-dominated soils in the region, which are being degraded by overgrazing, will be further impacted by climate change. Reduced rainfall and higher temperatures will lead to reduced pasture availability for livestock. In addition, heat stress can reduce milk production and increase mortality rates among livestock. This will have a significant impact on food security as well as other livelihoods such as the spread of animal diseases, land degradation and pollution, food contamination and pressure on natural resources.

### Fisheries: The main subsistence crops grown in the region are maize, pearl millet and sorghum while important cash crops include rice, groundnuts, cashew and mango. Smallholders also exploit a range of NTFPs such as Acacia gum, shea butter, guinean nuts, etc. These crops play an extremely important role in the local economies in Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal[1]. Climate change is expected to have significant impacts on these crops and their associated livelihoods. The findings of the IFAD Climate Adaptation in Rural Development Assessment Tool (CARDAT) indicate that in the targeted countries, the crop production systems are highly vulnerable to climate change with maize being most affected by climate change over the next 20 years in Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal and the northern dry part of Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana. This will have a significant impact on food security as well as other livelihoods such as the spread of animal diseases, land degradation and pollution, food contamination and pressure on natural resources.

### Human health: Climate change is also a threat to livestock production because of its impact on quality of pasture for grazing animals. In addition, climate change will have an impact on human health through increased heat stress, reduced biodiversity[2]. Multi-hazard analysis (heat waves, extreme rain events and dry spells) conducted on each of the five countries indicate that climate change is expected to increase the number of people at risk from water-borne diseases in all locations but all point to impacts that affect crop suitability (See Annex 2C Climate Hazards Targeted Areas Analysis).

### The COVID-19 pandemic has had severe impacts on the already vulnerable economies in the region and undermined efforts to adapt to climate change. In Côte d’Ivoire, for example, the closure of markets and border crossings due to the spread of COVID-19’s cashews dropped a record 47 per cent due to the decline in demand in domestic markets, while demand for cocoa has also been affected by the pandemic. The impact on food security is likely to be significant as many households rely heavily on cashew nuts and cocoa for their livelihoods.

### Unemployment increased while household monetary incomes declined due to the closure of several MSMEs in key agro-processing sectors such as the production of cashew nuts, cocoa beans, palm oil, etc. The pandemic also had a negative impact on savings or resort to other negative coping strategies by buying food and meet other basic needs. This, in turn, hinders their ability to adapt to climate change.

## IGREENFINI will provide technical skills in climate resilient, low emission agriculture (crop and livestock production).

### Forests
These immense challenges and associated land use pressures in the Sahel have led to major changes to the Sahelian environment. Since 1975, farmland has doubled in West Africa (to 100 million ha), while savannah and grazing lands have shrunk by about a third. The expansion of agriculture has been accompanied by deforestation.

Forests and woodlands play a key role in sustaining livelihoods by contributing to the conservation of biological diversity and water resources, as well as providing timber and non-timber forest products (NTFPs). They also provide opportunities for sustainable production of wood or non-wood forest products and services. Sustainable forest and woodland management has been identified as one of the objectives of promoting biodiversity and conserving change and land degradation using a programmatic approach.

### West Africa
In West Africa, the land use change and forestry sector was the main source of GHG emissions, responsible for 31.5 per cent (2014) of total regional emissions. The share of this sector in total emissions has decreased from 37.8 per cent to 27.1 per cent, 22.7 per cent, 19.5 percent and 7.9 percent, respectively. This trend is expected to continue due to the growing importance of energy activities as a source of GHG emissions in West Africa.

Overgrazing and land cover changes often lead to the deterioration of natural grassland ecosystems and the degradation of soil quality. The countries most affected by this are Senegal, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Chad, Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal which are also responsible for less than 0.1 per cent of global emissions.

### Energy Sector
In the energy sector, Nigeria is the highest emitter, as it is responsible for more than half of West Africa's regional energy emissions (27.5 per cent). The combined energy emissions account for more than 85 per cent of the region's GHG emissions from energy activities. Energy is also a significant source of emissions in five countries: Equatorial Guinea, Ghana, Côte d'Ivoire, Cape Verde, and São Tomé & Príncipe.

## Frequently Asked Questions (FAQs)

* **Q:** How will the proposed intervention address the issue of water scarcity?
    * **A:** The proposed intervention aims to promote climate-resilient, low-intensity agriculture and sustainable use of scarce water resources. This includes access to credit for small-scale farmers in rural areas and youth who rarely benefit from financial services.

## Financial System

* **Q:** What role does the financial system play in supporting the private sector?
    * **A:** The financial system plays a crucial role in building resilience and improving productivity, but it remains a key constraint. It is essential to ensure that small-scale farmers have access to credit for their livelihoods.

## Other factors and a history of chronic crises when dealing with fragile small loans, uneven and unfavorable land tenure and property rights.

If the broader business and legal environment that may affect women and men differently, such as access to credit, is not supportive, then even if women have greater access to financial services than men, they will be less likely to use them. This is because of factors such as specialization, location, formal/sectoral aspect, as the majority of smallholders operate informally), and (v) constraints within the broader business environment that may affect women and men differently. These factors are particularly acute in the sector in general. These constraints limit the ability of the selected countries to leverage more private resources for the investments they propose. The lack of a supportive business environment, as well as weak institutions, policies, and financial barriers would allow these institutions to face financial transformation change towards climate compatible development (CCD) projects. For example, IFC, KfW, DFID, GIZ and governments are very concerned about the impacts of the pandemic.

### Annex 2: Feasibility Study and Annex 4 Gender Equality Assessment

In Senegal, MFI women have better access to credit than men; however, only 20 per cent of women have a bank account compared to 27 percent for men. Moreover, due to cultural beliefs and practices, only a small minority of women are involved in agricultural activities. Women’s limited access to financial services is therefore an important impediment to developing viable business plans and obtaining loans, as they have nothing to offer as collateral. Furthermore, women are less likely than men to be able to save money or to have the ability to manage their own finances independently, which limits their ability to repay financial obligations, leading to a growing number of loan defaults. Special attention must thus be paid to the gender dimension in the feasibility study and the GEA.

### Annex 3: Financial Sector Assessment

Financing from the banking sector (including microfinance institutions or MFIs) for the whole agricultural sector in the irrigated areas is not very attractive, as interest rates are high. In Burkina Faso, for example, commercial banks charge around 20 per cent**, which is much higher than international standards**. High interest rates prevent smallholders from investing in their farms and improving them. This makes it difficult to finance agricultural research and leading their production to respond to the high demand in the face of changing climatic conditions**. Furthermore, the cost of credit is often too high for smallholder farmers, making access to financial services unaffordable for smallerholder farmers**. In general, green financing for agriculture projects is scarce in the region. The Coalition for 100% Green Finance (CGF) estimates that only 2 per cent of global investment is sustainable agriculture and dedicate a minimum of 10 per cent of national budgets to such investments**.

## Renewable energy access to energy has been acknowledged as being key for reducing poverty and supporting social and economic development given the limited access to energy across the Sahel* According to data from the Africa Energy Outlook 2019, 48% of people in sub-Saharan Africa live without access to electricity or approximately 33 per cent of the total population. In the five countries, 64 per cent of poor households are located in rural areas and 75 per cent of these households are engaged in agriculture as their main livelihood. These countries struggle to respond to the growing demand for energy spurred by rapid population growth, urbanization, and industrialization. The lack of access to electricity is a major contributor to poverty, environmental and social problems at the local, regional, and global levels, including the depletion of non-renewable resources, deforestation, air pollution, water pollution, soil erosion, and greenhouse gas emissions. Sub-Saharan Africa's share of overall emissions increased from 6% per cent in 1990 to 73 per cent in 2018*. Aware of the need to transition towards a low-carbon economy, many countries are taking action by investing in renewable energy sources. Off-grid renewable energy systems and technologies can be deployed at scale across rural areas to provide access to electricity for households, smallholder farmers, and other agricultural value chains (production, processing and storage), especially in poor smallholder farmers in areas far from the grid. The adoption of off-grid renewable energy systems by smallholder farmers will eliminate some of the drudgery associated with production, raise incomes, reduce GHG emissions, and improve food security. Smallholder farmers are also key actors for the achievement of several SDGs such as gender empowerment, nutrition and youth employment**. IGE/REMPF III will thus support the promotion of renewable energy access to smallholder farmers in the five countries through the following activities: Investments will help close the gap in smalholder farmers and enable smallholder households and businesses to access electricity for productive uses, such as irrigation, processing, lighting, cooking, and cooling. Access to energy, particularly RETs*, can trigger rural transformation and the transition from subsistence living to vibrant rural economies, which could be extended to women**. Table 6 below shows the common applications of RETs for the agriculture sector in the region.

| Wind energy | Irrigation<br>Farm mechanization<br>Grounding, etc.<br>Domesic uses |
| Micro-hydro-energy | Irrigation<br>Farm mechanization: irrigation, milling, grinding, etc. and for engines |
| Biomass energy | Biogas from human excrements (such as rice husk, coconut shells, olive seeds and other agricultural residues) |
| RETs in agriculture | Irrigation<br>Millling, baking, freezing, heating, drying, cooling of products (fish, meat, dairy)<br>Water purification<br>Communications (mobile phones, radios, TVs, etc.)<br>Refrigeration<br>Lighting for farming, lighting for security, poultry incubators<br>Domesic uses |

## Climate change and conflict

According to the Global Peace Index 2020 report, sub-Saharan Africa experienced a slight fall in its peace index score of -0.3 points from 48.5 in 2019 to 48.2 in 2020. This is due to conflicts related to the availability of natural resources and food insecurity, which can lead to migration, more conflict or a combination of both. The lack of access to energy exacerbates these problems by increasing competition for resources; for example, by causing agricultural yields to decrease, gradually contributing to the unsuitability of traditional grazing grounds or drying up important water bodies. A further increase in food insecurity without any form of land and water management may lead many natural resource conflicts to go on unabated. In some cases, this absence of clear, commonly accepted rules

### 54. Non-climate stressors and impacts.

Livelihood vulnerability in the selected countries is also linked to non-climate factors, such as unequal land distribution, insecure land tenure, poorly developed markets, existing trade barriers and conflicts over water resources. In addition to climate change, these factors are likely to exacerbate competition for water and the destruction of biodiversity; these processes are likely to become more marked as climate change progresses.

### 55. Since the COVID-19 pandemic began, governments have had to face ever greater budget constraints, so they were forced to direct record amounts of spending on national social, economic and humanitarian support plans and social safety net programs. This has left little room for investment in climate resilience. In addition, many countries are still recovering from the impacts of the pandemic and will need even more support to ensure an inclusive and climate-resilient recovery, which involves the participation of the private sector and civil society.

## Mitigation

High certainty on high levels of climate or migration-related displacement in West Africa.

### Source: Kanta Kumari Rigaud et al. (2021), Groundswell Africa: Internal Climate Migration in West African Countries, Washington, DC: The World Bank.


56. In light of the projected impacts of climate change and variability (mainly temperatures and rainfall) affecting forests and agricultural production**,** and to help smallholders move away from current unsustainable agricultural practices, the governments of Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali, Niger, Senegal, and Togo (Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal) elaborated Climate Smart Agriculture Investment Plans**.** In these plans, climate-smart agriculture is defined as a set of practices that are effective in boosting yields and climate resilience and reducing GHG emissions (as a co-benefit) as investment priorities. This will involve the development of a range of interventions to address the risks and vulnerabilities of each one, as well as the benefits of mainstreaming more sustainable practices.

57. # Sustainable agriculture and agroforestry

## Tomato
- High temperatures, excess water or drought stress reduce productivity levels.
- Studies show significant declines in yield due to heat and water stress at higher temperatures; effect varies by cultivar, with some more tolerant than others.
- Climate change increases incidence of disease.

### Adaptation
- Grip irrigation, soil amendments, and improved fertilizer management can address low fertility soils and improve yields for heat-tolerant, higher-yielding varieties.
- Improved use of RFS for storage and processing infrastructure to reduce post-harvest losses.
- Mitigation: Adoption of biofuels and efficient use of fossil fuels as well as energy conservation.

## Sustainable Mango (agroforestry model 1)
- Negative impacts include extreme weather events, reduced fruit production, increased CC buildup, and a decrease in the number of flowering trees by end of century; prolonged dry periods reduce productivity.
- Rainfall variability is high—there is rain during flood years but also reduce yields.

### Adaptation
- Grip irrigation, improved fertilizer management, and integrated use of agroforestry systems, intercropping with other crops.
- Mitigation: Mango trees have a greater capacity for carbon sequestration than other fruit species; they can use up more carbon than the amount released during mangrove processing to offset post-harvest losses.

### Adaptation
- Mitigation: Improved soil management through agroforestry systems that include native species contribute to soil and water conservation.
- Improved soil fertility and reduced erosion, ideal for carbon sequestration.

## Restoring degraded land and agroforestry

### Cashew (agroforestry model 2)
- Increased rainfall variability and risk of drought increase disease; two major pests are present.
- Higher temperatures lead to higher incidence of insect pests and diseases; increased CC buildup can impact yields on some soils.
- Though highly heat-tolerant, the variety during the rainy season is more prone to diseases.

### Adaptation
- Grip irrigation, improved fertilizer management, and integrated use of agroforestry systems, intercropping with other crops.
- Mitigation: Improved soil fertility and reduced erosion; ideal for carbon sequestration.

### Cashew (agroforestry model 2)
- Increased rainfall variability and risk of drought increase disease; two major pests are present.
- Higher temperatures lead to higher incidence of insect pests and diseases; increased CC buildup can impact yields on some soils.
- Though highly heat-tolerant, the variety during the rainy season is more prone to diseases.

### Adaptation
- Grip irrigation, improved fertilizer management, and integrated use of agroforestry systems, intercropping with other crops.
- Mitigation: Improved soil fertility and reduced erosion; ideal for carbon sequestration.

# Regional Approach to Addressing Climate Change:

## The Great Green Wall Initiative

To address climate change, restore and sustainably manage land and reduce poverty, the GGGI has initiated a regional initiative called the Great Green Wall (GGW). This is the earliest international land restoration initiative that brings together African countries and international partners under the leadership of the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) and the World Bank. The GGW partners are involved in the initiative through project implementation and development, or through the funding of several projects. As of 2019, 13 countries have joined the GGW initiative: Algeria, Benin, Burkina Faso, Chad, Côte d'Ivoire, Egypt, Gambia, Ghana, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, and Senegal.

### The GGW Initiative

The goal of GGW is to restore 100 million hectares of degraded land, sequester 250 million tonnes of carbon dioxide (CO₂), and improve the livelihoods of over 300 million people in Africa. The initiative covers a total area of approximately 7.6 million square kilometers, with the largest intervention zones located between the Sahara Desert to the north and the Guinean Forest to the south.

The following 11 countries have been selected as intervention zones for GGW: Benin, Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Egypt, Gambia, Ghana, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, and Senegal. These countries are among the world's most vulnerable and least resilient countries to climate change and the least ready to mobilize investments and implement policies that will help them adapt.

GGW has been implemented by GGW partners to contribute to the achievement of the GGW objectives, as described in the table below:

| Action Against Desertification Program (2014-2019) | Implementing entity | Activities |
|---|---|---|
| BOSQUE - Boosting Restoration, Income, Development and Services (2017-2020) | World Bank | 5,000 hectares of dryland forests and landscapes |
| Restoring the balance of non-timber forest products |
| Managing, compiling, and sharing knowledge and good practices on land restoration |

# Challenges and barriers to the implementation of The Great Green Wall

1. The report "The Great Green Wall: Implementation Status and Way Ahead to 2030" published by UNCCD in September 2020 provides an overview of the main challenges encountered by the countries and implementation partner in the execution of the GGW initiative.
2. Weak environmental governance and donor fragmentation. An overarching issue appearing in most GGW countries is weak environmental governance, which hampers the effective implementation of the GGW initiatives. Challenges include the lack of high-level political support for the environmental policy agenda from the governments of the countries, limited capacity to manage projects, and a fragmented approach to implementing projects or national initiatives or programs.
3. Limited coordination of knowledge management: The lack of coordinated knowledge sharing and coordination mechanisms at the national and regional levels lead to insufficient coordination and collaboration between GGW countries, resulting in duplication of efforts, lack of learning from each other's experiences, limited exchange of best practices, stories, whose replication and scaling up could accelerate and ensure an efficient expansion of the GGWs activities.
4. Access to financing, particularly from the private sector: An estimated US$2-4.3 billion is needed to restore the 100 million hectares of degraded land in Africa. The report highlights that there are significant gaps between project structures, processes and capabilities to be in place within national governments, at the local level and among project developers. These gaps include a lack of institutional arrangements for attracting, accessing, managing and reporting on targeted flow finance.
5. Mobilizing Private Investment in the GGW: Recommendations and Actions, ICON Conference, 23-25 March 2019

## B

and land management activities under the GGW initiative could be a great opportunity to complement the somewhat limited funding that has been mobilized so far. However, this will be a very challenging undertaking.

### There are various challenges in developing bankable projects in such an extreme environment when targeting the land use and land cover change (LULCC) sector. These include:

- **Limited data availability:** The lack of reliable and up-to-date information on land use and land cover changes, especially for the Sahel region.
- **Small-scale nature of investments:** Apart from a few larger commercial forestry or agribusiness/food projects, most conventional restriction projects generate very small volumes of carbon credits. These are not bankable in the current market conditions.
- **Lack of replication potential:** The limited scale and scope of these projects make them difficult to replicate and scale up.

### Pilot initiatives have established a range of resilient and sustainable land management approaches that have great potential for scaling up, but they face significant barriers to replication. These include:

- **Limited access to finance:** The sector is significantly underfunded in general, despite the sector's contribution to employment, food security, poverty reduction, and climate change mitigation.
- **Lack of long-term investment:** Long-term financing is insufficient as it is the technical assistance that is needed for scaling up these initiatives. This includes capacity building (e.g., training), technology transfer, value addition (including processing and marketing).
- **Conflicts and issues related to governance, land tenure, climate change adaptation, and resilience:** These include conflicts over water resources, access to land, and the rights of indigenous peoples.
- **Lack of market linkages for carbon credits:** The lack of market linkages is a major barrier. This includes the need for additional funding (e.g., GMSI), global value chains, high-value crops and new markets (such as food, carbon and humanitarian relief markets).

### Overcoming the barriers to investment in the GGW requires adequate information, awareness raising, implementation of enabling policy frameworks, and capacity-building (i.e., in mobilizing finance for the GGW) and addressing aggregators such as land tenure and public policy.

Its specific objectives include (i) preserve and enhance biodiversity resources and the fight against poverty, its specific objectives include: (i) preserve and enhance biodiversity resources; (ii) improve livelihoods through the promotion of income-generating activities and the sustainable use of natural resources; (iii) restore degraded lands and soils; (iv) promote soil conservation and soil and vegetation cover. |

It will build on and support existing initiatives in order to ensure that they are aligned with the GGW objectives and accelerate the realization of the GGW ambitions by 2030 by supporting GGW partners in accessing finance and other resources through innovative financing mechanisms supported by the UNCCD. |

Since January 2021, the UNCCD has been working to establish the GGW Accelerator. |

## Closing the Gap: The Great Green Wall

### The Great Green Wall Accelerator

#### Background and Context

The Pan African Agency for the GGGI (PAA/GGGI) created in 2010 is responsible for the coordination and monitoring of the implementation of the GGGI and for the mobilization of necessary resources in relation with the AU and Member States. The GGGI has been implemented across all six regions of Africa, covering a total area of approximately 6 million km², in all countries and tailored to each country’s specific needs.

#### Implementation Status

As of September 2017, the GGGI had been implemented in all countries and tailored to each country's specific needs. All countries have created National GGGI Action Plans (NGAPs) that are aligned with their national development plans and priorities. The NGAPs play a key role in coordinating country-level commitments to advancing the objectives of the GGGI and is currently being updated.

#### Current Status

As indicated in September 2017, the current level of funding and pace of implementation continue to fall short of the GGGI’s objectives and the co-benefits of this flagship initiative will not be met. According to the UNCCD, the GGGI needs an additional $3 billion over the next five years in order to support countries in achieving the GGGI objective by 2025.

#### Accelerator Initiative

With the shift in focus of the GGGI from tree planting to more comprehensive landscape restoration and management, the initiative is now being scaled up to include communities to increase food security, create jobs and boost the local economy through sustainable land management. The initiative will be implemented across 10 countries in West Africa, covering an area of approximately 25 million hectares, or about one-third of the entire Sahel region.

#### Support

Stronger support, coordination, and engagement by development partners, international financing mechanisms, and other stakeholders are needed to ensure that the GGGI is able to implement a range of options that unlock market opportunities and capital for scaling up interventions. Financial needs for impact at scale are estimated at $10 billion over five years.

#### Partnerships

The initiative will be implemented in collaboration with a range of domestic and international sources, including multilateral development banks, special financing mechanisms such as the Green Climate Fund (GCF), the African Development Bank Group, the GCF, as well as bilateral partners, civil society organizations and the private sector.

### The Great Green Wall Accelerator Initiative

In September 2017, at the online meeting of the GGGI Ministers organized by the PAA/GGGI, the ministers adopted a resolution to scale up the GGGI initiative. In response to this initiative, in January 2021, during One Planet Summit, partners committed to scaling up and accelerating the implementation of the GGGI initiative through increased funding and strengthening the coordination and collaboration of donors and stakeholders involved in the GGGI and better monitor and evaluate the impact of their actions.

The Accelerator will be coordinated through the PAA/GGGI with input support from UNCCD.

#### Objectives

This Accelerator aims to address several of the challenges presented above by providing a common M&E framework to all partners, including:

* Investment in small and medium-sized farms and strengthening value chains, local markets, organization of inputs.
* Sustainable management of ecosystems and land restoration
* Climate change adaptation and mitigation
* Favourable economic and institutional frameworks for effective governance, sustainability, stability and security
* Development of the private sector

#### Implementation

The Great Green Wall Accelerator will:

1. Coordinate efforts of all stakeholders through the harmonization of the impact measurement framework.
2. Support implementation of the GGGI initiative through a structured, multi-stakeholder approach based on the five pillars.
3. Facilitate more comprehensive mapping of available funding and projects
4. Connect stakeholders to scale up successful initiatives and promote the Sahel as a land of opportunity

### The Global Environmental Facility investments in the GGG

76. The Global Environment Facility (GEF) has played a leading role in influencing a shift from the initial vision of a tree planting venture to one focused on integrated management of natural resources for improving livelihoods and landscapes. In addition, the GEF has committed $1 billion towards the GGG Accelerator, which is part of the GGGI’s portfolio.

77. Building on the GEF's experience with programmes using an integrated landscape approach (GEF-8), the GEF 7 'Harnessing Landscapes for Climate Change' will be a key component in supporting the GGG Accelerator. The GEF 7 will engage GGG stakeholders in fostering dialogue with countries and fleshing out a longer-term vision. It will serve as the vehicle to develop a program with potential to mobilize larger investments in GEF-8. GEF-8 resources to support the GGG will be determined following the completion of the GGG Accelerator’s five pillars.

### The Green Climate Fund Grant High Wall Regional Support Programme

78. In response to the call for pledges during One Planet Summit in January 2021, the Green Climate Fund invited IFAD to develop a regional support programme (RSP) that would provide technical and financial assistance to GGGI projects. The RSP will facilitate collective impact of individual GCF projects and programmes which may otherwise be implemented in silo by facilitating collaboration among them. The RSP will also provide technical and financial assistance for the implementation of projects presented by Accredited Entities that contribute to the GGG Accelerator’s five pillars. The GGF GCG Regional Support Programme will contribute to enhanced collaboration and efficiency, improved streamlined actions and strategic alignment.

# Figure 15. Summary of the Long-Term Vision on Complementarity, Coherence, and Collaboration between the Green Climate Fund (GGF) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF)

## The Long-Term Vision on Complementarity, Coherence, and Collaboration between the Green Climate Fund (GGF) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF)

1. **Coordinated support for major initiatives**: To provide support in line with each Fund's strengths, as well as potential synergies.
    1.1. Facilitation of investment planning: Support through existing programming processes and complementarity analysis to ensure that both funds are aligned on the same project or portfolio level.
    1.2. Develop a list of activities or programs each fund will prioritize. The two funds will aim for better coordination in terms of thematic focus, geographical coverage, and implementation modalities (see relative 1 above), thus the two funds could support the establishment and deployment of collaborating projects.
2. **Sharing information, lessons learned and knowledge**:
    3.1. Integrate lessons learned from one fund's portfolio to another through a mechanism for sharing lessons learned across institutions in areas of project monitoring, indicators, and other relevant aspects of project implementation.
    3.2. Collaborate on the development of methodologies and guidance to maximize climate impacts through synergies between projects and programs, including joint programming and co-financing opportunities.
3. **Complementary approach**:
    3.1. Complement each other's efforts through mutual support in respective Fund's Programming: Relevant elements of LTV will be included in the strategic plans and work programmes.
    3.2. Collaborate on communications, outreach, and sharing of lessons learned. The two secretariats will ensure that both funds are well represented at relevant meetings and events to maximize complementary efforts.

## Table 12. Complementarities between the GGF Regional Support Programme, the GEF/UNEP MSP and the GGW Accelerator

82. Advancing the GGGF vision will require an approach that is underpinned by holistic and systemic thinking to maximize potential for harnessing synergies in this context and GGW programmes will create opportunities to implement this long-term vision and enhance complementarity and collaboration at the regional and country level through leveraging existing programming processes, including joint programming for the GGGF countries and the sharing of information, lessons learned and knowledge (as described in section 6.3).

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 41 OF 116

#### Key lessons learned from past GGW projects/programmes

83 Regional projects, programmes and programmatic approaches offer many lessons learned. The GGW GCF Regional Management Unit (RMU) has developed a regional approach to the GGW with the SEFI Impact Program on Ecosystem Restoration - to maximise the overall impacts of individual projects.

#### Table 12: Lessons learned and recommendations from regional approaches to programmes

| Limiting the number of implementing agencies of regional support programmes helps reduce the complexity of project implementation. | A consultative process involving all multi-agency programmes is preferred to a steering committee to ensure that all partners are involved in decision-making |
| --- | --- |
| It is key to ensure enough resources for knowledge exchange and capacity building, financial and human resources in support with a clear mandate. | A common ground for defining knowledge exchange and capacity building (e.g., thematic and site of projects, etc.) has proved to be effective |
| When building M&E and knowledge management systems, the sustainability and maintenance (hosted vs. managed by various agencies) should always be considered. | Countries are interested in designing an M&E system that can be sustained over time and is not dependent on a community of practice |
| When working across a region with various languages, there is a need to allocate sufficient resources for translation of documents. | Countries have found that geo-referencing, mapping and baseline data collection for all intervention sites are important |

#### Addressing climate change

84 To address climate change impacts, GREENFIN1 and the Regional Support Programme will use an approach that builds on existing national adaptation and mitigation investment plans and on green financing models prepared for each country and completed in Annex 2. EFA. The green business projects to be supported by this programme are in line with the priorities of the GCF and the GGW, as well as the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

The selected West African countries have ratified the UN Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), the Ramsar Convention on Wetlands, and the Kyoto Protocol. The Gambia is a signatory to the Paris Agreement. Liberia is part of the Great Green Wall initiative (Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana are not members of the GGW). As IFAD has more than 40 years of experience in addressing climate change related to natural disasters, including all the countries of the GGW it will leverage this experience to implement GREENFIN1.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 40 OF 116

## B

### Côte d'Ivoire (30/09/2015)
Côte d'Ivoire's goal is to unconditionally lower its GHG emissions by 28% relative to a BAU scenario, with the aim of reaching net zero emissions by 2040. Targeted sectors are water and industry. Adaptation target sectors are water resources, agriculture, livestock and fisheries, coastal zones, energy, land use and forestry.

### Ghana (11/09/2015)
Ghana's goal is to unconditionally lower its GHG emissions by 15% relative to a BAU emissions scenario of 3.95 MtCO2e in 2020. Targeted sectors are energy, water resources, agriculture and forestry.

### Mali (29/09/2015)
Mali's goal is to reduce its GHG emissions by 29% for agriculture, 31% for energy and 21% for forests and land use, each by 2030 and in comparison to a BAU scenario. This is an average reduction of 27%. The country has committed to reducing its emissions unconditionally. It includes a section on adaptation, though only for the period 2015-2020.

### Senegal (29/09/2015)
An unconditional reduction in emissions of GHG by 2030, compared to BAU levels, with interim targets set at 7% and 14%, respectively. The country has committed to reducing its emissions unconditionally. It includes a section on adaptation, though only for the period 2015-2020.

### Source: http://www.unfccc.int/files/finance/fundingmechanisms/nocip/documents/NOCIPdocumentSummary.pdf

## B.2. Theory of change (ms. 1900 words; approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

The climate problems that the proposed GREENFIN programme will address are:
- The vulnerability of the selected agricultural value chain to climate shocks and natural hazards (floods, droughts, diseases, intense rains, wildfires and locust invasions)
- Increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events (e.g. heatwaves, heavy rainfall, storms, hurricanes) due to higher temperatures and reduced precipitation
- Climate variability on agricultural production and productivity and water availability for irrigation
- Soil degradation and desertification as a result of climate change and land use changes
- Stressors (pollution instability, environmental and land degradation, urbanization, land tenure issues, etc.) that affect agriculture and rural livelihoods
- The need to strengthen the capacity of national institutions and reduce investments in key agricultural value chains, and the contributions of agriculture (livestock, forest and fisheries) to GHG emissions

Moreover, there is an absence of a regional support programme and an overarching framework for GCF funded projects and partners and countries funded projects to help strengthen institutional and regulatory capacity and reporting, generate and share knowledge and lessons learned.

### Shift in the development pathway
Building the low-carbon climate resilient market in agriculture in the GGW countries requires measures to encourage smallholder farmers to organize themselves as cooperatives. FCS and/or MSMEs so that they can access markets for their products. Smallholder farmers are perceived as risky and although agriculture is a major contributor to GHG emissions, it has traditionally been financially excluded from markets.

The agricultural sector offers considerable opportunities for mobilizing private investment for green businesses. In this proposal, "green business" refers to investments in the selected agricultural value chains that promote climate resilient, low emission agriculture and forestry. The GGW countries have a number of common challenges on their development agenda in the following seven sectors: sustainable livestock production (tomato), sustainable horticulture production (chili), sustainable cereal production (rice, millet), sustainable cassava production and sustainable groundnut production.

## Theory of Change against the climate impacts

The Theory of Change (ToC) below summarizes how a combination of the proposed interventions described under B3 are expected to yield maximum benefits in terms of transforming the target countries' agricultural systems and value chains towards climate-resilient, low emission agriculture. To achieve this transformation in selected countries, there is a need to address climate change impacts to stimulate a green business model that will be financially viable for smallholder farmers, FCOs, cooperatives, MSMEs, and other stakeholders. The programme aims to help these actors overcome the barriers they are currently out of their reach and build their technical capacity to design, invest in, and implement viable and scalable green business plans. This will be achieved by providing access to finance, offering technical assistance and improving coordination to help smallholder farmers, FCOs, cooperatives, MSMEs, and other stakeholders overcome the barriers they are currently out of their reach and build their technical capacity to design, invest in, and implement viable and scalable green business plans. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 48 OF 116

## Figure 17: Theory of Change for IGREMFIN I and the Regional Support Programme

### Background

- **Context:** The objective of IGREMFIN is to build resilience in communities, systems, and functions that are vital to the largest countries (Sierra Leone, DRC, Burundi, Rwanda, Malawi, Mozambique) and small island developing states (SIDS).
- **Drivers:** Climate change impacts on agriculture, water resources, and ecosystems; increasing demand for food, energy, and water; and limited access to financial services.
- **Interventions:** IGREMFIN will support the development of climate-resilient agricultural systems through improved land management practices, increased use of climate-smart technologies (CSTs), and enhanced access to finance.

### Theory of Change

#### Pathway Risk
1. **Funding Risk**
2. **Implementation Risk**
3. **Operational Risk**

#### Long-term results
- Increased productivity in agriculture and livestock (+$800 million per year)
- Improved food security, nutrition, and livelihoods for 5.4 million people (6.7% of the population)

### Results

1. **Increased agricultural production and income**
2. **Improved access to finance**

### Outputs
1. **Financially viable green business models in agriculture and livestock sectors**
2. **Technical assistance provided by IGREMFIN, Ministry, cooperatives, and other stakeholders**
3. **Creation of a regional support programme for green financing**
4. **Institutional and regulatory framework for the adoption of climate resilient, low-carbon agricultural practices**

### Activities
1. **Window 1 (adaptation):** Finance and technical assistance to promote climate-resilient agricultural practices in the selected VCA areas.
2. **Window 2 (mitigation):** Finance and technical assistance to promote climate-smart agriculture and mitigation land design and management.

#### Component I: Green Business Financing Facility
- Provides financial support for green business models, including grants, loans, and equity investments.

#### Component II: Technical Assistance Facility
- Provides technical assistance in areas such as capacity building, market access, and policy advocacy.

#### Component III: Regional Support Programme
- Aims to enhance the institutional framework of national and regional climate change programmes.

## Table 16: Preliminary selected sustainable value chains

### Climate risks
- **Cattle**
  - Projected increases in temperature, spatial variability and rainfall frequency and length of dry spells will reduce food and water availability.
  - Reduced feed and water availability contribute to livestock mortality, affect milk production, family income and livelihoods. During major droughts, cattle losses can range from 60-70% to 25%, thus leading to the degradation of pastures.

## B

### Sustainable horticulture and vegetable production

#### Tomato
- High temperatures, excess water, and low nutrient levels can reduce productivity.
- High humidity increases the risk of disease in the market and conserves moisture in the soil, making it highly perishable. Climate change also increases incidence of diseases.

#### 1,500 tomato green businesses
- Studies show significant declines in yield due to high temperatures, high humidity, and water stress.
- High temperatures can reduce fruit size, reproduction, with a reduction of about that. Lack of water reduces the formation of flowers and pods, leading to reduced yields reported poor yields due to climate changes.

### Sustainable mangoes with more species (agroforestry model 1)
- Negative impacts of high water stress and low soil moisture on flower production.
- High temperatures can lead to a rapid decline in fruit set if flowering induction days end by mid-June. Reduced flowering productivity through reduced flowering period – may also reduce yields.

#### 6,000 mango green businesses
- Influence of climate change on pest, disease incidence, and quality

### Restoring degraded land and agriculture

#### Cashew trees with native grasses (agroforestry model 2)
- High temperatures, high humidity, and low soil fertility can lead to reduced yields.
- High temperatures increase in humidity can cause leaf scorch and diseases. Two species of grass have been found to reduce this effect.

#### 6,000 cashew green businesses
- Adaptation: Drip irrigation, erosion control practices, and tree planting systems; intercropping systems; intercropping systems

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 49 OF 116

| Sustainable Rice (community-based model) |
| --- |
| Favourable habitats of rice are projected to decline by 75% (RCPs) by the end of this century due to heavy rainfall, drought, and increasing temperatures. Higher temperatures and more frequent dry spells will shift wheat, sorghum, and millet production growth, regeneration, and production levels. |
| 3,000 small scale rice farmers and businesses |
| Adaptation: Climate-smart agriculture as assessed through the adoption of sustainable forest management techniques. |
| Mitigation: Rice straw value chain development to reduce the carbon footprint of rice straw (RCPs). Rice straw has a negative carbon footprint compared to CO2 (IND-GEA, 2018). The use of straw helps reduce greenhouse gas emissions and soil erosion. |
| Sustainable Rice production |
| Increasing climate variability (variations) and drought growing severity increases the risk of prolonged drought, heat waves, extreme storms and floods, diseases, causing Autotoxicity, lodging failure, which can lead to yield loss in rice production; SLR increases salinity and salinization |
| 20,000 ha green businesses |
| Estimated reductions in rice yields range from 85% in irrigated rice fields during the dry season and up to 100% in rainfed areas. |
| Adaptation: SRC improved short-cycle rice varieties; irrigation management; reduced tillage; drainage and flooding; soil erosion control. |
| Mitigation: Adoption of RETA to power irrigation pumps, reducing water application, thus reducing greenhouse gas emissions from flood irrigation. |
| Sustainable Cassava production |
| Though resilient to drought stressors, cassava is highly susceptible to pests and diseases. |
| 18,000 small scale businesses |
| Over a third of cassava yield is lost every year due to pest damage which is exacerbated by higher temperatures. |
| Adaptation: Efficient irrigation management; interrupting with cover crops and intercropping (organic) fertilizer and pest control. |
| Mitigation: Improved water use efficiency, more effective water application, thus increasing farmers' incomes and livelihoods. |

# Sustainable cereal crop production

## Sustainable Groundnut production

* Rough productivity drought resistant: 20–35% higher yields under prolonged drought limit variability in grain yield and income for smallholder businesses.
* Reproduction due to climate change: 31% found to be more than 84%, higher temperatures are the most dramatic impact on reproduction, which can lead to unreliable rain, poor yields, and disease.

## Sustainable Millet production

* Though well-suited to hot areas, productivity increases with temperature up to a critical point of around 27°C. Yield is predicted to decline by about 10% over next 5 years and 15–25% in the long term as climate variability contributes to increased temperatures during flowering, which can cause crop failure.
* Production predicted to decrease on average by 30% over next 20 years.

## Control sustainable land management

* Mitigation: Compensatory measures for adoption of RETs to reduce soil erosion and irrigation.
* Adaptation: More resilient production systems (e.g., short-cycle varieties, intercropping, stone bunding, water harvesting systems, climate change adaptation techniques).

## Mitigation emission reduction due to use of RETs

* Adaptation: More resilient production systems (e.g., short-cycle varieties, intercropping, stone bunding, water harvesting systems, climate change adaptation techniques).
* Mitigation: As a co-benefit, GHG emission intensity can be reduced by using organic fertilizers. Solar energy and improved irrigation power drip irrigation systems.

## More resilient production and water harvesting (Zai pits) to reduce soil erosion and improve water management.

* Adaptation: Climate-resilient ag practices such as intercropping and water harvesting (Zai pits) to reduce soil erosion and improve water management.
* Mitigation: Use of RETs can increase crop yields and increase adoption of RETs.

## Theory of Change of Regional Support Programme

98. Several GCF projects and programmes are already being implemented in the GGW countries and new projects are being elaborated by Direct and Internationally Accredited Entities. These projects contribute to achieving the GGW's objectives by implementing climate-smart practices (e.g., agroforestry, intercropping, water harvesting, soil conservation, landscape restoration, climate-smart infrastructure, capacity building and institutional development). However, in the absence of a regional support programme, there is limited coordination among these projects, which results in missed opportunities for improved delivery of the GCF mandate. The GGW Regional Support Programme will address:

- Lack of coherence
- Duplication of activities by AEUs due to limited awareness of AEU and project staff of similar topics or activities being implemented in different countries by GCF and other projects including those supported by the GEF.
- Limited evidence-based knowledge generated and tailored to specific audiences to inform GCF project development.
