### B

#### Increased collaboration among AGS, countries and the GCF due to limited exchanges of knowledge on GCF project implementation and available studies (e.g., climate analysis, gender assessments, etc.)

- **NDAAs** are not a substitute for the development of a comprehensive understanding of the location of GCF, GEF and other resources that can contribute to the achievement of the GGW objectives resulting in a duplication of projects and activities. NDAAs are not intended as a substitute for the development of a comprehensive understanding of the location of GCF, GEF and other resources that could help them to better plan their respective Country Work Plan to the GCF project, iteration of their NDAs or OCPs.

- **Innovative solutions**, in particular digital solutions, to addressing climate change in value chains and landscape restoration portfolios.

- **GGW innovation** and digital solutions are increasingly recognized as a strategic path for addressing climate change impacts, the ecosystem to support innovation and digital solutions developers is in its very early stage of development, meaning considerable investment will be required over time. 

500. The benefits from the implementation of the knowledge management and innovation activities will contribute to several outcomes of the GCF Updated Strategic Plan (see Figure 18 below) by creating a transparent and collaborative environment for sharing knowledge and lessons learned, ensuring that best practices are captured, analyzed and shared with specific audiences to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of GCF projects, as documented in the GCF Knowledge Management Framework. The KM activities will also contribute to increasing the capacity of stakeholders across the region to adopt and scale up innovative and digital solutions through coalitions. Increased adoption and scaling-up of innovative and digital solutions will help to increase the impact of GCF investments by building capacities to replicate, streamline and scale up climate activities and investments that have proven effective in similar contexts.

#### To address the challenges mentioned above, the Regional Support Programme (RSP) will improve access to best practices, encourage learning and foster innovation and digital transformation across Great Green Wall countries so as to increase their capacity to implement GCF projects. The RSP is a regional programme that will be implemented by the GCF Secretariat geared towards building the knowledge and systems necessary to identify transformational pathways for the GGW area. The RSP will be designed in close collaboration with the AGS, countries and other stakeholders, including the responsibilities of the corresponding AGs. It aims to bridge through a structured framework to ensure that project implementation is aligned with the GCF’s strategic objectives and priorities. The RSP will also provide support for GEF projects and other entities contributing to tackling climate change in the GGW. The GCF RSP will be uniquely placed as it will have access to the knowledge, lessons learned and best practices from the GCF portfolio that can help increase the uptake of innovations and digital solutions developed by other stakeholders in current and future GCF operations.

## Figure 19: Theory of Change of GCF Regional Support Programme

### B.3 Project/programme description (max 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

**102.** The overall objective of this national flagship programme (GCRENFIN) is to build resilience in the five target countries (three from the GOW [Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal] plus Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana) by: providing support to selected local agricultural commercial banks (LACBs), cooperatives, women and youth-led organisations and MSMEs; addressing capacity-building, knowledge and policy gaps in these countries; enhancing the collective impact of all GCF funded projects, including GCRENFIN 1 through the establishment of a common platform for project coordination and information sharing; promoting investment and promoting innovation at scale to contribute to the improved resilience of ecosystems. The flagship programme will be implemented by Direct and International partners (DIPs) in Burkina Faso, Mali, Senegal, Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana with a potential total GHG emission reduction target of 5,637,184 metric tCO2e from the use of renewable energy technologies (RETs), improved agricultural practices and climate-smart development practices. The mitigation potential represents 3.3 per cent of the GFW emission reduction target (290 MCO2).

**103.** The Regional Support Programme covers all GCF projects and programmes being implemented by Direct and International partners in Burkina Faso, Mali, Senegal, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Ethiopia, Kenya, Uganda, Nigeria, Senegal and Sudan.

**104.** Programme beneficiaries: The programme will directly benefit 378,600 producer farmers who must be organized in farmer groups or cooperatives, as well as over 2,948,000 people indirectly, of which 50 per cent are women and 50 per cent youth by increasing their climate resilience through access to affordable long-term green loans. The programme will also benefit commercial banks (Jattipenwa Bank and the Islamic Bank of Senegal) and potential LACB partners such as MFIs, cooperatives, women-led organisations and MSMEs that are either identified or forecasted to support climate resilient, low emission development during the implementation of the programme.

**105.** The direct beneficiaries of the Regional Support Programme include: GCF National Designated Authorities; GOW National Fiscal Points and regional offices; GEF national focal points; Accredited Entities; country project teams and key

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE OF 116

#### B

country states implementing GCF country projects under the GGW Regional Support Programme. Indirect beneficiaries include the total number of beneficiaries reached by each of the projects funded under the GGWP RSP.

The programme has three components: (i) Green Business Financing Facility (Loan); (ii) Technical Assistance Facility (TAF); and (iii) The Regional Support Programme (Grant). This programme is in line with the five of the eight global climate change adaptation priorities as outlined by the National Determined Contributions (NDCs), as well as all local climate policy initiatives as outlined under Section D.5.

**Component 1 - Green Business Financing Facility (Pillars 2, 3 and 4 of the GGWP Accelerator)** will be established to provide concessional financing for smallholder farmers, cooperatives and MSMEs (including agriculture dealers and photovoltaic operators) to finance the adoption of best practices in climate change adaptation. The facility will support projects that address the most pressing climate hazards such floods, water scarcity, wildfire and drought, among others. These include, for instance: efficient irrigation systems; improved crop varieties; early warning systems; improved livestock management; nature-based solutions and mitigation measures such as solar energy to power the selected value chains, among others. The facility will provide concessional financing at a maximum of 75% of the project cost with a maximum concessional rates to promote adaptation practices, and the remaining EUR24, 657 million of GCF resources will be used for technical assistance.

The beneficiaries of the loans (Component 1 are: FOs, cooperatives and MSMEs. The sub-borrowers include also Attijariwafa Bank which operates only in Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Mali and Senegal; the Islamic Bank of Senegal which operates only in Senegal; and the Islamic Bank of Gambia which operates only in Gambia.

Eligibility criteria for Attijariwafa Bank: the Islamic Bank of Senegal and the IFI to access loan proceeds are described in Annex 21 Operations Manual and in the project document.

**Criteria for the selection of sub-borrowers under each financing window of Component 1:**

* **Definition of smallholder farmers:** The definition of smallholder agriculture varies from one country to another. For this programme, the mostly widely used definition of smallholder farmers was adopted, which is farmers who farm less than 2 hectares.

**Definition of Farmer Organizations (FOs):**

These organizations shall have:
(i) a defined membership,
(ii) a purpose for assembling, and
(iii) an organizational structure in place to support members pursuing individual and collective interests.

They may differ in their size (ranging from small groups to large national or local associations), function (e.g., producer, input supplier, or local development advocate), and legal designation (e.g., registered as cooperatives, clubs, groups or associations, among others). In the host country and the largest regions in general, they are often organized into federations or confederations of smallholder farmers.

**Definition of cooperative:** An autonomous association of people who come together voluntarily to meet common economic, social and cultural aspirations and needs within a collective enterprise where power is exercised democratically. Cooperatives are owned by their members and governed by elected representatives. They provide services that benefit the members and their communities. Agricultural cooperatives allow their members to seize new economic opportunities by offering them access to markets and resources in the agricultural sector.

**General definition of MSMEs:**

The definition for MSMEs is described in Tables 18 and 19 below.

### Selection Criteria for MSEMs

- Must be an officially registered agriculture enterprise, with more than 51% of its assets, holdings or shares owned by women. This will be assessed and validated with firm registration information from the relevant public registry.
- At least 30 percent of the board of director members or individuals in senior management positions are women.
- Minimum firm size of three employees, of which 60 percent are women.
- Activities must be carried out along the agricultural value chain.
- Record track of loan repayment.
- Bank account for the firm for more than two years.
- Records and bookkeeping systems in place.
- Agreements with at least ten farmers.
- Any other characteristics deemed necessary by the LNUA, subject to the Executing Entity's confirmation.

### For Non-MSEMs

- To be an officially registered agriculture enterprise with more than 51% of its assets, holdings or shares owned by youth, defined as individuals between 18 and 35 years of age. This will be assessed and validated based on firm registration information from the relevant public registry.
- At least 30 percent of board of director members or individuals in senior management positions are youth.
- Minimum firm size of three employees, of which 60 percent are youth.
- Activities must be carried out along the agricultural value chain.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GROUNDWORK PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 81 OF 116

#### Type of loans:
- **Farm operating loans:** loans for purchase of farm inputs and equipment with a renewable annual through a simple loan repayment.
- **Investment loans:** For setting up and/or expanding agricultural activities or restructuring an agricultural business. Tenor: Up to three (3) years starting from the date of disbursement, repayable in equal monthly installments between the selected banks and a sub-borrower.

#### Type of loans:
- **Direct investment loan for concrete projects:** loans for construction of income generation and high return on investment projects.
- **Working capital loans for FOBs, traders, retailers, installers and solar electricity service providers:** loans for purchase of solar panel equipment and systems including installation.
- **Loans for land restoration for viable agricultural activities:** loans to farmers.
- **Loans in NTFPs:** loans to farmers, traders, retailers, installers and solar electricity service providers.

#### Lending terms:
- **Based on each project’s repayment period:**
  - Repayment begins in less than a year with an interest rate of more than 10%.
  - Repayment begins between 2 years and 5 years with an interest rate of up to 8%.

**Eligible local national agricultural banks (LNABs)**

122. Eligible banks for the implementation of the GCF loan process (Component 1) that have been subject to capacity assessments and are listed in Annex I are:

They will be the primary recipients of the GCF loan proceeds.

#### Eligible Intermediary Financial Institutions (IFIs)

123. IFIs are selected following the Accredited Entity's procedures, and criteria and loans will be provided to such IFIs in accordance with the same requirements stipulated in the AMF and the FAA. The same conditions used for the LNABs will be applied to IFIs.

#### Commercial banks and microfinance institutions

124. Definition of commercial bank: A commercial bank is a financial institution whose basic activities are oriented mainly towards providing services such as savings, credit, businesses or public utilities. They can be physical establishments (branches, distributors) or virtual establishments (online or remote presence only). Their capital is held by private individuals and/or institutions, and they provide services to large-scale agriculture, agribusinesses, and smallholder farming in the host countries.

125. Definition of microfinance institutions (MFI): A microfinance institution is a local structure (NGO, association, etc.) that provides financial services to a population with access to the traditional banking system. MFI is mainly offer microlent, but also some other offer types of services such as savings, micro-insurance, support and training enterprises, etc., in accordance with the same conditions used for LNABs.

126. The initial commercial banks eligible to implement the loan process (Component 1) are: the Islamic Bank for Senegal and the Attijariwafa bank.

#### Eligible Intermediary Financial Institutions (IFIs)

127. IFIs may be private or public banks or MFIs that will act as intermediaries between the eligible LNABs under section II and the final beneficiaries and will ensure that financial resources are channelled between them in accordance with the same conditions used for LNABs. The initial commercial banks listed above (Component 1) can also enter into partnership with IFIs only if areas where a LNAB does not have any representation in targeted rural areas.

#### Eligibility criteria for selection of IFIs

- Have extensive experience in deploying financing towards economic sectors that could potentially include agriculture.
- Have a demonstrable track record of working with MSMEs, cooperatives, FVs and smallholders in the target countries.
- Must be licensed by the Central Bank of West African States (CEDEAO) to operate in Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Mali and Senegal or by the Central Bank of Ghana.
- Have a track record of providing financial services to MSMEs, cooperatives, FVs and smallholders and the beneficiaries and operational track record of loan repayments.
- Must have a portfolio of programmes dedicated to agriculture (livestock, agribusiness, food processing and grain).
- Must undertake financial services in the programme's target areas where selected LNABs are not represented.
- Must be able to provide financial services to MSMEs, cooperatives, FVs and smallholders and the beneficiaries and operational track record of loan repayments.
- Any other criteria deemed necessary by LNABs and aligned with the GCF.

129. The analysis for an IFI's eligibility to the green lines of credit will be carried out during the preparation and analysis stage of each LNAB's project cycle. In this stage, the IFI's credit worthiness will be analysed according to the type of institution it is.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 80 OF 116

B

## Using a credit risk methodology to assign it a credit quota which it may later use via the different differentiated credit programmes.

### 130. Each one of the above credit risk methodologies analyses areas such as the institution's capital, the quality of its assets, liquidity and profitability. For example, in the case of the IFIs, the most important indicator is their RIS's credit rating.

### Among the most important indicators there are financial vulnerability and delinquency, which reflect the quality of the institution's assets, the health and performance of the projects included on such institutions' balance sheets.

### 132. Also, environmental and social risk analysis will be carried out to ensure that the impact of the IFIs' operations is studied to mitigate environmental and social risks. Such analyses take into account two types of impacts: direct and indirect. Direct impacts are those related to the projects included in the IFI's main portfolio.

### 132. An environmental and social risk analysis will be carried out for each financial institution. It will review the size and probability of occurrence of adverse effects on the environment or society, as well as the likelihood that these adverse effects can be mitigated by the risks, as well as any additional measures that will need to be established to minimize, mitigate or compensate for such adverse effects.

### 132. The environmental and social risk analysis will also assess the effectiveness of the mitigation measures that the IFI shall take to manage the social and environmental risks and/or impacts stemming from its operations and projects. This assessment is carried out by an independent third party, which is appointed by the IFI's board.

### 132. The environmental and social risk analysis will be submitted to the IFI's credit committee for approval, and its credit rating is submitted to the LARs' credit committees, which is composed by different technical areas of the LARs, in order to determine whether or not the IFIs are financially viable.

### 132. The LARs is responsible for managing the direct relationship with the IFI and the underlying financing operations – from the identification and preparation stage until the loan is repaid in full – in order to stay adequately and timely informed of all aspects related to the financial institution's creditworthiness, as well as the risks associated with its liabilities of borrowers of the same gender, nature or type. In no case shall such credits, regardless of their nature, have a term exceeding 10 years.

### 132. The LARs will also monitor and evaluate the implementation of adaptation measures and the implementation arrangements at the beginning of the project's implementation phase until its completion.

### 134. As the Accredited Entity, FAD will provide support to the PMUs and PIIs during the first years to ensure that the IFIs in the targeted countries receive adequate training. To this end, the LARs will make several departments available to support the training of the IFIs' staff (including technical assistance, specialists of the Environmental and Social Office, legal affairs and international relations, among others).

### 135. All IFIs willing to participate in the programme and disclose funds will have to participate in the training on climate change, adaptation and mitigation activities supported by the GREEN CLIMATE FUND programme and monitoring arrangements.

### 136. FAD's activities will include the following:

- Identifying, assessing, giving clearance and monitoring the sub-subs for FOIs, MSMEs and cooperatives for adaptation projects.
- Overseeing the LARs' on-the-job training of climate change-adaptation specialists to be performed by the NEE, represented by the Ministry of Finance.
- Planning and monitoring the technical aspects of the programme, including regular field visits and periodic reporting to the LARs.
- Acting as participant in all relevant programme activities where appropriate.
- If applicable, adopting during the programme's systems, programmes and tools developed by the programme to ensure that they are adapted for the needs of the IFIs.
- Playing an active role in coordinating with other stakeholders throughout the programme and maintaining a close relationship with them (see Section 4.2).

### Key outputs of Component I and II

1. **Activity 1.1.0**: Establish a Green Business Financing Facility (revolving lending facility - window 1: adaptation, pillars 3 to 5) to provide credit lines for the following activities: drip irrigation systems; address deforestation; improve soil fertility; address growing livestock diseases due to climate change; solar mini grids that power efficient drip irrigation systems are more resilient and can be scaled up in a cost-effective manner. The facility will also support the development of solar mini grids that power the processing, storage and packaging of agricultural products which enable beneficiaries to seize more market opportunities. - Institutional strengthening
- Promotion of knowledge management. South-South cooperation, exchanges

Competence: Core 2.1-2.2: Two main outputs

Core 2.1-2.2 - Generate knowledge, conduct studies, support knowledge transfer, provide training and monitor climate impacts

148. The green business market has yet to be created in the GGGW region and many technologies and practices required for adaptation and mitigation are not yet widely known or practiced by farmers and other stakeholders. This situation must remain. In addition, to make green business projects commercially viable, a better understanding is required of both market conditions and the technical requirements needed to implement these activities. The capacity-building and training activities under this output will contribute to incremental capacity and knowledge building so as to accelerate the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices in the region. By working with existing organizations such as cooperatives, NGOs, organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs, this output strives to cultivate the restoration of 20,000 ha of degraded areas and increase smallholder farmers' yields for the following crops: millet, cassava, rice and groundnut. It will promote the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices in the region by working with existing organizations such as NGOs, cooperatives, and other entities in agroforestry systems; best practices in fruit and NTFPs (shea butter, cashew nuts, mangoes, etc.), energy-efficient stoves, water harvesting techniques, and improved livestock management. The TASP will work closely with farmers and youth-led organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs to prepare viable green business plans and test and tried technologies that can be adopted in the category of the best adaptation and mitigation solutions available in each country and across the region.

At level 146 of the TALMs, training will be offered to the banks' officials; consultants and contractors enhancing their capacity to provide technical assistance and guidance on eligible projects. The TASP will also support eligible projects through bankable green business plans and pipelines. The IFAD baseline PMRs (under the ministries of agriculture) will use the grant portion in coordination with the UNAIDS PLU to hire the relevant consultants and TASP's to support LAMRIs and selected

partners in the areas of green lending and climate risk management. FIs, cooperatives and MSMEs will be responsible for the TA processes. Training will be provided on how to conduct market assessments, data collection and analysis, the selection of appropriate financial products and instruments, and the development of green business plans. The training will cover FIs, cooperatives and MSMEs, among others focused on the acquisition and use of renewable energy equipment required along the agricultural value chain. Additionally, the programme will support the adoption of soil health management practices leading to enabling conditions for sustainable agriculture. These actions would gradually lead to reduced use of fossil fuel and wood for energy in the target countries. The TA will assist with the creation of a package of technical assistance (TA) activities that will be provided to FIs, cooperatives and MSMEs to support their adoption of climate-resilient emission-cleaning (CRL-EE) practices. The programme will link LAMRs and partners (MFIs and commercial banks) in developing countries with the private sector in developed countries for the development of green business plans, strategies and related plans. Activities for this output include (for activities disaggregated by country, see the appendix “Activity 2.1.1-2.1.7”):

* **Activity 2.1.1:** Provide capacity-building to FIs, women and youth organisations, cooperatives and MSMEs on understanding climate risks and climate resilient, low emission practices and integrating them into green business plans.
* **Activity 2.1.2:** Offer capacity-building on the development of banks’ green business plans
* **Activity 2.1.3:** Provide training to FIs, MSMEs and cooperatives on how to implement green business plans in practice.
* **Activity 2.1.4:** Support women and youth-led FIs and MSMEs to increase their participation, improve their financial management skills and access to finance for the implementation of climate-resilient emission-saving and cost-competitive CRL-EE technologies and support for gender-related studies to guide decision-making.
* **Activity 2.1.5:** Provide technical assistance to enhance governance and management of loan products. Will be green credit risk assessment, monitoring and evaluation.
* **Activity 2.1.6:** Provide technical assistance to enhance governance and management of loan products. Will be green credit risk assessment, monitoring and evaluation.
* **Activity 2.1.7:** Develop a package of tools and instruments, including ESMs, for LAMRs to assess green business plans.

Output 2.2: Improve policy dialogue, government technical and institutional capacity, advocacy, training, knowledge management and dissemination.

This output will provide a transformative approach and a feedback loop for scaling up investments in green business through enhanced public and private sector engagement and partnerships and the provision of capacity-building and training on climate-resilient emission-saving technologies. The programme will also support the development of national policies that will strengthen knowledge management, information dissemination and stakeholder engagement through the creation of social networks and platforms to share best practices and lessons learned. The programme will promote policy dialogue between FIs, LAMRs, MFIs and government authorities (national and local). With regards to renewables and energy efficiency, the programme will support the development of national policies, strategies and plans for renewable energy and financial instruments at the national and sub-national levels. The programme will also organize high-level technical and events and develop a platform for exchanges between value chain actors and financial service providers. A knowledge management system will be developed to share best practices and lessons learned. The programme will be developed. To promote the uptake of technologies and adoption at scale, the programme will promote South-South and North-South cooperation in the development and implementation of green finance solutions. With regards to financing systems, the Productive Investment Initiative for Adaptation to Climate Change, or C4IIS, in Central America and the Caribbean will be supported by the programme. The programme will also support the development of publications and catalogues for decision makers. With the support of the Regional Support Programme (Component 3), the programme will promote the continued use of green finance worldwide and connect the selected LAMRs with similar initiatives around the world.

To ensure sustainability beyond the programme life cycle, technical assistance will be provided to the target countries’ financial institutions on climate-resilient emission-saving technologies. The programme will also support the development of management practices at the programme level (standards), developing and harmonizing green lending products, instruments and tools such as ESG benchmarks; generating and disseminating knowledge on green finance within the financial system

The TAF will be implemented by the World Bank, with technical assistance provided by the African Development Bank (AfDB), the African Union Commission (AUC), the World Meteorological Organization (WMO), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), the World Food Programme (WFP) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The TAF will engage with government officials and private actors on green finance and GREA. ## Knowledge management structure

### Regional level: structure, streamlining, analysis and disseminate knowledge generated by GCF projects across the GCF portfolio. The Regional Support Programme will provide support to the regional KM Specialist who will be in charge of coordinating activities at the regional level.

### Country level: facilitate and support GCF projects in sharing the knowledge generated by each project and fostering an enhanced culture of knowledge sharing within the country. A National Knowledge Management Specialist will be hosted by the NDA in each country, as a way to address the issue of the limited human resources.

## Figure 22: Regional Support Programme

### Key activities for Output 3.1 are:

- **Activity 3.1.1 Establish a knowledge baseline:** The rationale goal here is to map all relevant information on climate financing projects and determine where new knowledge gaps exist.
- **Activity 3.1.2 Develop a Knowledge Communication Strategy:** Based on the knowledge baseline, the RISP Support Unit will work with all AES implementing projects in the GGW to build a knowledge management and communication strategy. This will include developing a project-specific knowledge management plan, defining roles and responsibilities for knowledge sharing, communication, internal communication, gathering, analyzing, documenting, translating information, adapting information, disseminating information, and managing intellectual property rights. The knowledge management strategy will define KM indicators, responsibilities for individual projects.
- **Activity 3.1.3 Create a knowledge centre:** A knowledge centre will be created to execute the knowledge management plan. It will provide a focal point for all AES implementing projects in the GGW to share information and resources with multiple stakeholders and also to disseminate it. It will organize knowledge into the GGW RISP five filters, and by thematic areas. The knowledge centre will also provide project-specific guidance on how to use the GGW RISP five filters.
- **Activity 3.1.4 Establish a Knowledge Centre Functionality:** The knowledge centre will include good practices, indigenous knowledge systems, CLEA, land restoration, value chain development, climate-resilient infrastructures, water management, etc. It will collect existing gender assessments, climate assessment tools, and other relevant information and make them available to all AES, especially DAEs in GGW countries.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.1 | PAGE 72 OF 116

## Activity 3.14 Create communities of practice.

Communities of practices (CoPs) will help connect practitioners across the projects/programmes of GCF, GFED, AF and other AIs aligned with the GGW Accelerator’s five pillars so that they can share knowledge and lessons learned from their experience in implementing these projects/programmes. CoPs will be established at three levels: country level, regional level, and global level. The CoPs will focus on specific areas of expertise where there is a wealth of experience in this area. GCFs are effective in identifying best solutions and lessons learned based on evidence and in disseminating these to other practitioners. The GGW Accelerator’s five pillars will be the main focus for these projects. The RGF’s regional and national KBF experts will support the creation and facilitation of CoPs to generate a common understanding, share knowledge and lessons learned across the GCF, GFED, AF and other AIs’ projects.

## Activity 3.15 Generate knowledge products to disseminate lessons learned and scalable good practices

and create knowledge products that facilitate the analysis of experiences in different contexts. The knowledge products will be developed from individual GCF and GEF projects by creating knowledge products that facilitate the analysis of experiences in different contexts. These knowledge products will be based on a set of good practices that can be easily adapted to and adopted in similar contexts and scaled up for wider dissemination. The knowledge products will be disseminated through various channels, including webinars, workshops, and publications. They will target investors and policymakers to inform evidence-based decision-making.

## Activity 3.16 Organize peer-based knowledge exchanges and knowledge sharing activities

Knowledge management is a continuous process that involves the creation of new knowledge from existing knowledge. The exchanges and sharing activities will be part of a broader process, and not stand-alone activities, to guarantee uptake of the knowledge and engagement in continuous learning.

### Country level workshops will focus on:

- (i) raising the awareness of stakeholders to improve the collective understanding of the relationship between KMF, M&E, communications, advocacy, staffing plans and overall strategies.
- (ii) developing a common understanding among national programmes playing an active role in supporting implementing entities in defining and designing their knowledge products, learning and aspirations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 75 OF 116

## B

### Introduction
These workshops will also provide an opportunity to connect projects, including those area funded by GGF or AF with, small intervention plans and objectives.

### Areas of Focus
The workshops will focus on sharing the knowledge and lessons learned from other GGW partners such as the Permanent Interim Committee for Drought Control in the Sahel (CILSS) and independent organizations such as the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD), Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS), FAO, the EU and other international partners, UNCCD, UNEP, World Bank, Global Environment Facility (GEF), and others. The workshops will also focus on Water, Climate Change Adaptation Approaches and Technologies (WICAT) and the Africa Regional Initiative, among others. Knowledge Share Fair participants will be encouraged to share their experiences in a variety of formats including presentations, posters, and discussions.

### Results
The workshops will provide an opportunity for participants to learn from each other about best practices and lessons learned in the links and connections people make at the event. Share Fair bridge social, hierarchical and technological divides by bringing together diverse stakeholders with different backgrounds and perspectives to the generation of a community around the themes discussed.

### Results
The workshops will also provide an opportunity to share good practice on gender equality and other related issues. These events will include training and periodic study tours of GCF, GGW and AF projects.

#### Activity 3.17: Enhancement coordination and programming among GCF, AEs, and GEF, AF and GGW national focal points
The Coordination Committee will be established to enhance the coordination between the Green Climate Fund Programme and the GEF/UNEP regional project "Harnessing the Great Wall Initiative for a Sustainable and Resilient Environment in West Africa" (GWII). The Coordination Committee will also aim at ensuring complementarity of activities and the mobilization of financing. At the country level, coordination committees will be established to ensure that the GGW national focal points are able to coordinate their activities with other relevant sectoral ministries and AEs. The coordination committee will aim to streamline the development of GCF Country Programmes and the preparation of National Adaptation Plans (NAPs) by ensuring alignment on related projects by sharing working programmes, information, knowledge and updates on portfolios on both the design and implementation phases of GGW projects. The Coordination Committee will also ensure that there are no overlapping activities of the Coordination Committees.

#### Output 3.2 Innovation and digital transformation technologies mapped and ecosystem built
In this proposal, innovation is defined as finding and applying new approaches to addressing existing problems or serve unmet needs. Innovation can be seen as a process of increasing organizational adaptability to accelerate impact. Innovation can be fuelled by science and technology, entail improved ways of working with new and diverse partners or the development of new products and services. The GGW is an example of innovation in that it aims at providing solutions for delivering essential services and products. Innovation has been and will be pivotal for reaching sustained, scalable solutions to climate change.

105. In 2015, the Paris Agreement stipulates that accelerating, encouraging and enabling innovation is critical for an effective, long-term global response to climate change and promoting economic growth and sustainable development. The agreement also calls on countries to promote research and development of low-carbon technologies and new forms of connectivity, financing models and governance arrangements. According to UN and other experts, artificial intelligence (AI) and digital transformation are key enablers for the transition towards a low-carbon economy. Disruptive climate solutions for technology, policy, finance, business models, leadership-and capacity-building are key to accelerating climate resilient, low emission resident development.

106. Innovation is closely linked to digital transformation, as they both break down silos and create transparent and unified data ecosystems. Digital technologies can provide new offer solutions to monitor, mitigate and adapt to the impacts of climate change, which range from using remote sensing observation technologies to track deforestation and forest degradation to early warning systems for extreme weather events. In the context of the GGW, innovation and digital solutions can contribute to the transition towards a low-carbon economy by providing new ways of working, building resilience to climate change and protect natural resources. Examples of emerging innovations include:


---
**Page 78**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 74 OF 116

## Social enterprise innovations that bridge service delivery gaps: For instance, the firm Hello Tractor in Nigeria connects producers with providers of agricultural services (e.g., tractor owners) using Internet of Things (IoT) platforms and mobile phones. These platforms not only connect farmers to inputs and services available at scale via machinery; but also connect them to an ecosystem of partners who use the tractor data to provide additional services such as crop insurance.

## Climate technologies such as early warning systems that provide farmers information and advice on when to plant or harvest crops, and weather forecasting. The Agri-Food Information System provides countries both to monitor crops and food prices and produce analyses and briefs in real time.

## Digital financial services (DFS) are another example of innovations that bridge service delivery gaps by providing access to loans. In Kenya, Apollo24 provides bundled products for farmers that include financing, farming inputs, insurance, and weather forecasts. The company also offers a mobile app that allows customers to make payments and satellite data to service customers throughout the entire lifecycle, from acquisition to decisions on credit and farm management.

## Open data and geo-mapping for improving natural resource planning and monitoring in countries. Tools such as Full Farming Systems (FFS) provide farmers with information about soil quality, water availability, assessing, monitoring and planning land use, including the monitoring of deforestation and desertification. The technology also provides tools to help farmers make decisions on crop choice, planting dates, fertilizer application, coverage and usage.

## Clean energy technologies such as solar panels that generate sustainable energy. The World Bank Lightening Africa programme256 provides electricity through off-grid solar products by working with local distributors in sub-Saharan Africa.

## Financial innovations (e.g., crowdfunding, social impact bonds, mobile money). FIDA Africa has developed an innovative approach to financing smallholder farmers in the region.

## Innovative adaptation approaches that provide climate smart services to villages. CGIAR climate-smart agriculture projects are a good example of this type of innovation. These projects combine climate-smart fertilizers and energy-efficient machinery with other climate-smart services, such as tailored weather forecasts to help farmers make better decisions on planting dates or crop choice. The project also provides mobile phones which farmers can use to buy index-based insurance that gives them some protection in the event of extreme weather.

162. In the context of the GSPW and GREENFUND’s specific attention will be given to support accredited entities in leveraging opportunities to integrate precision farming technologies in projects in view of the potential they have to boost value chain resilience and contribute to landscape restoration. Precision farming technologies can be defined as a whole-farm management approach that uses information technology, sensors, and data analytics to optimize crop production and resource collection for more efficient use of certain inputs and water resources. In the GSPW, these technologies will be introduced in the context of climate-smart agriculture projects. The three main components of precision farming are: (i) sensors and hardware (which are involved in using or collecting information); (ii) software (which is used to process data collected by sensors into useful information for algorithms which gather and process data to turn it into something meaningful); and (iii) commodity and whole-farm inputs. The benefits of precision farming include: (i) increased yields; (ii) reduced use of water, fertilizers, pesticides not just individual fields). Alongside the economic benefits of precision farming, there is also much discussion on the vital role that this technology can play in reducing greenhouse gas emissions. In fact, a recent study257 found that precision farming enables farmers to maintain or increase productivity yields while reducing overall inputs (including fuel) and water use. The same study also found that precision farming has the potential to reduce agricultural GHG emissions by 10-30%. There is some direct evidence of emission reductions; to date, few studies have been performed on precision farming technologies in relation to their contribution to GHG emission mitigation.

163. Recent decades have seen considerable progress towards the development and diffusion of all forms of rapid adaptation and low carbon innovations. Despite the numerous success stories and encouraging trends, overall progress towards innovative solutions has been slow. This is partly due to a lack of understanding of the specific needs of countries where transformational innovation

## Key activities for Output 3.2 include:

### Activity 3.2.1 Build a digital and innovation ecosystem.
A digital and innovation ecosystem is the evolving set of actors, activities, institutions and relations (including complementary and substitute relations) that are important to the development and deployment of innovative climate change adaptation solutions. The GCF will support the development of an innovation ecosystem in the region that fosters the creation and promotion of innovative climate adaptive solutions that can be deployed at scale. Such an ecosystem fosters economic stability and promotes sustainable development by creating new opportunities for private sector investment, public-private partnerships, and other innovative platforms such as the UNFCCC Global Innovation Hub Launched for Transformative Climate Action (GIIH) and the GCF Accelerator.

#### Key activities:
- **Mapping existing services and digital platforms** that provide extension services, financial services, farming practices, climate information services, etc., to identify gaps in service delivery among services. This mapping will include key players in the region and existing platforms and services, including those developed by governments, civil society organizations, AgriTechs, FinTech actors, value chain players, MSMEs, entrepreneurs, academia and other players in the region.
- **Facilitating knowledge sharing** on climate change adaptation practices and share expertise across the region, which will be accessible to GCF, GEF and other partners at the country and regional level.
- **Organizing annual fairs based on the model of Sanaeiro Expo**, where climate technologies in specific areas such as farming, water management, energy, etc., are showcased. These fairs will be organized within the GGW context and beyond will be sessions and shows to organize and discuss and address specific climate change adaptation solutions that can be deployed at scale. The GGW Accelerator will match interests or create standardized technical interfaces and communication forums. The events will be competitive to the marketplace by providing opportunities for real-time demonstrations and networking.

### Activity 3.2.2 Create a digital marketplace with all services provided by platforms and other providers.
Based on the mapping of existing services, digital platforms, and actors in the region, civil society organizations, AgriTechs and FinTech actors, value chain players, MSMEs that provide services for value addition, etc., will be identified. These actors will be invited to participate in a digital marketplace within the region. In many productive sectors of the economy, platforms have already become a preferred method of collaboration among actors. The digital marketplace will facilitate the exchange and sharing of information and foster new market opportunities.

## B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

### IFAD Regional Coordination Units (RCU)
165. In its capacity as AE for IGRENFIN, IFAD will create a Regional Coordination Unit (RCU) which will oversee, compile, and report on the implementation of the GGR.
The RCU will implement the programme through IFAD baseline investments PMUs (grant programs) and the LINNIE Plus. The RCU team will be established and located in IFAD’s West and East Africa Regional Offices. The RCU will also work closely with the NEEs to ensure that they are aware of the requirements for the Finance Officer. IFAD PMU staff will undergo an annual performance evaluation in line with IFAD performance assessment policy and procedures, including the identification of the duties and responsibilities for each AE and EE.

### National Executive Entity (NEE)
The NEE for IGRENFIN is the Republic of Burkina Faso, the Republic of Côte d’Ivoire, the Republic of Guinea, the Republic of Mali, the Republic of Niger, the Republic of Senegal, the Republic of Togo and the Republic of Uganda. The AE will inform each NEE of the relevant obligations and requirements established in the GGR and the IFAD policy on climate change. The NEEs are responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented in a manner consistent with the financing agreement and guarantee the repayment of the GCF loan portion.
The NEEs have a proven track record in similar projects and are well positioned to implement IGRENFIN.

### Selected LNAEs responsible for the implementation of green credit lines with the GCF loan proceeds (Component 1)
- The Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso
- The National Investment Bank of Côte d’Ivoire
- The ARB Apex Bank Limited of Ghana
- The National Development Bank of Mali
- The National Bank of Niger
- The National Bank of Togo
The LNAEs will be responsible for the implementation of green credit lines (windows 1 and 2), together with their preliminary assessment and recommendations on the feasibility of each project. If a project is found to be feasible, it will be submitted to the NEE for approval or rejection of a subject loan via the NEE. For the implementation of the GCF grant proceeds (Component 2), the LNAEs will be responsible for the identification of projects and their preliminary assessment and recommendations on feasibility. If a project is found to be feasible, it will be submitted to the NEE for approval or rejection of a subject loan via the NEE.

### Executing entities of the GCF grant proceeds for IGRENFIN (are): the Ministry of Agriculture, Hydraulics-Cultural Heritage and Environment of Burkina Faso; the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock of Côte d’Ivoire; the Ministry of Agriculture of Mali; the Ministry of Agriculture of Ghana; and the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Equipment of Niger. The GCF has concluded agreements with these entities in all five countries.

### Regional Steering Committee (RSC)
Given the geopolitical and macroscopic scope of the different components, a joint committee will be established to establish the Regional Steering Committee (RSC) and specify its composition, mandate, and functioning. The nomination of members to the RSC will be done by each NEE in compliance with the GCF Gender Policy. The RSC will be responsible for providing strategic guidance on the implementation of IGRENFIN, including AWP(s) and their periodic progress report. It will designate the key stakeholders that are to be part of the National Steering Committee (NSC), which is responsible for the overall coordination of IGRENFIN in each NEE country. The NSC will include FMOs, cooperatives and MSMEs, relevant regional institutions, private sector entities and civil society organizations (CSOs). Any recommendations or measures adopted by the RSC are subject to final approval by the NEE, represented by its members.

## IFAD Baseline Project Management Units (PMUs):

IFAD baseline project management units will be anchored within each IFAD baseline PMU. Each baseline PMU will budget for two additional headcounts to manage IGREENFIN I. Baseline PMU staff working on IGREENFIN I will receive training and orientation in line with baseline PMU's performance assessment policy and procedures.

### LNABs Project Investment Units (PIUs):

In each country, the selected LNABs will implement the IGREENFIN I loan proceeds. Each LNAB will set up a dedicated PIU to manage IGREENFIN I funds. The PIU will be responsible for all financial management and accounting issues related to IGREENFIN I. The PIU will also be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented in accordance with the approved project document, including access to target beneficiaries. It is the NEES's responsibility to determine how they will work with RFLs. The PIU will also be required to provide regular progress reports on the implementation of IGREENFIN I.

## IFAD RCU

173. The IFAD RCU will carry out an ex-post review of a risk-based sample of Withdrawal Applications (WAs) but will not be directly involved when Baseline PMUs make WA or Direct Payment requests to IFAD. The ex-post review is to be conducted by the IFAD RCU and will be based on the same criteria as the ex-post review for the baseline PMU and recommended actions included in the assurance review tracker for monitoring of implementation.

## IFAD Baseline PMU in the flow of funds

173. Baseline PMUs will open two new bank accounts to use exclusively to manage GIREENFIN I proceeds, namely a project account and a loan disbursement account. The project account will be used for payment of project operating costs relating to GIREENFIN I incurred by the baseline project and for the replacement of Bank PMU operating costs. The loan disbursement account will be used for the repayment of the IFAD Loan Disbursement Account and transferred to the bank according appropriate reporting using the revolving fund mechanism.

## PIU within the LNABS:

173. All GIREENFIN I proceeds to the LNABS will be based on a revolving funds mechanism. The LNABS will open two GIREENFIN I operating accounts within the bank: one account for the GIREENFIN I loan process and the other account for the project operations. The amount of money that will be available in each of these accounts for the entire life of the programme will be stipulated in the IFAD Financing Agreement. The LNABS PIU will receive an indicative allocation to the budget for the first six (6) months of operations for both the loan and grant proceeds, as stipulated in the AWPS.

173. The LNABS PIU will then transfer further disbursement requests to the Baseline PMU for approval and disbursements on a quarterly basis, justifying the expenditures related to the previous tranches of payment made in the grant and loan project operations. The Baseline PMU will review these requests and approve them based on the information provided in the Disbursement Report and supporting documents.

### External Audit:

#### IFAD RCU will be in charge of reviewing each of the five baseline PMA's annual audit reports and preparing a summary report, however all GIGRENFIN I stakeholders are recommended to ensure that GCF non-compliance is granted each year on the external auditor’s terms of reference by verifying that GCF requirements regarding external audit and reporting are met in accordance with external auditors’ TGRs. The IFAD RCU will ensure a timely delivery of each country's external audit report to GCF.

#### Each UNRRC and the respective Ministry of Agriculture will each undergo an annual external audit as mandated by the GCF, which will be conducted by an independent auditor appointed by the GCF. Each external auditor’s TGR will include a statement of adequacy of IGREENFIN I, which will provide a specific opinion on whether the financial statements are in accordance with the GCF and the GCF’s Financial Rules and Regulations. The auditors’ notes to the accounts and management letter will be submitted to IFAD for non-object ion at least one month before the audit report is issued by the auditor. The UNRRC and Ministry of Agriculture will provide a copy of the LNA's external auditor’s opinion to an audit opinion on the use of IGREENFIN I funds managed by the UNRRC and the Ministry of Agriculture.

#### At the regional level, an external audit will be undertaken on the RCU of IGREENFIN I and the Regional Support Programme’s activities annually.

#### B

**Support Unit**

The Support Unit will be responsible for implementing activities under Component 3 (RSP). It will be composed of a regional unit based in Dakar, Senegal and one country-level specialist per country who will support the NGOs. At the regional level:

- **Program Coordinator**: overall coordination of Component 3
- **Country Specialist**: responsible for Output 3.1
- **Innovation Specialist**: responsible for Output 3.2
- **Technical Officer**
- **Administrative Assistant**

**Level country specialists:** For each country, IFAD will hire a Knowledge Management Specialist to support the NGOs. The KM Specialist will be based in the regional office and will report directly to the Regional Support Unit’s KM Specialist and will share their time between the NDAs office and the IFAD office as well as other relevant offices within IFAD. They will have a coordinating role for knowledge management, communication and policy specialist. They will have a coordinating role for knowledge management, communications and policy in support of the RSP.

**GGW RSP liaison Officers:** The GGW Accelerator will work with a GGW Regional Support Programme liaison officer to ensure close alignment and collaboration with the GGW Accelerator. These liaison functions will include:

- Ensure the collection of results and impacts by aligning the RSP’s knowledge management and innovation activities with those of the GGW Accelerator.
- Coordinating the reporting of GGW RSP activities and progress to the GGW Accelerator to increase the collective impact of the GGW Accelerator and RSP.
- Ensure alignment and regular feedback between the RSP knowledge hub and the Accelerator GGW knowledge hub on best practices, lessons learned and innovations.
- Liaise with GGW structures, processes and institutions (PAAGGW, AU, and GGW Steering Committee) to ensure they are informed of RSP activities.

A second liaison officer will be co-funded with the UNCCD and hosted by the Great Green Wall Pan-African Agency to ensure coordination with the regional entity actors.

**Country Coordination Committees:** The committees will be composed of NGOs, GWP focal Points, GGW Focal Points, Adaptation Fund representatives, IFAD representatives, sectoral national ministries and AUs. Building on relevant platforms wherever possible, committees are responsible for coordinating the alignment of GCP work programmes, GGF STAR activities and RSP activities to ensure that all three initiatives are aligned in their respective countries. The committee is responsible for the coordination of the activities reported to the GGW Accelerator to ensure transparent and comprehensive reporting at the country level and reporting. Countries may decide to invite additional stakeholders to take part in the Country Coordination Committee.

The Support Unit will be responsible for preparing the following reports for submission to the IGREENFIN PMU:

- **M&E indicators**
- **Annual progress report: best practices**
- **Financial report**
- **Annual budget**
- **Annual progress reports (for each country)**

- Annual progress reports

**191.** Loans repayment: Beneficiaries (FOs, cooperatives, MSMEs), commercial banks, MFIs) will repay their loans to the selected national agricultural banks (Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso, The National Investment Bank of Côte d'Ivoire, the National Investment Bank of Mali, and the National Agricultural Development Bank of Senegal). Repayments will be made directly into the EEs (The Republic of Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal), represented by their respective ministries of agriculture or other relevant government agencies. The repayment process will be facilitated through a system based on IFAD as an Accredited Entity of the GCF), irrespective of the performance of the co-lending to the respective LNAbs, and therefore we only accept the loan risk for the borrowing countries.

**192.** Grant counterparties will support the credit systems in each country in: (i) promoting with regulations and overcoming barriers to attracting private sector investment; (ii) enhancing coordination at the financial industry level; and (iii) financing and offering advisory services to financial institutions and beneficiaries. The Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso, the National Investment Bank of Côte d'Ivoire, the National Investment Bank of Mali, and the National Agricultural Development Bank of Senegal will disburse GCF and co-financing loans to beneficiaries (FOs, cooperatives, MSMEs), etc.) in a timely manner. The counterparties will also provide technical assistance for project implementation, including capacity-building through selected consultants and service providers to help LNAbs conduct marketing activities, develop business plans, and manage credit risk. They will also support the development of an information system in place monitoring systems. IFAD will report to the GCF based on the conditions established in the FAA and AMA.

**193.** The counterparties will be responsible for ensuring that all loan disbursements are made according to the terms agreed with the LNAbs. AES will contractually ensure that the conditions required by the GCF are transferred through the Credit Line Agreements to each country, and that plans and payments may be financed with GCF resources. If necessary we do not meet the GCF’s condition.

**B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (more than 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)**

In the GGG region where markets are still small, the cost of capital, high credit terms, unverifiable, and the upfront costs of investment are often prohibitive. This is a major barrier to access to finance for smallholder farmers and other actors in green business investment and support the achievement of the GGG’s objectives by 2030. The main barriers hindering access to finance include: (i) high transaction costs; (ii) lack of collateral; (iii) limited creditworthiness; (iv) capacity and appetite of international institutional investors for green business initiatives. This is mainly due to the perception that smallholder farmers are not a priority in terms of investment, as well as their low credit rating and concerns with the macroeconomic environment. Limited credit worthiness of smallholder farmers, country risks, policy uncertainty, and lack of collateral are also major barriers to access to finance for smallholder farmers. Access to finance that will leverage additional private sector investment to create a viable green business market in the region. Covid-19 has further exacerbated these challenges. The GGG is therefore seeking funding from the GCF to support its interest in smallholder agriculture – become all the more risk avers. The West Africa region’s growth prospects were expected to be among the highest in sub-Saharan Africa, with an average annual GDP growth rate of 5.2 percent according to ADB. West African countries were expected to enter recession in 2020, with a 3.8 per cent contraction of real GDP and a 14.7 per cent decline in exports. In contrast, the East Africa region is expected to experience an average GDP growth rate of 5.6 percent in 2020. The GGG countries are expected to be among the most vulnerable to climate change as a whole, which was expected to stand at 4.3 per cent, against over 2 per cent in 2019. Forecasts suggested on average an

## INCREASE IN ECOFINA PUBLIC DEBT AS A PROPORTION OF GDP, WHICH WOULD STAND AT 41 PER CENT IN 2020 AND 42 PER CENT IN 2021, AGAINST 35 PER CENT IN 2019.

### Therefore, IFAD has approached the GCF to seek the required "tough protection" to incentivize and de-risk public and private sector investments in green business pipelines, and attract private sector investments. The public sector in the targeted countries can play an important role in green business but limited public resources are available for this purpose. In addition, the public sector is not always able to provide the necessary emission evidence. Because of COVID-19, these economies are predicted to go into recession and therefore, all efforts should be made to ensure that public and private sector investments are directed towards climate change mitigation and adaptation.

### To attract private investment, the GCF countries Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana are leveraging highly innovative financing mechanisms to enable private funds flow more easily to green businesses. The public and private sector investment for green business in the GCF countries is still limited.

### For the targeted beneficiaries of this programme, which are the smallholder farmers organized in cooperatives, MSMEs, FOSs, cooperatives and local public investors, there is a need to provide additional concessional climate finance to attract private funds to address market failures and support the development of new green business pipelines. The GCF countries Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana are leveraging highly innovative financing mechanisms to enable private funds flow more easily to green businesses.

### The justification of the use of GCF concessional finance is based on the fact that most sub-Saharan African countries have some public sector capacity to support the development of new green business pipelines. When resources become available, lending terms are as follows: interest rates are high (as seen in tables 24 and 35) and repayment periods are short. The GCF concessional finance will be used to provide a loan at zero percent interest rate for up to ten years to cover the incremental costs for green businesses, and incentivize MSMEs, FOSs, cooperatives and local public investors into green business.

### The GCF Grant instrument under Component 2 will support the LMAFs in transitioning towards green banking (green portfolios, governance and policies) and attracting new market opportunities. IGEF/REIFEN will help the selected banks build their capacity to develop green business pipelines, improve environmental and climate governance procedures and with co-financing from other sources, provide technical assistance for the development of green business pipelines, improve their environmental and climate governance procedures and with co-financing from other sources, provide technical assistance for the development of green business pipelines. The GCF will also support policy dialogue on green finance in agriculture with central banks and other local commercial and MFI. More specifically, the GCF will support the implementation of the Green Finance Strategy (GFS) under the Côte d'Ivoire National Agreement and through their NCSs as well as the involvement of private actors. The grant proceeds will also support capacity building for public sector institutions and cooperatives to build their capacity to develop green businesses on high return on investment value chains.

### Due to the target countries' huge solar potential, the programme will work closely with cooperatives, FOSs and private sector investors in order to ensure that the investments made under the GCF (and other sources of financing such as BID) will be promoted, whenever possible, to ensure the sustainability of the energy infrastructures.

### Regarding the loan proceeds. The reduced interest rates (given by zero percent interest rate for public banks but only with two percent interest rate for private banks) and longer repayment periods are expected to result in an IRR presented in Annex 2 EFA for each crop with longer repayment periods. The GCF loan at zero percent interest rate will be used to provide a loan at zero percent interest rate for up to ten years to cover the incremental costs of green businesses. De-risking support can catalyse positive lending decisions by enhancing the collateral and joint venture arrangements, as well as providing technical assistance in the development of green business pipelines. The GCF will also support policy dialogue on green finance which will contribute to greater collaboration between agricultural development banks and financial sectors.

### The programme will be implemented through close collaboration and coordination of each country project, thus facilitating the identification of complementary actions that can be taken by public sector institutions and cooperatives in order to ensure a more efficient use of resources. This will lead to positive feedback loops that will enhance the programme's impact given the common development challenges related to green finance and rural development. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a multilateral climate fund established under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). It aims to provide financial and technical support for projects that address climate change mitigation and adaptation in developing countries.

### Training and capacity-building for secondary actors

Training and capacity-building are essential components of the GCF's approach. These activities aim to build the skills and knowledge necessary for effective implementation of climate action projects, particularly those related to energy efficiency and renewable energy technologies. The training is designed to cover a wide range of topics, including:

- **Energy efficiency**: Training on how to identify and implement energy-saving measures in buildings, industries, and transportation.
- **Renewable energy**: Training on the installation, operation, and maintenance of solar panels, wind turbines, and other renewable energy systems.
- **Energy management**: Training on how to manage energy use more efficiently through better planning and control.

### Partnerships

The GCF encourages partnerships with a variety of stakeholders, including:

1. **Public sector actors**: Governments at all levels (national, regional, local) are key partners in implementing climate action projects.
2. **Private sector actors**: Businesses can play an important role by investing in renewable energy and energy efficiency technologies.
3. **Civil society organizations**: NGOs and other civil society groups can provide valuable insights into the needs of communities and help to ensure that projects meet their priorities.

### The Green Climate Fund (GCF) as a financing mechanism

The GCF is designed to be a flexible and responsive financing mechanism for climate action projects in developing countries. It aims to:

- **Support national climate change strategies**: By providing funding for projects that align with national climate change plans.
- **Promote innovation and technology transfer**: By supporting the development and deployment of new technologies, such as renewable energy systems and energy-efficient appliances.
- **Build capacity at all levels**: By investing in training and capacity-building programs to ensure that countries have the skills and knowledge needed to implement effective climate action projects.

### The GCF's role in promoting sustainable development

The GCF is committed to promoting sustainable development by:

1. **Fostering inclusive growth**: By ensuring that climate action projects benefit all members of society, including women, youth, and marginalized groups.
2. **Promoting innovation and technology transfer**: By supporting the development and deployment of new technologies, such as renewable energy systems and energy-efficient appliances.
3. **Building capacity at all levels**: By investing in training and capacity-building programs to ensure that countries have the skills and knowledge needed to implement effective climate action projects.

## D.1. IMPACT ASSESSMENT AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

### D.1.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

213. GREENFIN will support the greening of the financial system in the five selected countries by building the capacity of financial institutions to provide green finance services and products. This includes providing technical assistance (green governance and lending products, green credit indicator system for management, long-term loan periods and underwriting criteria), training of staff at banks, MFIs, cooperatives, and as well as the transfer or licensing of 200 techniques and innovative solutions for climate resilient, low-carbon agriculture and forestry. This will be achieved by mobilizing up to $5 million in GCF resources (i.e., GCF resources plus additional resources mobilized) can be used to produce positive impacts and ensure both financial institutions and the targeted communities are able to access a pool of financial assistance in each of the targeted countries and in the GGW region.

214. Innovative green business models including climate resilient crops and crop varieties, sustainable land restoration techniques, solar power to grow drip irrigation and processing, packaging and storage equipment, among others, for the five selected countries will be developed by FOFs, MSMEs, cooperatives as well as the credit channel. (i.e., GCF resources plus additional resources mobilized) can be used to produce positive impacts and ensure both financial institutions and the targeted communities are able to access a pool of financial assistance in each of the targeted countries and in the GGW region.

215. GREENFIN will contribute to the implementation of the GGWR, which aims to restore 10 million hectares of currently degraded land by 2030. The GGWR is a landscape-scale restoration initiative that covers an area spanning the entire width of Africa – from Senegal to Djibouti – as well as a mosaic of projects and initiatives in each country. It will be implemented through a combination of large-scale, integrated landscape programme to address land degradation and desertification, boost food security, environmental and economic benefits for local communities, and contribute to the achievement of the SDGs. The GGWR aims to achieve its goals by: 1) restoring 5 million hectares of degraded land in five countries (Senegal, Mauritania, Mali, Burkina Faso and Niger); 2) safeguarding 30% of the total area under restoration; 3) ensuring that at least 75% of the restored areas are managed by local communities; 4) increasing agricultural productivity on degraded land by up to 100%; 5) safeguarding food security for the 20 million people in the GGWR region (i.e., GCF resources plus additional resources mobilized) can be used to produce positive impacts and ensure both financial institutions and the targeted communities are able to access a pool of financial assistance in each of the targeted countries and in the GGW region.

The response to the migration crisis, with EUR 49.314 million set up to migrate to Europe from degraded parts of Africa, has been slow. The GCF is currently supporting only one project in this area (the Greening of West Africa so far). Within GREENFIN, IGGREENFIN and the Regional Support Programme, this programme will be scaled-up by providing additional resources for the implementation of the GGWR. In addition, two major African-led initiatives set up with the bold ambition to restore the productivity and vitality of the Sahel region are also supported: 1) The Greening of West Africa (GWA) initiative; and 2) the Greening of Central Africa (GCA). These initiatives will be implemented through a combination of large-scale, integrated landscape programmes and channel resources to beneficiaries and provide crucial funds to help accelerate the implementation of the GGWR. which began in 2017. It will also contribute to advances on the African Union Agenda 2063 as it regards the Greening of Africa.

IGREENFIN I aims to engage national agricultural banks, national banking associations, banking regulators, green standards setting bodies, local universities and business schools on green finance in agriculture. The programme will support the production of studies and policy papers on climate risk management and adaptation in the agricultural sector, as well as the development of a set of guidelines for banks to use ESG benchmarks. It will also build knowledge on green finance invested in the agricultural sector by both the private sector and public institutions. This will be done through the creation of a regional database that will provide information on their ESG, institutional capacity and better apt part of their lending portfolios on the NDCs. By building the banks’ capacity to use ESG criteria for lending decisions, it will contribute to the development of green finance in agriculture and alter the perception smallholder from a risky borrower to a vast market with great potential and opportunities.

The programme will also provide training on ESG criteria from training activities and affordable finance (available from local financial institutions) and techniques that help them reduce their environmental footprint. This will be done through a series of workshops and seminars where banks’ operations are expected to demonstrate to end users the effectiveness and value of the solutions proposed, which will stimulate demand for green products and services in the agricultural sector. 222 The GCF GGRF RSP embodies a new approach piloted by IFAD and the GCF which aims to catalyse impact beyond a one-project or programme investment by creating bridges for knowledge sharing and learning between GCF projects in Africa, Asia, Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) and other regions of the world.
