### D3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximate 1 page)

223. IGREENFIN and the Regional Support Programme have identified and adopted the following UN SDGs and associated targets as relevant to the Upstream Programme: Clean water and sanitation (SDG 6); Gender equality (SDG 5); Clean energy and water sanitation (SDG 6); Affordable and clean energy (SDG 7); Decent work and economic growth (SDG 8); Climate action (SDG 13); Life on land (SDG 15).

#### 2.4 Environmental co-benefits

The programme is designed to help secure multiple environmental benefits for water, air, land and nature by promoting the adoption of best adaptation and mitigation practices and technology, which will reduce greenhouse gas emissions from energy production and consumption, soil erosion and desertification and increasing the potential for carbon sequestration. These benefits could also contribute to the increased resilience of the region’s ecosystems and human livelihoods to climate change and variability and to the GCF’s goal of promoting sustainable development in Africa. The programme will support the transition to reduce the use of energy from biomass, including firewood and agricultural residues, imported diesel fuel and charcoal, which are all high-emission sources of energy. This will be achieved by increasing access to renewable energy. It is expected that CO2 emissions (5.4 million tCO2 eq) will be reduced during the implementation period through the promotion of renewable energy technologies and practices, including integrated emission from RET is integrated) and limits will be placed on the use of biomass and diesel for energy consumption through the introduction of a Renewable Energy Act. The programme will also contribute to reducing deforestation by increasing access to renewable energy. It is expected that CO2 emissions (5.4 million tCO2 eq) will be reduced during the implementation period through the promotion of renewable energy technologies and practices, including integrated emission from RET is integrated) and limits will be placed on the use of biomass and diesel for energy consumption through the introduction of a Renewable Energy Act.

#### 2.5 Social co-benefits including health impacts

The activities proposed under this programme will generate multiple job opportunities for rural populations such as women, youth, people with disabilities, promote inclusion and support investment in social infrastructure (FOI, roads, MSMEs). By improving access to clean water and sanitation, the programme will contribute to cost-saving health benefits thanks to cleaner air for local communities and access to safe drinking water. The programme will also contribute to improved nutrition through increased access to nutritious food. In addition, the programme will support women’s participation in decision-making, increase their access to financing and assets and improve their livelihoods by increasing their income from agriculture and services and secure through VAD baseline investments will be positively impacted by the programme.

#### 2.6 Economic co-benefits

The economic benefits of this programme are reflected in its high potential for poverty reduction through increased access to clean water, sanitation and energy, as well as in increased revenue activities (AGA such as the production of value addition and different fuel such NTPPs), inclusive transport, Arab gum, bebab, gaba, coffee, cocoa, tea, tobacco, cotton, cashew nuts, palm oil, rubber, etc. The programme will also contribute to the IGA since 2017 have amounted to approximately EUR37.91 million across all 11 GGRF countries, with Burkina Faso being most active in terms of revenue generation from these activities. In 2018 alone, Burkina Faso generated EUR54.6 million, Burkina Faso, EUR8.8 million, and USD300,000 for Mali and Senegal each. A stable inflow of revenues from natural resources will be a key driver in the sustainability of the Upstream Programme and its associated projects over time. By promoting IGA and opportunities for livelihood improvement, the programme will help better the living conditions in the five participating countries that are among the poorest and the most vulnerable to climate

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 91 OF 116

## Gender-sensitive development impacts: Women are increasingly and disproportionately affected by climate change. In the developing countries, women play a central role in agriculture, food security, water needs, fuel for firewood, and income generation. They have to bear harder the consequences of declining crop yields due to climate change (primarily higher temperatures and unpredictable rainfall patterns). The programme will address these gender-sensitive development impacts by promoting the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices and pastoralism, both men and women. The implementation of targeted activities can potentially offer an array of advantages for women in terms of increased access to productive assets, financial services, education, improved water and wood collection time for women, freeing up their time so they may engage in other production tasks. Through the programme, women will be empowered by increasing their access to land, credit, technology, and information that have historically been denied them.

The programme has set targets: 50 per cent of loans are to be granted to women-led MSEs, cooperatives and FCOs; 70 per cent of the beneficiaries in the targeted activities will be women; and 60 per cent of the farmers and FCOs in which young women participate. Specific actions will be developed to strengthen the technical and managerial capacities of women through training, capacity building, and mentoring. The programme will also develop gender plans and improving their level of financial education. As stated in the gender action plan, gender-disaggregated data will be collected at all levels of implementation.

The programme aims to close the gap between men and women: as women represent 60 to 70 per cent of the work force and do not have access to productive assets, finance and knowledge. And also 1/3 of the women will be included into the National Steering Committee to ensure their representation in decision-making.

### D.4. Needs of recipients (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

228. Participating countries in these programmes are highly vulnerable to flooding, flooding, rising temperatures and water scarcity, droughts, desertification, soil erosion, landslides, locust invasions, etc. Their exposure to vulnerability to climate hazards is projected to increase in the future. Climate projections suggest that the West Sahel and southern Africa will experience more frequent and intense heat waves, while the East African region under certain scenarios, temperature rise will accelerate, meaning that the continent on average could be between 2 and 6°C warmer by mid-century (IPCC AR5).

229. Climate hazards have a direct impact on agriculture. They reduce productivity, food security and GDP, cause endemic diseases (malaria) and increase rural poverty. Given the five countries' high poverty rate (see feasibility study for more details) and the vulnerability of most change, the country has few financial instruments that want to risk investing in agriculture despite its fact that it is a key sector for economic development.

230. The five countries’ financial system remains weak and has a limited number of instruments for financing agriculture. (NAIBs have limited possibilities of mobilizing highly concessional loans on the capital markets. FCOs and MSEs are not able to access international capital markets, due to their small size and relatively high risk profile. Long-term financing with affordable conditions (high-interest rates, tenors and grace periods) due to small loan sizes and relatively high transaction costs is also difficult for these institutions. The lack of financial infrastructure, the absence of skills and knowledge on climate finance and climate resilient, low emission agriculture, the nascent regulatory framework and the limited experience in green financing are some of the key barriers that NAIBs face.

231. Technical assistance and funding for the establishment of a Green Financing Facility that will provide concessional loans and climate smart credit risk tools will enhance access to credit, contribute to de-risking the sector and build capacity among financial institutions to better serve the concerned countries. They will directly help overcome the key barriers that NAIBs face in rural finance and enable them to engage in green financing.

In the section on adaptation, actions proposed would result in an increase of 17% in water availability and 68% in food production. |

This programme was designed at the request of the GCF national designated authorities (NDAs) of the respective countries following the approval of the Inclusive Green Financing for Climate Resilient and Low Emission Smaller Agro-enterprises in West Africa. The objective is to support the development of a regional strategy for financing climate resilient smaller agro-enterprises in West Africa, with a focus on Senegal, Mali, Côte d'Ivoire, Burkina Faso and Ghana. This strategy was brought together the NDAs, the CGI of the agriculture and investment banks and government representatives from Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Mali, Senegal and Togo attended the GCF meeting in Accra. The CGI also led to the formation of a coalition of local state-owned banks (agricultural and investment banks) in West Africa that will be involved in the implementation of this strategy. The CGI was also used by the CGI and FAD then engaged with the NDAs of the five target countries through face-to-face meetings, presentations and email exchanges to discuss the proposed strategy. A series of workshops were held with stakeholders, including two survey organizations, had been conducted, the GCF National Focal Points from the participating countries endorsed the proposals. The GGF RSP has been developed in response to a call from the GCF boards for the GCF and the international community to increase the level of support to accelerate the pace of implementation of the activities contributing to the achievement of the GGW’s objectives. It is fully aligned with the principle of country ownership and it respects the GGF’s governance structure, including its Board, Secretariat, and Country Programmes.

The GGF RSP is aligned with GGE/Consortium, which is led by the GGGW Agency with the temporary secretariat hosted at IFAD. The GGF RSP will be implemented in close collaboration with the GGF Consortium as well as of the GEF and AF focal points, by hiring GIS and KM specialists to help them during the planning of future GCF, GGE and AF proposals and their implementation through enhanced AID exchanges.

## 42. Regarding mitigation, 10 value chains for each country were analyzed with estimated the economic and financial performance of different types of local stakeholders. The IRR for the various VCA is as follows: Nigeria (58 per cent), Kenya (63 per cent), Tanzania (79 per cent) and Uganda (82 per cent). Mali (50%) with payback time in the range of one to five years. When we consider country performance, the IRR ranges from 21 per cent for Côte d’Ivoire to 46 per cent for Nigeria. The highest IRR is found for SMEs in Côte d'Ivoire (78 per cent), for cooperatives in Côte d'Ivoire (74 per cent), Mali (68 per cent) and Senegal (52 per cent). The IRR for farmer organizations is generally the lowest in all countries, ranging from 10 to 39 per cent.

## 24. The GCF contribution will be very important to both improve the financial performance of the investment (i.e., by targeting investments with a higher IRR and lower payback period), as well as to reduce the risk for investors. A 20 per cent decline in revenues or a similar increase in production costs will result in negative IRR and NPV for projects.

## 43. When taking a societal approach that considers the economic valuation of externalities (e.g., carbon and other ecosystem services), including those that do not generate a cash flow, the IRR increases to 100 per cent for smallholder farmers in Côte d'Ivoire, which is more than double the IRR obtained by directly considering the economic impacts of the investments proposed, resulting in a net positive contribution to society.

## 45. Regarding mitigation, the IRR and NPV are positive, and the debt coverage ratio (DCRE) is above one for energy generation projects with diesel generators. The DCRE is below one when using solar PV or wind power as these technologies are financially viable than bringing centralized power generation to rural areas and using fully decentralized solar power generators. It is worth noting that with any price lower than $0.8—$1/kWh, farmers would also benefit from cost savings in the range of 25 per cent (with a price of $0.6/kWh) to 43 per cent (with a price of $0.7/kWh). The IRR decreases from 9 per cent with a price of $0.8/kWh and declines to 14 per cent (with a price of $0.6/kWh). The GCF contribution makes the DCR more desirable for projects, increasing it by 3.8 times (with a price of $0.7/kWh) or increasing them from 1.9 to 1.61 in case of a price of $0.6/kWh.

## 45. With regard to farmers as consumers, the IRR is positive if a farming cooperative were to invest in a new grid, it would result in cost savings for access to affordable electricity and increased income from selling excess power. The payback period for the mini-grid investment to be economically viable is $0.4/kWh. This is the minimum price that would justify investing in a mini-grid (i.e., the IRR is positive). The IRR increases with an increase of the price, reaching 15 per cent and those working in the food value chain) up to $0.5/kWh consumed when compared to using diesel generators or solar PV. This means that the savings are large enough to reduce the IRR to a value that is comparable to the interest rates required by financial institutions (e.g., 8–12 per cent). The GCF contribution makes the DCRE more desirable for projects, increasing it from 0.75 to 3 times when considering the price of $0.6/kWh. The contribution of IGES/REDD+ in GCF and CIF is essential. In fact, while both IRR and NPV are positive, the debt coverage ratio (DCRE) is smaller than 1 when considering bank rates. When using the preferential rates guaranteed by the IGES/REDD+, the DCRE increases to more than one for all projects with a price of $0.6/kWh, which makes them economically viable from an IRR and NPV. Further, we carry out a more systemic analysis and consider indirect benefits and reduction of externalities (e.g., carbon sequestration) that are not included in the financial analysis.

## 47. Overall, we find that with the implementation of the programme, investments in mini grids is economically viable for all economic actors interested in investing to replace reliance on diesel generators, which would generate both positive returns for investors and savings for consumers.

### E. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max 900 words, equivalent to ~1 page)

#### Reporting from FAID to GCF:
- Reports will be submitted on a rolling basis in accordance with the FAID's Communication Agreement (FAA). Through the RCU, the FAA includes an annual progress report on the status of the funded activity throughout the relevant reporting period, based on the above-described annual, mid-term and final reports from each country. The GIREENFIN project submitted by FAID baseline investments and activities in the first year of implementation will be reported upon at the end of that year. Subsequent years will deliver numbers in a flag format: a) annually within sixty (60) calendar days after the end of each year of programme implementation, b) mid-term within ninety (90) calendar days from the midpoint of the second year and every two years thereafter until the programme resources have been disbursed, whichever occurs first; and c) at programme end up to six (6) months after the fifty percent (50%) disbursement point.

#### GIREENFIN monitoring and reporting:
- GIREENFIN will apply standard procedures established by FAID for monitoring and evaluating investment operations, including co-financing (FAID, ISDB, and AfDB). The FAID RCU will comply with all relevant requirements of the GCF's Financial Rules (GFR) and related policies. All financial reports, mid-term review and final evaluation to be submitted to the GCF.
- The agricultural banks and selected partners (commercial banks, MFIs) will be responsible for assessing beneficiaries' business plans in accordance with the programme's eligibility criteria. They must ensure that all information is recorded on the beneficiary's file, including: name, address, currency, maturity and interest rates), the value of the contract, loan proceeds and eventual use of proceeds for monitoring purposes.
- The financial institutions must be properly identified in the banks' accounting systems and be in compliance with what is stated in the programme's eligibility criteria. They will also ensure that all information related to the transaction (loan amount, currency, maturity and interest rates), the value of the contract, loan proceeds and eventual use of proceeds for monitoring purposes are recorded on their file.
- The financial institutions must provide a report at the end of each year of implementation of the programme in order to identify the intermediate milestones achieved in each phase and evaluate its outcomes and elements of proposed targets. These indicators or benchmarks are included in the flag format in section E above.

## ESG Reporting (IFAD RCU will receive an annual report on the ESG performance of the national agricultural banks)

The PIU units will take inputs from relevant teams and compile the annual report for each applicable reporting period. Annual reporting will be undertaken by the IFAD RCU, while the independent mid-term and final year verification of climate impact will be undertaken by an independent provider.

### 251. GHG Reporting: ICRAF, a strategic partner of IGEFIN/IFN, and the Regional Support Programme will be in charge of the reporting on GHG emissions in line with the methodology in the EXACT Annex.

#### Regional Support Programme impact assessments:

The Regional Support Programme aims to assess the contribution and impact of programs and to improve the next generation FGP programmes that can benefit from the best evidence-based design options available. The impact assessment will be conducted by an independent third party, which will be selected through a competitive process. It will assess the contribution of the Regional Support Programme activities to the paradigm shift. It will entail the development of a questionnaire to define the outcome variables before the Regional Support Programme starts. A baseline will be established and updated every two years. The impact assessment will be conducted at least once during the project, submitted upon completion to the beneficiaries, namely the 11 countries NDAWs and GWP Focus Points as well as the Accountability Board.

#### Monitoring and evaluation in the context of the GCF GW RSP:

The Regional Support Programme will not substitute the AES during the M&E process. Each AEQ remains accountable for its own monitoring and evaluation activities. The AES will be responsible for reporting to the GCF Secretariat. AEQs will provide additional support through the SURAGRAID project to the AES and the NDAWs to help them understand which means of verification will be used by the Board Accelerator, as they can also be used by the Independent Audit process.

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF), environmental and social risks that could impede achievement of the programme objectives from being achieved. Also describe the proposed risk mitigation measures. Insert additional notes if necessary.

### 254. The programmes may face risks in several categories: macroeconomic, implementation, financial, technical, environmental and social. The levels of these risks are considered moderate to low and expected to be mitigated by IFAD and other partners. This section provides a description of the potential risks that could impede achievement of the programme objectives as well as recommendations for mitigation measures.

It is foreseen that the programmes will be affected by the COVID-19 context, which could cause delays in the start-up and general implementation phases. The IFAD RCU has prepared a comprehensive COVID-19 risks management approach, which includes the setup of the COVID-19 Rural Poor Fund (RPF) to provide financial support for rural poor households during the pandemic. This fund will be used for: developing design and modern reform; repurposing baseline investments with a set of activities such as training on safe labour practices and health protocols, providing personal protective equipment (PPE), setting up mobile testing units, using drones and other digital extension tools for labour and input-purchasing practices; shared mechanization; digital marketing platforms; and the development of an online platform to provide information on COVID-19. The RPF will also be used in the 2019 pandemic to reduce women's exposure to violence: increased access to finance, agri-service centres for women; distribution of inputs (seed, fertilizer, fodder/legumes sapping, fingerings, vaccines, medicines); increasing allocations to debt reduction and microfinance programmes; supporting the affected activities to local institutions and NGOs, including existing partners such as FAO and international NGOs.

Selected Risk Factor 2

ForestForex/forex exchange
Low/Low,Low,Low
Low/Low,Low,Low
Description

258. Recent countries experience currency devaluation or currency fluctuation linked to external shocks: Foreign exchange (FX) risk (or currency risk) exists in the foreign exchange component as IFAD loans are denominated in Euros for the WAMZIMI unit and loans to final borrowers are likely to be disbursed in a currency other than USD (i.e., the local currency). The FX risk is also linked to the fluctuation of the Euro against the local currency. Thus, FX risk arises from the possibility of unforeseeable fluctuations between the local currency and the Euro that could lead to an increase or decrease in the amount of Euros received by IFAD for repayment of loans denominated in the involved currencies. The XOF currency is pegged to Euros and there won't be any loss. However, for Ghana which has its own currency (GHS), the risk will be higher as it is not pegged to Euro. In this case, application of appropriate hedging tools such as forward contracts or options can mitigate the risk. But neither GFA nor GEESY generally have the capacity to hedge this risk (i.e., place Euros in the market and manage profits to increase their capacity to offer concessional terms in local currency).

Mitigation Measure(s)

259. For Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali and Senegal: These countries are part of the West African Economic and Monetary Union (WAEMU) and have a common currency (XOF). The risk will be more specific as explained above. Owing to the FCFA’s fixed exchange rate to the euro, which is currently established at EUR 1 = CFA Frs 655.958, the XOF is pegged to the Euro. Thus, the risk of devaluation or appreciation of the XOF against the Euro is denominated in euros. The latest devaluation occurred in 2004, which illustrates the stability of the FCFA, which is also supported by the good performance of WAEMU member’s states. Devaluation or re-evaluation of the FCFA in the long term will not be a problem as the XOF is pegged to Euro and the exchange rate is fixed at EUR 1 = CFA Frs 655.958. The WAEMU zone is well known as a strong monetary zone with all member’s countries positive GDP growth projections, which have a stable economic situation. However, in case of devaluation or re-evaluation of FCFA, the risk will be mitigated by prudent policy, liquidity buffer and continued fiscal and macroeconomic consolidation efforts by member countries.

260. Ghana: The grant proceeds will be allocated to FADG which has strong credit risk management including bringing strategic reserves to cover 15% of its total exposure. In addition, the Bank of Ghana (BoG) in Ghana regulates the conduct of the interbank foreign exchange business in the Ghanaian financial market and establishes and regularly updates exchange rate policies. The BoG also monitors the performance of FADG’s foreign exchange transactions and will continue to develop and deepen the foreign exchange market in Ghana. The Bank of Ghana publishes in its latest annual report the foreign exchange reserves held by the country, which are usually invested in US dollars. These reserves usually involve mechanisms such as forward contracts, options and others, which can protect them from exchange rate fluctuations. Capacity building and proper implementation of such mechanisms will be part of the TC activities under Component

263. Sustainable finance practices and integration of financial management into banking operations is new for all the countries involved in this programme. Moreover, neither the banks, their clients nor regulators are accustomed to the new products to be developed under this programme.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

264. The programme results from a demand-driven process. IFAD has initiated this programme upon confirmation of the interest and willingness of regional development banks, smallholder farmers and governments during the design phase. The commitment of all stakeholders will ensure that the programme is implemented in line with the approved project document and policy dialogue interventions are planned during the programme implementation phase. The implementation of a participatory approach will help to ensure the sustainability and adherence of the beneficiaries. Policy dialogues between public and private actors will be promoted to mitigate the risks.

### Category: Financial

#### Probability: Medium

#### Impact: Medium

**Description**

265. Proper due diligence process led by IFAD and the national executing entities, the local Fl’s financial experts, technical assistance from the regional development banks, and support from the central banks – Western African Central Banks Central Bank and the Central Bank of Ghana) will all contribute to ensuring robust engineering, procurement, and governance systems. Additionally, the agreements between the recipient governments and their implementing agencies will be reviewed by the IFAD country team to ensure that they are in line with the project lifecycle. Programme operation risks will not be mitigated by a thorough due diligence process led by IFAD technical and financial experts alone; however, it is expected that the programme will be implemented in accordance with the procurement procedures established at each EE level to support and ensure the high delivery quality outcomes.

### Category: Financial

#### Probability: Medium

#### Impact: Medium

**Description**

267. The IFAD grievance mechanism will be used for the reporting of complaints or allegations of wrongdoing within the programme. The complaints will be managed in accordance with the established procedures as outlined in the ESFM. The programme will establish a community engagement process and provide access to information on a regular basis, which will help to ensure that all stakeholders are informed about the project’s progress and impact. Individuals and communities can contact IFAD directly and file a complaint if they believe they are or might be adversely affected by an IFAD-funded project/programme that does not comply with IFAD’s Social and Environmental Policies and mechanisms for addressing complaints. The programme will also establish a process to address allegations of fraud or corrupt activities in relation to programme implementation; these are dealt with by IFAD’s Office of Fraud and Oversight.

268. Eligible parties according to IFAD’s grievance mechanism:

To file a complaint for alleged non-compliance with IFAD's social and environmental policies and mandatory aspects of its SECAP, IFAD will consider only complaints meeting the following criteria:


---
**Page 115**

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 111 OF 116

The complainant claim that IFAD has failed to apply its social and environmental policies and/or the mandatory provisions set out in SECAP and Safeguards.

The issue must be of a nature which has been or will be adversely affected by IFAD's failure to apply these policies.

Complaints must be put forward by at least two people who are both nationals of the country concerned and/or living in the programme area. Complainants from foreign locations or anonymous complaints will not be taken into account.

Complaints concerning projects with a total cost of less than US$10 million, or those that are more than 95 per cent disbursed, will not be considered.


288. The process according to IFAD's grievance mechanism: Complainants should first bring the matter to the attention of the government non-governmental organisation responsible for implementing the project and/or the relevant implementing agency and the ministry of economy and finance and the environmental protection agencies involved in this programme that have with the responsibility for overseeing compliance with IFAD's Social and Environmental Policies and mandatory aspects of its SECAP. The complainant should be advised to submit a written complaint to the government non-governmental organisation responsible for implementing the project and/or the relevant implementing agency and the ministry of economy and finance and the environmental protection agencies involved in this programme that have with the responsibility for overseeing compliance with IFAD's Social and Environmental Policies and mandatory aspects of its SECAP. The issue may be brought to IFAD if the complainants feel they might be subjected to retaliation if they went to the government non-governmental organisation responsible for implementing the project and/or the relevant implementing agency and the ministry of economy and finance and the environmental protection agencies involved in this programme that have with the responsibility for overseeing compliance with IFAD's Social and Environmental Policies and mandatory aspects of its SECAP.

How to submit a complaint: Complaints regarding alleged non-compliance with IFAD's Social and Environmental Policies and mandatory aspects of its SECAP can be submitted in any of the following ways:

- Download the complaints form (Word) through IFAD website: https://www.ifad.org/en/governability-and-complaints
- Send an email to SEC@complaints@ifad.org

In addition, complainants are encouraged to utilise every available grievance redress mechanism including associations (including farmers' organisations/organizations), traditional council (Paramount chiefs and elders), village square engagement (consisting of representatives of men, women, and social groups), village general assembly, the project NGO(s), etc.

### G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) FMD

#### 277. **Environmental and social risk assessment** (max. 700 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
The Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) is a key tool to promote good practices in environmental and social governance (ESG). IFAD's ESMF provides guidance on how to integrate environmental and social considerations into the proposed projects and ESMF, ensuring that appropriate and robust procedures and systems are put in place. The ESMF and screening of subprojects by the LMA will ensure that all projects comply with the ESMF. Environmental and social performance indicators and programme activities and investments and their social and environmental impacts are closely monitored, evaluated and reported.

278. During the due diligence phase of the UNRRD, IFAD will assess the ESMFs of each institution and ensure that it is aligned with the programme's ESMF, including the Environmental and Social Safeguards of the banks and the GCF. Participants in the programme are required to have an environmental and social management system (ESMS) in place and to comply with the ESMF. The ESMS will be used for assessing environmental and social risks and categorise operations while evaluating the environmental and social risk of their portfolio. The measures include:

- **Verify that the operations being financed are not prohibited by law:**
  - Verify compliance with the programme's List of exclusions in the ESMF.
- **Enforce compliance with national legislation related to permits and environmental licenses, use of natural resources and activities with dangerous substances:**
  - Categorise operations according to their environmental risk category and the risk of the operations to be financed.
- **Verify that the amounts requested by clients include the implementation of the measures to minimize the environmental and social risks:**
  - Present reports on the environmental and social management of its operations according to the frequency established in the ESMF. The ESMS will be updated according to the risks of its portfolio and the magnitude of the credit granted by the LMA or a partner.

279. When ESMS systems are either not in place or are weak, GIREENFIN II TAF will provide technical assistance to help participants establish an ESMS system that is aligned with the ESMF. The programme will also provide training for participants in the programme. All sub loans will be screened by LMA(s) for their environmental and social risks and impacts prior to disbursement of funds.

280. **GIREENFIN II** will promote gender equality and women's empowerment both at the fund- and project-level. As part of its Gender Analysis, GIREENFIN II will identify gaps in existing systems and propose solutions such as systems requiring participatory LMA(s) (agricultural banks), commercial banks and MFIs to collect and report on gender disaggregated data. GIREENFIN II is proposing an action plan for all banks which help them integrate or strengthen their MFI's capacity to provide financial services to women, including the establishment of a Gender Equality Task Force Facility, 50 per cent to go to women-led and youth-led MFIs, FIs and cooperatives. Under Output 2.3, GIREENFIN II will also provide technical assistance in the form of training for MFIs on gender equality and financial inclusion through the provision of capacity building workshops and training sessions.

# BUDGETING

## IFAD RCU:

* In each fiscal year, the IFAD RCU will communicate the budget timetable and templates to be used for submission to the Baseline PMU, which must comply with GCF guidelines.
* The IFAD RCU will emphasize that the GREENFIN 1 overhead costs should be disclosed as a separate component of budget and will be funded through the GREENFIN 1 grant proceeds.

## Baseline PMU:

* The Baseline PMU staff will closely collaborate with the bank PMUs throughout the AWPR preparation process, from inception to reception of IFAD's non-objection letter.
* This is to ensure that IFAD/GCF budget guidelines are followed. Once the Baseline PMU has prepared the project document and it is dimensionally and complies with both budget guidelines and that the appropriate budget templates have been used in the budget preparation, the Baseline PMU will submit the project document to the bank PMUs.
* The bank PMU will prepare the GREENFIN 1 budget while paying attention to the milestones in the timeline communicated by the Baseline PMU. Most importantly, they must submit the final draft to the Baseline PMU no later than 30 days prior to submission of the project document to IFAD RCU.
* The bank PMU will ensure that the project document is dimensionally correct and includes all relevant information such as activity category, sub-category of expenditure, zone, etc. The loan component of the budget will be drawn up from the approved project document while the grant component is drawn from the indirect costs required to operate the scheme.

## Bank PMU:

* The bank PMU will prepare the GREENFIN 1 budget while paying attention to the milestones in the timeline communicated by the Baseline PMU. Most importantly, they must submit the final draft to the Baseline PMU no later than 30 days prior to submission of the project document to IFAD RCU.
* The bank PMU will ensure that the project document is dimensionally correct and includes all relevant information such as activity category, sub-category of expenditure, zone, etc. The loan component of the budget will be drawn up from the approved project document while the grant component is drawn from the indirect costs required to operate the scheme.

## ACCOUNTING STANDARDS AND FINANCIAL REPORTING:

* The Baseline PMU must ensure that its financial statements are in accordance with IFRS. Refer to Section 6 of annexure 22 for list of financial statements.
* The role of the IFAD RCU Financial Unit is to harmonize, compile and consolidate country financial statements into a single consolidated statement.
* IFU financial reports comply with reporting guidelines prior to submission to IFAD RCU Bank PMU will ensure that they can generate the required information in accordance with the GCF Financial Reporting System. All financial information related to GREENFIN 1 must be expressed in the currency stipulated in the IFAD Agreement, namely US dollars.

## Project Implementation and Programme:

* Project implementation and programme will be carried out in accordance with IFAD applicable rules and practices, as well as the IFAD's Programme Implementation Manual (PMI), the programme specific PMI and loan submission guidelines. This is done through the Letter to the Borrower and the Financing Agreement between IFAD and each National Borrower/Recipient.

## G.4 Discussion of funding proposal:

* The discussion on the proposed project financing proposal includes confidential information.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP183

## Proposal: Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN) I: Generating Agricultural Banks & the Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Great Green Wall of Africa Countries - Phase 2

### Accredited entity:
International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)

### Country/(ies):
IGREENFIN [Components 1 and 2]: Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal
GGW-IFAD [Component Programme Component 3]: Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal and Sudan

### Project/programme size:
Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is considered for Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The programme will enhance access to credit and financial assistance for smallholder farmers, organizations, cooperatives and micro and small units and contribute towards climate-resilient low-emission agriculture, value chain processing and mini-grid supply linked to adaptive technologies. | The use of GCF funds exclusively for climate-agriculture and irrigation practices needs to be ensured. |
| The programme will incentivize the participation of the private sector through partnerships with commercial banks and microfinance institutions. | The programme is subject to project-specific risks, such as macroeconomic, political project implementation, and environmental and social impacts. |

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in annexure V and addendum V, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations," respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### 2.1 Programme background


---
**Page 150**
---

1. The **11 countries** of the Great Green Wall (GGW), as well as Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana, are among the world's poorest and most vulnerable countries to climate change. They ranked at the bottom in terms of human development index in 2020. A large majority of their populations lack access to food insecurity, water migration and conflict. Agriculture, livestock and forestry are the main economic activities in these countries. The livelihoods of millions of country depend directly on rainfed agriculture (crops, livestock, fisheries and forestry) for nutrition, income generation and social security. These countries face a high risk of climate change and variability owing to the geographical location of these countries and their socioeconomic and technological development levels.
2. Climate projections indicate that temperatures will continue to decrease while precipitation is expected to increase by between 1 and 3.7°C during the period 2031-2050 compared with the 1986–2005 reference period. According to these projections, agricultural productivity in the region could decline by up to 40% by 2050. This will have a negative impact on farmers' income, drive competition over diminishing natural capital and lead to greater poverty and food insecurity. The effects of climate change are likely to be more severe than those of other shocks such as conflict or economic instability.
3. Climate change also destabilizes local markets and curbs economic growth and investment, which in turn affects the ability of governments to provide public services. This makes it even more difficult to overcome the current lack of access to credit and technical capacity barriers, one of the main constraints for smallholder farmers. The majority of these farmers are organized into cooperatives and micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) in the GGW region.
4. Increasing access to financing is key to improving on-farm productivity by providing working capital, allowing for investment in post-harvest value aggregation of products and increasing market access.

**References:**

* FAO. 2019. The Great Green Wall: A Framework for Action. Available at: https://www.fao.org/3/ca8756en/ca8756en.pdf
* GGGI. 2020. Africa’s Green Climate Fund: Progress Report. Available at: http://www.gggf.org/sites/g/files/bkzkbm149/f/Progress%20Report%202020.pdf
* International Institute for Environment and Development (IIED). 2020. Building Peace in a Complex World. Available at: https://www.iied.org/building-peace-complex-world
* Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD). 2016. Agriculture in Sub-Saharan Africa: Prospects and Challenges. Paris: OECD Publishing. Available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1787/9789264253522-en
* OECD. 2021. The Great Green Wall: A Framework for Action. Paris: OECD Publishing. Available at: https://www.oecd.org/greengreenwall/
* OECD. 2021. IFAD Inclusive Financial Services: Portfolio Stocktaking. Available at: http://dx.doi.org/10.1787/5j4317n9z623-en. Web publication (March 2021). aHdMdeAa/hc/s/142-5897927733


---
**Page 151**
---

2.2 Component-by-component analysis

The proposed GREENFIN programme has three components: component 1: the Green Business Financing Facility; component 2: the Technical Assistance Facility; and component 3: the GWF Regional Support Programme.

(a) **Component 1: Green Business Financing Facility** - this will be established to provide concessional loans to foster adaptation and mitigation practices for green businesses within the targeted sectors (agriculture, forestry, energy, water). Operated by local national agricultural banks (LNAIs), the facility will offer special lines of finance with a focus on women's empowerment. It will also support NGOs, civil society organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs (including agribusiness actors and operators in the targeted sectors).

(b) **Component 2: Technical Assistance Facility (TAF)** - this was created to address capacity, knowledge and policy gaps hindering the uptake of green business projects in the entire region. It will be implemented by a consortium of international organizations, NGOs, and cooperatives, with a special emphasis on women and youth.

(c) **Component 3: GWF Regional Support Programme** - to address the above-mentioned increasingly complex and interrelated challenges and support the transition towards climate-resilient development in the region. The GWF initiative will be implemented by IFAD through its two ongoing programmes (IFAD GREENFIN and IFAD Green Climate Fund). It aims at increasing the uptake of good practices, increasing learning and informing policy and investments across GCF and other programmes. The programme will focus on three main areas: (i) scaling up climate-resilient technologies and building a digital and innovative ecosystem; (ii) the development of innovative green business models for low-carbon technologies; (iii) the development of innovative green business models for climate-resilient, low-carbon technologies.

(d) GREENFIN I seeks EUR 73.517,000 in loans and EUR 84,443,199 in technical assistance grants from IFAD. The funds will be mobilized from IFAD (EUR 60 million), the Islamic Development Bank (USD 15 million) and the African Development Bank (USD 18 million). The programme aims to create a market for green business and will provide additional climate finance to complement IFAD baseline investments in agriculture, forestry, energy, water and other sectors.


---
**Page 152**
---

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

- **IGreenFUND** will support the greening of the financial system in the five selected countries (Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal) by providing a pool of five LNAFs to offer green financing for climate-resilient, low-emission agriculture projects (green finance and trading products, green credit market instruments, long-term loans, etc.) and unlocking of bank lending in the sector. It will also support 1,500 MSEs and 2,500 farmers' organizations and cooperatives, as well as the transfer or licensing of 200 technologies and innovations to these actors. These interventions will contribute to reducing the targeted countries' greenhouse gas emissions by 64.8 MtCO₂eq annually (equivalent to 13.7 million tonnes of CO₂). The expected impacts of this programme are estimated to reach 378,600 direct and 2,494,000 indirect beneficiaries, of which 50 per cent will be women and 50 per cent will be men (comprising 50 per cent youth and 50 per cent adult males). Innovative green business models, including climate-resilient breeds and crop varieties, improved agricultural practices, value addition to agricultural products, processing, packaging and storage of equipment, for the 10 selected value chains (cattle, chicken, tomato, sustainable mango agro-forestry, cashew, rice, groundnut, millet, cassava production and processing), will be promoted. The programme could be used in other countries.

- **IGreenFUND** could be used to produce positive impacts and ensure both financial and economic returns for the MSEs and farmers' organizations involved. It would also be scalable to other regions of each of the targeted countries and in the GWR region.

- **IGreenFUND** will contribute to the implementation of the Green Initiative, which aims to return 10 million hectares of degraded land to productive use by 2050 (MCO₂eq) and create 10 million jobs in rural areas by 2030. Spanning the entire width of Africa – 8,000 km from Senegal to Ethiopia – the Green Initiative will focus on three main components: (i) the greening of agriculture; support of development partners as a pan-African sustainable landscape programme to address land degradation and desertification in the Sahel region; (ii) the greening of cities and support the adaptation of communities to climate change. With the aim of achieving much more than just re-greening, the Green Initiative will also contribute to addressing three major challenges in a region expected to experience warming temperatures: (i) safeguard food security for the 20 million people in the Sahel that lack sufficient food every year; (ii) support the creation of green jobs to reduce poverty and unemployment among young people; and (iii) prevent the migration crisis, with 60 million people set to migrate to Europe from degraded parts of Africa by 2031; and (iv) foster peace and security in the Sahel, where conflict has already displaced 2.8


---
**Page 153**
---

# 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

## Scale: Medium to high

The proposal demonstrates the potential for a paradigm shift by shifting away from current development models and towards more sustainable agricultural practices and a set of ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) measures that will break the current model of degradation, desertification, and loss of biodiversity. This can be achieved through climate-smart agriculture interventions, such as crop rotation, intercropping, agroforestry, and soil-planting techniques. The proposed programme aims to demonstrate the potential for positive adaptation results. If implemented robustly and sustained after programme completion, such interventions will have a significant impact on food security and livelihoods in the region.

### 3.2.1 Positive impacts

If implemented robustly and sustained after programme completion, such interventions will have a significant impact on food security and livelihoods in the region.

### 3.2.2 Engagement with financial institutions

IGreenFIN aims to engage local national agricultural banks, national banking associations, and other financial institutions, as well as universities and business schools in green finance in the agriculture sector. Through the programme's mainstreaming work, these institutions will be able to sustain the GCF-funded activities and concessions beyond the project period.

### 3.2.3 Development of policies

The programme will develop studies and policy papers on climate risk management to define the relevant standards and indicators for green finance in agriculture. It will also develop tools such as environmental and social governance benchmarks. It will build knowledge on green finance investment opportunities, including those related to smallholder farmers' access to NDCs and work closely with national agricultural banks to develop and improve their environmental and social governance frameworks.

### 3.2.4 Building capacity

By building the banks’ capacity to offer affordable green finance for smallholders in the agriculture sector, the programme will start to fill the gap in financing for agriculture and alter the perception of financial institutions towards sustainable development opportunities.

### 3.2.5 Green credit lines and awareness campaigns

Green credit lines and awareness campaigns will support the development of demand for green products, particularly among youth and women, support a viable pipeline of green business projects, reduce the financial sector's perception that smallholders present a high risk in terms of defaulting on loans, and increase access to finance for small-scale farmers who are traditionally difficult to reach (e.g., the MME sector). Beneficiaries such as MSMEs will benefit from training and capacity-building activities related to green finance products and techniques that help them reduce their operating costs while increasing their productivity and income. The programme will also develop a tool to assess the financial viability of potential users, the effectiveness and value of the solution proposed, which will stimulate demand beyond the project period.

### 3.2.6 Transition from grant-funded projects

The programme will support the transition towards sustainable long-term markets for such projects by covering the costs associated with the transition from grant-funded to market-based financing through GCF, which aims to capture impact beyond a one-off project or programme investment by creating a pipeline of green finance products and techniques that can be scaled up nationally otherwise would not be implemented in situ, and among accredited entities (AIs), GCF national designated authorities (NDAs), and GCF and GEF focal points. This innovative approach has the potential to create a replicable model for scaling up climate-smart agriculture interventions in other large landscapes and ecosystems such as the Amazon or in countries with relatively


---
**Page 154**
---

# Sustainable development potential

## Scale: High

The proposal demonstrates high potential for sustainable development. GEF/IN21 and the GGGI Regional Support Programme have identified and adopted the objectives of the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) as well as the targets related to them:

- No poverty (SDG 1): Zero Hunger (SDG 2): Gender equality (SDG 5): Clean water and sanitation (SDG 6): Decent work and economic growth (SDG 8): Climate action (SDG 13): Life on land (SDG 15); and Partnerships for the Goals (SDG 17).

The programme is designed to help secure multiple environmental benefits for water, air, land and nature by promoting the adoption of best adaptation and mitigation practices and technologies. These include: (i) agroforestry techniques; (ii) improved soil management; (iii) threatened dryland biodiversity against erosion and desertification, and increase the potential for carbon sequestration in soils; (iv) reduced emissions from livestock production; (v) reduction of the region's ecosystems and human livelihoods to climate change and variability and the overall objectives of GEF (Land restoration, soil creation and carbon dioxide (CO2) emission reductions). The programme will also reduce the use of fossil fuels for energy consumption in agriculture and agricultural residues, imported diesel fuel and charcoal, which are the main sources of emissions from biomass burning. This will be achieved through the promotion of renewable energy. It is expected that CO2 emissions will be reduced (total emissions reduction of 5.6 MtCO2eq) during the programme period by promoting agroforestry techniques and other climate-smart agricultural practices (this emission reduction will further increase once the avoided emissions from residues are considered). The programme will also reduce the use of diesel for energy consumption through the use of biomass and diesel for energy consumption through the adoption of RET.

With the promotion of Agri and agroforestry techniques, more than 100,000 hectares of degraded land will be restored and improved. This will lead to increased agricultural production and services. The programme will build up each link in the selected agricultural value chains (including livestock) by promoting the adoption of climate-smart practices that will increase the productivity of key commodities and farmers' incomes while safeguarding the environment.

The activities proposed under this programme will generate multiple job opportunities for people living in rural areas, especially women. The programme will also contribute to more coherent investment through farmers’ organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs. By promoting local investments, the programme will help to reduce poverty and improve food security with health benefits due to cleaner air for local communities and access to clean energy, water and food, thus reducing toxic income. As described in its gender analysis and action plan, the programme will also contribute to women's empowerment by increasing their access to financing and assets and improve their livelihoods. The targeted areas considered the most vulnerable were selected through FAO-based investments will be positively impacted by the programme.

The economic benefits of this programme are linked to its high potential to reduce poverty and increase income generation, especially in rural areas where women play a key role in income-generation activities, such as the production and value added to a variety of fruit and


---
**Page 155**
---

3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

The vulnerability of the country and target populations and their adaptation needs are explained in the funding proposal and feasibility study, including a general analysis of the scale and intensity of exposure to climate change risks. The countries participating in this programme are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts on agriculture, livestock production, fisheries as well as other climate change hazards (e.g. wildfires and locust infestations). Their exposure and vulnerability to climate change is expected to increase over time.

The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) projections suggest that the Western Sahel and northern parts of Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana will be hotter and drier with more frequent heat waves. The projected increase in rainfall intensity, frequency, and duration suggests that climate change will accelerate, meaning that the continent of Africa could be between 2 and 4 °C warmer by the end of this century.

The countries participating in this programme are highly vulnerable to the adverse impacts of climate change. They reduce productivity, food security and grain domestic product, cause endemic diseases (e.g. malaria) and increase rural poverty. Given the five countries' limited financial resources, their capacity to adapt to the multiple aspects of climate change, the countries have few financial institutions willing to risk investing in smallholder farming, despite the fact that agriculture remains the main source of economic growth.

The five countries’ financial systems remain weak and have a limited number of institutions providing credit. The majority of these institutions are state-owned and provide highly concessional loan in cash markets. Farmers' organizations, MSMEs, cooperatives, and youth and women's associations are unable to access affordable credit from commercial banks due to small size and lack of collateral. In addition, the countries face a number of barriers related to climate change at the country level, lack of skills and knowledge of climate finance and climate-related financial instruments, as well as the nascent regulatory frameworks and the need to de-risk the agriculture and banking sectors.

Technical assistance and funding for the establishment of a Green Business Financing Facility (GBFF) will be provided by the GCF. The GBFF will provide smallholder farmers with access to credit, contribute to de-risking the sector and foster greater knowledge of climate finance in rural populations. The GBFF will also help to overcome some of the key barriers they face in relation to providing access to finance to rural populations, and enable them to engage in green financing.


---
**Page 156**
---

3.5 Country ownership

**Scale: High**

The proposal meets the programme’s alignment with the participating countries’ national development plans and policies and strategies and NDCs. It is recommended that the programme's progress and results are monitored and quantified, and the extent to which the programme contributes to the implementation of the objectives of the three Rio Conventions ratified by all countries, including the UNFCCC, the CBD, and the Cartagena Agreement, the SBI and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015–2030.

This programme is fully aligned with the participating countries’ national development plans and policies and strategies and NDCs. It contributes to the implementation of national adaptation programmes of action, national climate change policies and strategies, NDCs, national communications and national strategies for disaster risk reduction, as well as the SDGs.


3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

In the GGOV region, green businesses are largely financially underserved, both in terms of available financing and access to financial institutions and technical assistance. Activities under IGREENFIN aim to fill a financing gap through the promotion of green finance products and green business models in the GGOV region. By leveraging existing resources and building on IGREENFIN resources, the programme will reach the maximum number of beneficiaries. Partnerships with LBNAs are essential for scaling up IGREENFIN activities through the provision of green loans and technical assistance to de-risk all potential subprojects.

GCF financing will overcome the existing barriers to accessing financial and non-financial services in the GGOV region, including lack of access to finance for climate change adaptation and mitigation measures (e.g. solar energy for agriculture). Additionally, even though LBNAs have made significant progress in developing green loan products, their loans are still not sufficiently concessional to stimulate greater adherence to low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways. The IGREENFIN programme, as it is the energy sector, several bilateral donors have put in place concessionary financing schemes that combine grants with credit to promote agricultural and rural finance; they work mainly through LBNAs. These programmes are also important for de-risking investments in additional LBNAs. Early results from these programmes are highly encouraging, although strong oversight and technical assistance will be required to ensure the quality of the loans. The leverage ratio is 1:0.7 (i.e. USD 0.7 of additional public and private funding leveraged per USD 1 of GCF investment). This programme has a total budget of USD 90 million in additional public and private contributions (including co-finance).

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The programme aims to support the building and scaling up of the resilience and adaptive capacity of farmers' organisations, cooperatives and MCMIs by increasing their beneficial use of climate-resilient agricultural practices. These practices would result in low greenhouse gas emissions and climate resilient and sustainable agricultural practices. The programme will also contribute to the conservation and enhancement of the sustainability of natural resources, protecting threatened dryland biodiversity, and preventing soil erosion. This is achieved through the adoption of improved farming practices and technologies. The social co-benefits for local communities include improved health due to cleaner air and increased access to clean energy, water and food, and enhanced food security.

Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instrument: The programme is categorised as a low-risk category B project under GCF’s environmental and social safeguards framework. It is allocated to providing concessional loans to beneficiaries, through the LNRAs, for adaptation and mitigation practices and businesses. The programme will be classified in category A or high-risk only if it involves any of the following: (i) activities that are likely to lead to significant adverse impacts; (ii) activities that lead or require involuntary resettlement or involve conversion or degradation of critical natural habitats; or (iii) other circumstances as defined by GCF’s Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) which outlines the potential risks and impacts of the proposed programme and the mitigation measures required.

Compliance with GCF's environmental and social safeguards guidelines: Paragraphs below describe how the programme proposes to address the requirements of the GCF environmental and social safeguard guidelines.


---
**Page 158**
---

### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

Given the generally poor working conditions and limited awareness of non-compliance with health safety standards in the agriculture and energy sectors, there is a risk that child labour or forced labour may occur in projects related to labour and working conditions (e.g. child or forced labour) in financed subprojects. Such risks may occur in programme activities that may require the participation of external service providers, such as drilling boreholes for irrigation systems, construction of small-scale hydroelectric power plants, and maintenance of irrigation systems, or the drilling of boreholes, where underage workers may be engaged. The AE will ensure that all relevant parties involved in the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of the programme are aware of selection criteria to identify any forms of child labour and forced labour in industrial sectors, including training and capacity-building on existing labour regulations, as well as implementing a need-to-know principle for information sharing with respect to the programme. The AE will also ensure that all parties involved in the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of the programme during implementation. These actions are part of the AE's regular monitoring across its implementing partners. While most of the beneficiaries will be small-scale farmers who may not employ workers, some of them (e.g., medium-sized agricultural service providers or micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs)) may employ workers, in which case the programme intends to ensure that they respect both the labour regulations and the principles of decent work.

### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The programme's activities include supporting 'carbon sensitive processing and packaging', which could result in factories carrying out their production processes in a way that reduces carbon emissions, thereby reducing water and soil pollution from factory effluents. To mitigate these risks, the programme requires participating farmers to adopt good agricultural practices (GAPs) for the use of fertilizers and pesticides, and to avoid using them near water bodies or in industrial zones or far from communities; promote the use of recyclable or reusable packaging materials; and ensure that all waste is disposed of properly. To reduce the environmental risk associated with the use of resources, the programme will promote strategies such as using renewable energy sources as well as reducing the use of fertilizers and pesticides. In addition to promoting the responsible use of agrochemicals such as fertilizers into water bodies during agricultural production, the programme will provide training to farmers on proper use of fertilizers and agrochemicals; and a particular emphasis is placed on the safe handling and storage of these products. To reduce environmental and health risk from potentially hazardous wastes in the RET installations (e.g., solar panels), the programme will ensure that all waste generated during the installation process is disposed of properly, and that the management plan for the disposal of the waste is established. To ensure that borehole drilling and irrigation expansion activities are carried out safely and with minimal environmental impact, the programme will comply with nationally and/or internationally established guidance and the programme will require a supply/demand assessment to be conducted prior to implementation to ensure that there is adequate water availability in the area.

### ESS 6: Community health, safety and security

The programme may have environmental and health risks associated with the activities of the programme. These include health risks related to noise and air pollution, inadequate handling of RET wastes, release of agrochemicals into the surroundings and exposure to hazardous substances such as pesticides and fertilizers. To mitigate these risks, the programme will provide training and capacity-building activities and development of hazardous waste and pesticide management plans for all participating farmers. The programme will also ensure that safe drinking water is available in the area through the implementation of the programme's activities. In addition, the programme will ensure that all RET installations are installed according to a "no tolerance" policy for SEAH in the programme's supported operations to ensure that appropriate prevention and control measures are taken. These measures include ensuring that all safety protocols are incorporated in the programme design and are embedded in the AE's policy on SEAH. Regarding the potential for conflicts relating to the agriculture-based activities, the programme will ensure that project beneficiaries are made aware of selection criteria in the selected project districts and regions.


---
**Page 159**
---

### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The programme’s activities will likely involve involuntary resettlement and land acquisition, particularly for activities that may require population displacement (e.g., for agricultural production or infrastructure construction). The ESS stipulates that critical biodiversity hotspots in target areas will be identified and mapped. A biodiversity impact assessment will be conducted to identify potential impacts; the ESS stipulates that critical biodiversity hotspots in target areas will be identified and mapped. An environmental baseline will be established, and an environmental monitoring activity will be conducted by the programme’s safeguards team in collaboration with the environment protection agencies in the target countries so as to monitor any irreversible changes identified can include land use change, habitat loss, or species extinction.

### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The programme will also support activities that may have a potential risk of habitat fragmentation through the development of production plots or infrastructure construction, as well as other activities that could lead to adverse impacts. The ESS stipulates that critical biodiversity hotspots in target areas will be identified and mapped. An environmental baseline will be established, and an environmental monitoring activity will be conducted by the programme’s safeguards team in collaboration with the environment protection agencies in the target countries so as to monitor any irreversible changes.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous peoples

Pastoralists and other socially vulnerable groups are present in the countries where the program activities will be undertaken. The ESS stipulates that these groups should be considered and applied by the AE. The programme excludes activities that could lead to adverse impacts on indigenous peoples, including those with fewer than 20 people. Within its ESS framework, the AE provides the following entry points to ensure the consideration of indigenous peoples in project planning: a social analysis template for planning to exclude adverse impacts on indigenous peoples; and a rapid social analysis template that provides the basis to understand the baseline and social diversity at the sub-project level.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy

The AE notes that its free, prior, and informed consent requirements apply not only to projects but also to activities. The AE will ensure that all relevant local communities where land uses and access rights may be affected. In terms of potential benefits for indigenous peoples, the programme will consider the inclusion of specific local communities can be found in components 1, 2, and 3, and indicators for the number of beneficiaries from these communities are included in component 4. The AE notes that as well as the engagement of vulnerable groups could be used to promote the participation of indigenous peoples. The programme will promote the benefits of indigenous knowledge by including it in project planning and implementation.

### ESS 8: Cultural heritage

The programme will exclude activities that will lead to conversion or degradation of cultural heritage sites, and will be part of its screening criteria. Should instances where chance finds (of physical cultural resources) that are not identified during the baseline survey occur on the site being considered for inclusion in this facility, the programme will redirect the work into other sites to prevent any damage.

### Adequate “Chance find” procedures

In the operational guidelines of external service providers, adequate "chance find" procedures should be included under the budget for ESF monitoring.

# Gender policy

## 4.2 Gender policy

### a: The AIF has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.

#### a1: The gender assessment provides the context of the enabling environment in the five countries targeted by the IGREENFIN regional programme. All five countries have ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), as well as a number of other international and regional instruments, such as the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol). The constitutions of the respective countries also reflect principles of equality between men and women, and prohibit discrimination based on gender. Four of the countries have established women's ministries/agencies with responsibilities of implementing, promoting and protecting the rights of women. However, there are significant gaps in terms of institutional arrangements, legal frameworks and policies. IGREENFIN-targeted countries remain among the countries with the lowest levels of gender equality in Africa.

While Senegal ranks higher than the other focal countries, gender gaps remain high across all countries targeted by the IGREENFIN regional programme due to a lack of implementation of laws and policies, and a lack of resources dedicated to reducing gender gaps.

#### a2: The gender assessment was completed based on a desk review. The gender assessment found that women in the five countries targeted by the IGREENFIN regional programme face significant constraints when it comes to their access to credit for agriculture, as well as gender inequalities for accessing other

## The programme aims to build the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate risks in Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal. The programme intends to do so by: (i) providing support to local LMAbs to create green credit lines to finance green agricultural projects for smallholder farmers; (ii) strengthening the capacity of national implementing organizations and MSMEs; (iii) addressing capacity-building, knowledge and policy gaps that hinder the implementation of GCF-funded projects in Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal; and (iv) enhancing the collective impact of all GCF-funded projects, by establishing a coalition of LMAbs to share lessons learned and best practices.

The AE seeks EUR 74.1 million in loans and EUR 30.8 million in technical assistance grants from the GCF. The total cost is estimated at EUR 259.6 million (EUR 199.6 million from the Green Climate Fund, EUR 12.3 million from the International Development Bank (IDB) and the African Development Bank (AfDB) EUR 14.8 million).

### Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute the current programme (medium risk)

The AE, IFAD, has been in place for over 40 years of experience in providing loans and grants to support smallholder farmers. IFAD will create a BCR that will oversee, compile, consolidate and control the work of the EEs. IFAD already has a presence in the region. Given the number of countries involved, it is important to have an entity with a coordinating role to ensure coherence among the activities.

The EEIs for the reimbursable funds are Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal. These entities will be responsible for the disbursement of credit lines and finance. The EEs will be recipients of the GCF financing and guarantee the repayment of the GCF loan portion. The AE notes that the EEs have a good track record in disbursing loans to smallholder farmers from IFAD and other financial institutions on rural finance. The funds will be implemented by the LMAbs.

The EEIs for the GCF grant proceeds for the programme I are: the Ministry of Agriculture, Hydro-Agricultural Development and Mechanization of Burkina Faso; the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Equipment of Côte d'Ivoire; the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock of Ghana; the Ministry of Agriculture of Mali; and the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Equipment of Senegal. The AE has conducted a capacity assessment of the ministries of agriculture of the five targeted countries.

### Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

#### Market demand: Low demand for agricultural financial products offered would result in lower impacts for the programme. Comfort is derived from confirmation received from the AE of the interest rate and grace period for loans, as well as the terms and conditions agreed with governments during the design phase of the programme.

#### Credit risk: GCF credit will be exposed to repayment risks from the governments of Burkina Faso (Côte d'Ivoire), Ghana, Mali and Senegal. The AE has a total exposure of EUR 120 million in reimbursable loans with equal tenure and grace period as GCF.

#### Political and security risk: Burkina Faso and Côte d'Ivoire are included in the red list of the AE. Each country will propose an action plan for the safety of the intervention sites that will be considered by the AE.

#### Tenor: The longer tenor of the programme (up to 40 years) could result in the proposed interventions becoming less additional or of lower impact over time as a result of changes in the operating environment. In such cases, the LMAbs would have the option to repay the loans in case reinvestment in line with the eligibility criteria is no longer feasible.

# Complaints

Complaints must concern environmental, social and climate issues only and should not involve accusations of fraudulent or corrupt activities in relation to programme implementation; these are dealt with by the IFAD Office of Audit and Oversight.

## Addressing complaints

All parties involved in the programme will utilise (to the extent possible) every available grievance redress mechanism, including associations (e.g. farmers') associations, local community groups, village savings and loan associations, etc., village-square engagement (consisting of representatives of men, women and social groups), village general assemblies and the programme's national programme coordination unit.

## Review of GCF portfolio

In accordance with the IFAD Policy on Programme Implementation, a review of the programme in accordance with relevant GCF Board approved policies and does not find any material non-compliance. As such, there is no need to provide further information or documentation for this funding proposal; the OIMC/Complaint Team has determined a risk rating of "medium" and has no objection to this request proceeding to the next step for processing.

The Board will consider the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of result area and single proposal is not material.
