### Fiduciary

IFAD will serve as the AE for this programme. As the AE, IFAD will provide oversight and implementation support, ensure the quality of the programme, and be responsible for ensuring that programme funds are properly accounted for.

The financial management of and procurement for the programme will be carried out in accordance with applicable IFAD rules and practices, as well as the IFAD PIM; the programme-specific budget will be prepared by the AE and submitted to the Board for approval prior to the borrower. Financial statements will be prepared in accordance with internationally accepted standards.

Programme procurement plans will be implemented in accordance with the IFAD Procurement Handbook and procurement plan guidelines will be prepared annually and updated by the programme steering committee.

## Results monitoring and reporting

### The cross-cutting programme covers 13 countries in Africa, namely Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Ethiopia, Eritrea, Kenya, Mali, Niger, Rwanda, Senegal, Somalia, Sudan and Uganda. The programme is part of the GGF Initiative and the second one under the umbrella programme supported by the GCF.

### The target populations of the programme are among the poorest and most climate-vulnerable countries in the world. Climate change, political instability and unsustainable agricultural practices have led to a decline in food security and economic development in these countries.

### The program consists of three components. In component 1, a green financing facility will be established to provide concessional loans to foster adaptation and mitigation best practice projects in agriculture and rural development that are aligned with the GCF target country. In component 2, a technical assistance facility will be created to address capacity, knowledge management, information exchange, digital transformation and other overarching activities.

The program is expected to have a cumulative mitigation impact of 5,637,108 tCO2e over the life program and reach 378,600 direct and 4,294,000 indirect beneficiaries in the 13 countries. Program projects were based on transparent and credible methodologies for greenhouse gas emissions reductions and carbon program implementation. The impact generated in each of the participating countries is described in Annex 13.

The Theory of Change clearly shows the logical pathways for generating results via program activities, and those contribute to the outcomes and impacts of the program. Relevant indicators that measure these logical pathways and their associated outcome bases are addressed has been provided.

The AMA was signed with the AE on 24 September 2018, and it became effective on 9 November 2018.

The proposed programmes will be implemented in 13 countries: Burkina Faso, Chad, Côte d'Ivoire, Djibouti, Ethiopia, Ghana, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal and Sudan. # Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP183

## Proposal name: Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (GREENFIN I): Greening Agricultural Banks & The Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient and Low Emission Agriculture in the Great Green Wall (GGW) Countries – Phase 1

### Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)

#### Country/ies:
- GREENFIN (Components 1 and 2): Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal
- GCF GGW Regional Support Programme (Component 3): Chad, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Somalia and Sudan

#### Programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### Impact potential: Scale: High

1. This lending proposal is for a medium-sized, cross-cutting programme in environmental and social sustainability (ESS) Category B. The programme is submitted by 13 African States, 8 of which have least developed country (LDC) status. Five states will participate in Phase I of the GREENFIN initiative: Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal; plus six other countries: Chad, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Mauritania, Niger (and Sudan). A Regional Support Programme (RSP) under GCF Component 3 will be established. The total amount of funding proposed is USD 214,900,720; including a GCF loan contribution of USD 90,000,000 and a GCF grant contribution of USD 56,000,000, with the balance to be provided by IFAD. The remaining USD 90 million, or 41.5 per cent of the total project cost, includes grant contributions from other donors.
2. The eleven countries of the GGW, and Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana, are among the world's poorest countries with high levels of poverty and food insecurity. Agriculture, livestock, and forestry activities are central to their economies and more than 70 per cent of rural communities depend directly on rainfed agriculture. The region's agriculture, forestry and other land use, and energy sectors contribute significantly to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions generated mainly by deforestation (wood and clearing land for agriculture), the burning of biomass for cooking and heating, and livestock production. Over recent decades, increasing annual average temperatures have combined with increasingly unpredictable rainfall patterns to put significant stress on production of staple and low emission, climate resilient practices.


---
**Page 171**
---

other crops and some parts of the region have experienced more frequent droughts and/or floods. Climate projections indicate that precipitation levels will increase in some areas, while decrease in others, with increasingly erratic timing of growing seasons, while temperatures are expected to rise by up to 2°C above pre-industrial levels over the period from 1980-2005 reference period. These changes, combined with direct human pressures from unsustainable land use and population growth, as well as increasing competition for resources, are expected to cause a decline in agricultural productivity, reducing food availability and farmers’ income opportunities, thereby undermining natural capital, greater inequality, conflict and migration, especially of rural youth.

3. The overall objective of the proposed GCF programme is to create an enabling environment for climate-resilient agriculture in the three selected countries (Mozambique, Madagascar, and Comoros), through: (i) promoting low emission agricultural practices in the GVM area and Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana; IGGRENTIN 1 (Component 1 and 2) will support the development of climate-resilient agriculture to reduce current and future climate risks for the target countries by: (i) providing support to selected national agricultural banks (NABs) for the creation of “green credits lines” to finance the business projects of smallholder farmers, microfinance institutions (MFIs), cooperatives, women’s groups, and youth-led organizations; and micro, small- and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) that will either reduce their greenhouse gas emissions or adapt to climate change through technologies such as solar photovoltaic-powered boreholes for drip irrigation; and (ii) addressing capacity-building, knowledge sharing and technology transfer in the three countries. The Regional Support Programme (Component 3) aims to enhance the capacity of NABs and other financial institutions in the region to finance low-emission IGGRENTIN 1 through the establishment of a coordination framework for knowledge generation and sharing, project implementation and reporting, mobilizing private investment and promoting public-private partnerships.

4. Adaptation impact: The programme will help agricultural communities in the three selected countries (Mozambique, Madagascar, Comoros, Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana) plus Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana, to reduce their vulnerability to a range of natural hazards exacerbated by climate change, including droughts, flooding, wildfires, water scarcity, sandstorms, extreme heat and desertification. The programme will benefit 105,264 smallholder farmers (37% female), 378,600 smallholder farmers, 50 per cent female, and 50 per cent youth, indirectly benefiting the 2.949 million people in their communities. This will contribute to food security and nutrition security, as well as the health of the ecosystems on which their livelihoods depend. This will be achieved through: (i) increasing access to climate-resilient agricultural technologies and practices; (ii) the capacity of five local national agricultural banks to offer affordable financing for climate resilient projects; (iii) the creation of a green credit system in each country, with a green credit indicator system for management, long-term loan uptake and unlocking barriers to innovative climate finance); it will support loans and technical assistance to an estimated 1200 MSMEs and cooperatives; (iv) the development of a regional knowledge sharing platform; and (v) the dissemination of 200 technologies and innovative solutions in agriculture and agro-processing.

5. Impact mitigation: The programme is expected to have a modest but significant impact in the five countries, through GHG emissions avoided as a result of the activities undertaken by the borrowing organizations using renewable energy technologies and

### Green Climate Fund

#### Sustainable Forest and Land Management Practices (with mitigation benefits derived from 50 per cent of the programme’s total costs). The major impact will come from activities undertaken in the farming/agroforestry sector, applying agroclimatic principles and using the farmers’ managed natural resources to improve soil health. These practices are expected to help the region switch from ongoing lines of above- and below-ground carbon through landscape degradation, to effective sequestration of carbon in soils and vegetation.

These activities, with ongoing technical assistance, are expected to result in better practices across 250,000 hectares of land over a period of approximately 25-year lifetime, compared to a scenario without the project which climate change combines with human actions lead to an ever-worsening land degradation and desertification, with consequent loss of livelihoods.

The Regional Sustainable Forest Programme (RSP) is designed to reinforce the adaptation and mitigation efforts already underway in GREENFIN Phase 1. The RSP builds on the results as well as 20 already approved GCF projects being implemented through 17 accredited entities (AEs) in 11 countries, which have a total project value of US$358 million. The RSP will also support initiatives to restore the productivity and vitality of the highly climate-change-vulnerable Sahel region, with an estimated potential for sequestration of approximately 200 million tonnes of carbon, and create 10 million green jobs by 2030.

The RSP aims to strengthen GGW countries’ institutional capacity on reporting, generating and sharing innovative solutions and knowledge products. The RSP will also support the development of a financial mechanism for enhanced financial participation in green business plans. The RSP has a technical capacity component that will provide training and mentoring services to national institutions, as well as peer-to-peer practices and learning and informing policy and investments.

One of the aims of the RSP is to facilitate more consistent and credible reporting of climate change impacts and adaptation activities, which can be better understood as part of the interventions of the GGW. The independent TAP foresees that it will be challenging to verify the impact of the proposed project on the 2030 targets for greenhouse gas emissions related to the GGW, given the diverse nature of the 29 projects already existing, and believes this project will contribute positively in terms of capacity building, monitoring and evaluation systems, but understands that it will be important to try and achieve.

The independent TAP assesses the impact potential of this cross-cutting project as high, including both mitigation and adaptation aspects.


#### Paradigm shift potential: Scale: Medium

The programme aims to enable a paradigm shift to low-emission and climate-resilient agriculture by providing access to affordable and cost-effective green credit lines through the target countries’ national agricultural banks. The intention, as expressed in the funding proposal, is that “Through the mainstreaming work, these institutions will be able to sustain their own operations and continue to provide financing for smallholder farmers with a sustaining loan portfolio that ensures institutionalization of green credit lines within the banks in the long term.” It is expected that the target countries’ national agricultural banks are expected to lend at the same interest rate and thus influence market rates. Subsequently,

interest rates are likely to decrease, gradually making bank loans become more attractive once the IGREENFIN programme has ended.”


In discussion with the AE, the independent TAP expressed the view that the programme’s capacity-building of the national agricultural banks and intermediary financial institutions (IFIs) is not sufficient for them to be able to provide credit on a large scale by enabling more accurate risk assessment and eliminating misperceptions, and also enabling business success and resilience. The AE noted that the programme has had some success in building their influence; however, such as undersupervised communications and transportation infrastructure, and corruption are still major challenges. Despite these challenges, interest rates have decreased, and default rates remain fairly high, once the subsidizing effect of the programme’s concessional financing ends. This is also reflected by the fact that the number of agricultural and other banks post-project increase again, indicating that there has not been a fundamental shift in how the sector operates.

However, given that a general perception of smallholder agriculture as a risky business to one that views this sector as a very marginal activity, it is difficult to attract private banks to invest in this sector.”

### Social co-benefits

The project has a number of important social co-benefits including: strengthening cooperative organizations and businesses and creating expanded employment for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in rural areas, particularly women-led SMEs in villages and small towns, that reduce the phenomenon of rural-urban migration; improving the food security of households through increased yields from existing and new tree crops, and enhancing livestock productivity by diversifying fodder biomass income streams creating opportunities for cash crops and value addition to sustenance crops; enhancing communities' access to agricultural inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, and tools; increasing women's participation in activities previously exposed to such opportunities, and including female-headed households; and improving the livelihoods of pastoralists through increased availability of animal feed and agro-processing through new mini-grids.

### Economic co-benefits

The implementation of the new "green credit lines" in the five large commercial banks and microfinance projects of smallholder farmers, mini-grid providers, and agro-processing businesses should have a significant effect, both in terms of direct job creation and indirect job creation. The project will also create new markets for tree crops and fruits in urban centres and beyond. Enhanced yields and new income streams will be combined with savings on inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, and tools. This will enable smallholder farmers' cooperatives with capacity to take loans to further expand production and processing facilities.

The programme will reduce poverty through increased productivity of existing cash crops and livestock production, and by diversifying into other activities such as the production of value addition to different fruit and non-timber forest products, including tamarind, Arabic gum, beekeeping, and honey. The project will also increase the income from sales in the selected agricultural value chains from production to markets, enabling value addition and preventing post-harvest losses. This is expected to create 105,624 jobs directly and indirectly by creating an estimated 783,600 jobs on totals in farms, in renewable grid operations and agri-businesses, and in the processing of tree crops and fruits. The project will also establish a platform that could spur economic development across all the countries of the Great Green Wall.

### Gender sensitivity and social inclusion

The funding proposal and gender analysis achieved the following: (1) The proposed project activities are consistent with the national strategies of the five target countries, as well as the high rate of youth under- and unemployed, which increases the potential for economic growth. (2) The gender analysis shows that women play a central role in feeding their families and ensuring other basic needs (water, firewood, etc.). Women also contribute to agricultural production by planting trees and managing declining crop yields due to climate change. Although women represent 60 to 70 per cent of the on-farm work effort, they often lack access to productive assets, finance and knowledge. The programme will address this issue through a number of activities that will provide economic benefits to smallholder farmers and pastoralists, both men and women. Injection of loan funds and technical assistance into these communities will enable them to diversify their income, make more animal fodder available, and reduce time needed for wood and water collection.

25. The programme will likely face many challenges in reducing entrenched gender inequalities and that the loan will be made without collateral requirements and with flexibility on proving ability to repay, all of which will enhance women's chances of accessing finance to grow their own businesses. A total of 70 per cent of the loan will be dedicated to women-led MSMEs, cooperatives and farmer organizations, with a special focus on female-headed households in rural areas within the region. The other 50 per cent will be dedicated to men, of which 50 per cent will be youth-led MSMEs. A gender action plan will be developed as part of the project design process. Gender actions will be developed to strengthen the technical and managerial capacities of women, aimed at providing them with tools and skills for identifying and developing bankable business plans and accessing finance. The effectiveness of these interventions will be measured by gender-disaggregated data will be assessed against the appropriate indicator to measure women's empowerment in the project area. At least 50 per cent of the project steering committee members will be women, to ensure that they influence the main strategic decisions.

### Needs of the recipient Scale: High

27. The needs of the five target countries of the western Sahel, proposed to be involved in IGEREEN1N and the 13 counties of the entire Great Green Wall, proposed to be involved in the Regional Programme, are related to food security, water resources management, land degradation, climate change, and in relation to the level of development of the financial sector.

28. As outlined in the funding proposal: “The Sahel stretches from the Atlantic Ocean to the Horn of Africa and includes 15 countries, including all of West Africa south of the Sahara Desert, and parts of coastal countries such as Benin, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Guinea and Togo. One of the largest semi-arid and arid regions in the world, it is characterized by a high degree of inter-decadal variability, with the Sahel Desert to the north, which is expanding southward up to 10 km per year, depending on the severity of droughts. The Sahel is one of the most vulnerable areas to desertification and degraded regions in the world and therefore one of the most vulnerable to climate change, as the necessary adaptation measures are not always available or affordable. In addition, extreme events will only worsen the already harsh living and growing conditions. According to the IPCC [Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change] 4th Assessment Report, monsoon precipitation is projected to decrease by up to 20 per cent in the Sahel region over the next century. The onset of the monsoon season is expected to have a delayed onset and retreat. The IPCC also reported an observed increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, and projected increases in meteorological droughts at GWL [groundwater level] (mostly in seasonal rainfall areas) and hydrological droughts.”

29. Scientists agree that there has been a gradual warming that is quite variable but has averaged out to 0.5°C per decade across West Africa, and at 1°C per decade in the Sahel, since the late 1970s. The IPCC also reported an observed increase in the frequency of extreme weather questions from the independent TAP about the specific experience of five IGEREEN1 N target countries. In addition to the general warming trend, there has been a decrease in meteorological data on normal, dry and very dry years for at least 30 years for four of the countries (Benin, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana and Togo), with an average increase of about 1 year per decade, but showed a high degree of inter-decadal variability. It is therefore inadvisable to state, as the funding proposal does, that “climate change has increased the frequency, intensity and duration of extreme weather events.”

30. The AIF highlighted a number of publications analysing meteorological data from the Sahel region over time, all of which concluded that the region experienced extremely high variability in rainfall during the twentieth century with wet periods during the 1970s and 1980s following a wet period in the 1950s and 1960s. Since the late 1990s, rainfall has partly


---
**Page 177**
---

recovered to earlier levels, but has been accompanied by changes in the intra-seasonal scale compared to the 1950s and 1960s, for example delayed onset and later retreat of the main rainy season in Mali's Sahelian regions, resulting in uncertainties regarding when to plant crops.

* The All Africa case study which investigated the recent precipitation changes in the West African Sahel (covering the entire region) targeted a high resolution Climate Hazards Group InfraRed Precipitation with Station product over the period 1981-2015. This analysis shows an increase of wet days (defined as days with more than 0.1 mm rainfall) in the central part of the West African Sahel (+3 mm d−1 on average). However, this overall increase in precipitation is associated with a decrease in the number of wet days over the western part of the region and over the entire West African Sahel (hand on average by 30%), and with precipitation intensity that is decreasing (around 3 mm d−1 during the study period) in the western part of the West African Sahel.

* The independent TAP acknowledges that there is significant complexity in observed meteorological data, and that it is difficult to make definitive statements about future climate scientists' rainfall projections for the region over the coming decades. Scientists agree, however, that the high inter-annual and inter-decadal variability that results from the effects of the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ) shifting as the climate evolves, and increasing temperatures will enhance evapotranspiration.

* However, the observed changes in rainfall patterns are likely to increase the vulnerability of rural communities and crop production in the Sahelian regions of the target countries, and that the needs of these regions for improved agricultural productivity and resilience are not being met by existing small-scale producers and MSMEs largely excluded from the commercial financial sector historically. Because of this, there is a clear need to support the development of the rural economy's potential (with the right technical assistance and value chain strengthening), there is a clear need to support the development of the rural economy's potential (with the right technical assistance and value chain strengthening), there is a clear need to support the development of the rural economy's potential (with the right technical assistance and value chain strengthening).

* Despite the ongoing efforts of the Governments of the target countries and their financial and technical partners to strengthen resilience of the agriculture sector and enhance climate change adaptation, there are still significant gaps in the provision of finance for small-scale producers. The programme will see investment in improving the enabling environment for central banks to access affordable capital for rural lending, as well as support for the development of a range of innovative financial products that can be used by commercial lenders to scale up commercial finance to the agricultural sector in these countries, paving the way for greater private sector involvement and closer adaptation and mitigation.

* The needs of the recipients in this programme are rated as high.

### Country ownership has been demonstrated for both the IIGRENFIN programme Phase 1 in the five target countries (Components 1 and 2), and the Regional Support Programme for the 11 Great Green Wall countries (Component 3).

- IIGRENFIN was designed at the request of the GCF national designated authorities (NDAs) of the five target countries, following approval of the simplified approval process IFAD-Niger IIGRENFIN project (SAP012) “Inclusive Green Financing for Climate Resilient and Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture” in December 2019. In February 2020, the NDAs, the Chief Executive Officers of the agriculture and investment banks, and government officials from Niger met with IFAD to request IFAD’s support to provide similar projects with GCF. The meeting led to the formation of a coalition of five national development agencies (NDAs) in Niger providing financing for climate resilient, low-emission smallholder agriculture. IFAD then engaged the five NDAs as co-funders and the target countries in developing the programme, including consultations with the country’s key stakeholders such as the five Ministries of Economy and/or Finance an national executing entities (EEs), and by the five local national development banks. The coalition also included the GCF trust fund, the revolving fund structure, bonds well for the loan facilities being sustained post project.

- The IIGRENFIN programme is aligned with the five countries’ national development plans and strategies, as well as their commitments to the Paris Agreement in their national adaptation programmes of action, national climate change policies and strategies, national biodiversity strategies and action plans, the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), and national strategies for disaster risk reduction, as well as to the GCF’s strategic plan.

- The independent TAP notes that the NDAs of four of the five target countries have recently been revised, with new versions submitted to the UNFCCC between December 2020 and October 2021. The TAP also notes that the NDAs are increasingly aware that the transfer of climate resilient, low carbon technologies and the development of innovative green business models is a key element in their adaptation and mitigation targets contained in each country’s NDC.

- For the Regional Support Programme, countries own its design through the process of developing the programme. In September 2020, at an online meeting organized by the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCCD) and involving the responsible NDAs of all 11 Great Green Wall countries, the UNCCD Secretariat issued a joint Ministerial Declaration inviting GCF and other international entities to increase their support for the GGWP. The GGWP was officially launched at the GCF’s third replenishment, January 2021 One Planet Summit saw a range of development partners committing to the launch of a Great Green Wall Accelerator, to be led by the Pan African Agency for the Great Green Wall (PAAGWW) and supported by the GCF. The GGWP will provide technical coordination, collaboration and monitoring of GGW efforts.

- In addition, the RSP is designed as an integral part of the Great Green Wall Regional Support Programme of the GCF, and will work in partnership with other agencies including the PAGWW, UNCCD, United Nations Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO), Global Environment Facility (GEF) and others. The RSP is being developed by the AE for all 11 Great Green Wall countries. Component 3 of this programme, the RSP, is designed to build on the success of the IIGRENFIN project and the GCF’s support for the GGW initiative. At the country level, the RSP aims to enhance the capacities of GCF NDAs, as well as of the GEF and other agencies involved in the GGWP, through the engagement of youth systems and knowledge management specialists, and through south-south knowledge exchanges to help in the planning of future GCF, GEF and Adaptation Fund projects and their implementation.

1. **Efficiency and effectiveness:** Scale: Medium

2. The total project cost is USD 216,900,720, including a GCF grant contribution of USD 36,900,720. The GCF USD-90 million loan contribution is matched with USD 90 million of co-financing from the World Bank and other development partners, bringing total project financing to USD 51 million. Concessional loan finance totalling USD 48 million, from IFAD, Islamic Development Bank (IDB), and Asian Development Bank (ADB) will be blended with the highly concessional GCF loan funds to provide an affordable interest rate for loans to smallholder farmers and small businesses, many of whom have been historically excluded from the market for loan finance.

3. The cost of the GCF investment in the five target countries is USD 110,283,232 (Component 4). This represents a significant increase over the USD 57 million invested by the GCF in the same five countries under Component 3. Cost-effectiveness comes from its cross-cutting nature – with, on one hand, climate resilience and cost-effectiveness coming from agriculture and selected agricultural value chains contributing to climate change adaptation, and on the other hand, the co-benefits of these investments being applied to climate change mitigation within those same value chains. Component 3 is designed to add value to the GCF’s existing portfolio in the five target countries by providing USD 57 million at a cost of USD 13,035 per project. It is noted that 65 per cent of the project's benefits correspond to climate change adaptation and 35 per cent to mitigation. This corresponds to an average cost-effectiveness ratio (CER) for adaptation of USD 84 per million USD invested in the five target countries, as well as switching to solar PV for powering rural agricultural and agro-processing enterprises.

4. In response to a question from the independent TAP about how high the cost of the grant portion of the project is compared with other GCF projects, it was noted that the GCF has allocated USD 139 million for the Regional Support Programme in Component 3 – the All-Clear initiative “that grant resources are being used to address three key barriers to scaling up climate change adaptation and mitigation practices in green business projects: (i) capacity and knowledge gaps; hindering the transfer and adoption of effective climate adaptation and mitigation practices in green business projects; (ii) limited access to green finance for smallholder farmers and small businesses, which is a major barrier to increasing the economic feasibility of potential green business projects in the GCF area. Component 3 will provide USD 10 million in grant resources over five years through this grant to support the implementation of a loan portfolio of about USD 30 million per country [GCF and blended finance] for smallholder farmers and small businesses, which is being provided by the GCF, such as SAP Nger, where the grant portion is USD 5 million vs. USD 7 million; the ADB USD 49 million loan, where the grant portion is USD 10 million vs. USD 38 million; and IDB concessional loans.” The independent TAP notes that this technical assistance loan ratio for GCF/IDB of 1:8.11 is much more cost-effective than those for the comparable programmes, which have ratios of about 1:2.

5. With regard to the other grant portion, Component 3 – the Regional Support Programme in the All-Clear initiative – will provide USD 10 million over five years through a regional support programme and an overarching framework for GCF-funded projects to strengthen institutional capacity on reporting, generating and sharing innovative solutions and knowledge, and increasing access to finance. The RSP will draw lessons from the learnings of the GCF initiative and past programmes such as the World Bank SAWAP (Solar Access in West Africa Programme) and the ADB’s Solar for All programme. Led by leaders to support the GCF agenda, it is strategic to provide the Regional Support Programme with enough grants to play a key role in: (i) enhancing knowledge management and exchanges

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

This programme meets all the investment criteria of GCF. It represents an important intervention to collectively contribute to GCF to the climate risk management objectives of the Great Green Wall, and to the efforts of the five target countries to support vulnerable rural communities in adapting to climate change. The project is expected to have a significant but modest significant contribution to GHG emissions reductions.

If the programme is approved by the GCF Board, the independent TAP makes the following recommendations for consideration on how to enhance the programme's contribution to the investment criteria:

(a) The AF should conduct an analysis of the revised nationally determined contributions of four of the five target countries for Components 1 and 2, which have been submitted to the UNFCCC subsequent to the development of the funding proposal, to ensure that REDD+ activities are aligned with national priorities.

(b) The AF should work with the TAC in the inception phase of Component 3, the regional Support Programme, to clarify the expected deliverables of the activities, as well as the differences and relationships between project-level and RSP-level indicators and results. This will help ensure that the programme is designed to effectively deliver and monitor portfolio-wide achievements.

The independent TAP recommends this funding proposal for approval by the Board.

## Impact potential
The AE welcome this assessment of potential impact of the program as High and thank ITAP for the comprehensive assessment.

## Paradigm shift potential
The AE thank ITAP for the assessment of the program paradigm shift as Medium. Traditional to the present, the GEF has been the only multilateral institution providing Islamic finance, which applied lower interest rate than the market and fully institutionalized with the financial sector after just few years of its launch. These low interest rates have been set for specific projects and not for general use by the financial sector. The program intends to work with Central Banks and Financial Institutions to include Green Finance in their mandate and to provide Islamic finance products and other special products in the region. The AE believes with this program, low-interest rate financing and climate resilient agriculture will be institutionalized beyond the program duration.

The AE welcome this assessment of potential impact of the program as High and thanks ITAP for the comprehensive assessment.

# Introduction

In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social policy, a Gender Assessment has been conducted for the Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGEFIN), a regional program dedicated to greening agricultural banks and the financial sector to foster climate resilient and low emission smallholder in five West African countries of the Great Green Wall initiative (GGWI). Women are targeted as key stakeholders in this initiative. The Gender Assessment is intended to provide an overview of the gender issues in five countries, namely Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal. This document will also highlight the opportunities for women to access finance to redirect financial flows towards the sub-projects of Micro, Small and Medium-sized Enterprises (MSMEs) and Women’s Empowerment in Agriculture (WEA). The GCF project aims at reducing GHG emissions therefore. Therefore, the proposed adaptation and mitigation solutions for the GCF project are to: access to finance and further promote the adoption of climate resilient, low emission agricultural practices and technologies that will contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gases to reduce GHG emissions.

The IGEFIN Regional Program focuses on 3 countries of the Great Green Wall (Burkina Faso, Mali, Senegal) and Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana, one of the most environmentally degraded region in the world, also considered as one of the most vulnerable regions to climate change, regularly hit by droughts and floods. West Africa is expected to be among the regions most affected by climate change due to the most persistent and early emerging prominent hot spots forecast for the twenty-first century because of its location at the northern edge of the tropical rainforest belt where deforestation and desertification are occurring. According to an IPCC report, West Africa and the Sahel are likely to experience increases in the number of hot nights and longer and more frequent heatwaves even if the global temperature increase is limited to 2°C above pre-industrial levels. The region is also expected to experience a decrease in rainfall, despite uncertainties on precipitation trends. Most of West African countries are likely to experience decreasing rainfall, despite uncertainties on precipitation trends. Most of West African countries are likely to experience decreasing rainfall, despite uncertainties on precipitation trends.

The region vulnerability to climate change is exacerbated by its high dependence on rain-fed agriculture. Agricultural activities account for 70% of total employment in the region and crops are still all grown in these areas. Although it is an important source of income and employment for the majority of rural populations, smallholder farmers face a number of constraints that limit their access to productive resources. This is especially relevant when it comes to access to financial resources for agriculture. Credit to the agricultural sector accounts for roughly, 2% of total credit offered; and when it is available, the interest rates are very high. According to the World Bank’s Agriculture Sector Diagnostic Study (ASDS) and Monetary Union (WAEMU) zone, agriculture receives only 5.10% of total credit in the short term, 6.80% in the medium-term and 7.30% in the long-term. The lack of access to credit is a major constraint for smallholder producers to invest in agriculture place women at a disadvantaged and vulnerable position as they are less likely to have access to credit reduces their access to input, land, other productive resources and extension services.

Gender inequalities persist in the agriculture sector of the five countries targeted by IGEFIN Regional Program. Women play an important role in agricultural production but face significant constraints for agriculture, in addition to gender inequalities for accessing to other productive resources. The gendered division of labour in the households place the burden of securing water and fuel supplies and caring for the children on women. This is further exacerbated when women are also responsible for income-generating activities or to further their education. On average, women spend six times more than men on unpaid care work – cooking, cleaning, collecting water and firewood, and caring for children,


---
**Page 187**
---

## Component 1: Green Business Support (GBS)

The GBS component will be established to provide concessional loans to foster better adaptation and mitigation practices for green businesses within the selected agricultural value chains in Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Mali, and Senegal. The targeted banks (LNBs) are:
- The Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso
- The National Investment Bank of Côte d’Ivoire
- The AIBN Bank of Mali
- The National Development Bank of Senegal

The GBS component will offer special lines of financing for green businesses prepared by farmer’s organizations (FOs) and/or other local actors, including agribusiness dealers and solar operators. The proposed investment criteria and list of specific eligible green business projects are presented in Annex 1.

## Component 2: Technical Assistance Facility (TAF)

The TAF will be created to address capacity, knowledge, and policy gaps hindering the uptake of green business projects in the selected countries. It will target public institutions, private sector actors, civil society organizations, and youth. The TAF will provide support for specific technical assistance to the following Direct Access Entities (DAEs) in Burkina Faso: Caisse d’Épargne et de Retraite du Burkina (CEREB), and in Mali: Société Économique (SE). A strategic IGREENF1 partner that is to perform activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.8 with an emphasis on the creation of enabling environments for financing green business projects will provide technical assistance and policy regulatory support to the Central Bank of the West African Economic and Monetary Union (BCEAO) in Côte d’Ivoire, and the National Development Bank of Senegal in Ghana on the creation of an enabling environment for financing the uptake of green business projects (pillar 1). The TAF will also provide technical assistance and policy regulatory support to the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources Management (pillar 2) and the Ministry of Water and Land Resources (pillar 3) Climate resilient infrastructure access and improved management of ecosystems and land restoration.

## Component 3: GGGI Regional Support Programme

This component will increase the collective impacts of the individual GGB projects and programs (including IGREENF1 and T) through two outputs. Output 1: The GGBs will be scaled up by sharing lessons learned, best practices, and good practices; increasing learning and info policy and investments across GGB and other projects. Output 2: Digital tools and digital innovation technologies are mapped and a digital and innovation ecosystem built.

This Gender Assessment is based on quantitative and qualitative evidence derived from a literature review and secondary databases from national and international sources. For each country, the gender analysis is conducted addressing gender inequalities at the national level, as well as summarizing the existing legislation and policies related to gender equality. A Gender Action Plan (GAP) will be developed to set the tone on how gender issues resulting from the assessment can be addressed through the implementation of specific actions. The GAP action list will be updated the first year and resubmitted to the GCF after 6 months of each country's implementation.

# Key gender indicators for IGRENN countries

IGRENN targeted countries are among the one with five rankings in terms of gender indicators, as well as HDI (Human Development Index). Table 1: The Social Institutions Gender Index (SIGI) measures discrimination against women in social institutions across 180 countries. The SIGI shows clear regional trends; Western Africa being one of the region where legislation, social norms and practices are most discriminatory towards women. In this region, there is a strong correlation between a social norm lens to gender equality*. Among the IGRENN countries, Mali and Côte d'Ivoire are the only ones that have a high score in the SIGI, which measures discrimination against women across the four dimensions of the SIGI on women's access to productive and financial resources. The Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) is an index that measures progress towards gender equality in four key areas: Economic Participation and Opportunity, Education Attainment, Health and Survival, and Political Empowerment) and tracks progress towards closing these gaps over time. In all IGRENN countries, the gap between men and women in terms of education attainment is very large. Senegal has the largest among the four gaps tracked by the Index. Senegal is far better than the other four countries where more than 50% of the population are still out of school. The GGGI measures gender equality across all four dimensions, which are economic participation and opportunity, education attainment, health survival, and political empowerment. The Gender inequality that measures the gender inequalities in three aspects of human development index components: education, health, and economic activity. In terms of education, Senegal is better than the other countries. As shown in Table 2, the maternal mortality ratio is high in all targeted countries, ranging from 388 deaths per 100,000 live births in Ghana to 671 in Côte d'Ivoire. Regarding the percentage of seats in parliament held by women: 4 regions belong to 5% for Mali and AI 2% for Senegal.

1. According to the Global Gender Gap Report (2021), Ghana scored 0.598, which places it in 113th position out of 164 countries*. More specifically, Ghana scored:
   - (i) 0.588 in economic participation and opportunity, with a ranking of 119th position,
   - (ii) 0.978 for Health and Survival; ranked at the 40th position,
   - (iv) 0.135 for political empowerment, placing it at the 126th position.
   
Despite its progress in some areas such as women's leadership to mainstream gender at various levels, it is still lagging behind in terms of gender empowerment and equality. The report highlights that cultural norms and stereotypes are among the biggest barriers to gender equality and women’s empowerment.

2. The Government of Ghana has made great strides in enacting the legal frameworks for promoting gender equality and women's empowerment. Indeed, Ghana's supreme law, the Constitution, which fundamentally commits the country to the elimination of gender discrimination and provides the constitutional framework for the protection of human rights, is gender-sensitive and applicable to all citizens, men and women. In addition, the country set out national legal and policy frameworks and related institutions to promote gender equality and women’s empowerment.

The 1992 Constitution ensures gender equality and freedom of both men and women, girls and boys from discrimination based on social and economic status among others. Ghana engaged in institutional, administrative and legislative reforms to ensure that the law is gender-sensitive and applicable to all citizens, men and women. At the national level, there are the Domestic Violence Act (2015), the Child Marriage Prohibition Act (2016) and the Anti-Traditional Practices Act (2017). The 1998 Children Act prohibits discrimination against children on grounds of gender or other similar status. Ghana is a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), and it is a signatory of the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, the Beijing Platform for Action, the Commonwealth Plan of Action, the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights, the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (1999) and the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), as well as the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights. Ghana is a party to the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (2003) and ratified on 13 June 2007. (ii) The UN Security Council Resolution 1325 and 1820 on Women, Peace and Security and violence against women; (iii) The 2015 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs); (iv) The Convention Against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment – New York (11 December 2004, ratified 7 September 2000), etc.

3. Ghana is a party to the CEDAW, Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women; and the Optional Protocol thereto. The Government of Ghana has demonstrated its commitment to the tenets of the Convention by ensuring that the Constitution, new legislation and policies are gender-sensitive and applicable to all citizens, men and women. In 2015, the Government set up an agenda for national action to end such discriminatory practices. Ghana has submitted jointly its sixth and seventh report to CEDAW in 2014, highlighting various areas where progress have been made in implementing the Convention. The country is also a signatory of the Beijing Declaration and Platform of Action (1995), which is an agenda for women’s empowerment with the


---
**Page 191**
---

commitment to remove all obstacles constraining the active participation of women in all spheres of public and private life. It also aims to guarantee that women have full and equal share in economic, social, cultural and political decision-making in alignment with the Sustainable Development Goals, an explicit aim is to ensure gender equality and women empowerment.

The achievements in the period 2014-2019 under review have been categorised under four major themes: gender equality and women empowerment; mainstreaming gender into socio-economic development; development of policies and strategies for gender equality and women empowerment; and institutional arrangements.

### At the policy level, the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection (MoGCSP) has developed the National Gender Policy (NGP). Its overarching goal is to maintain gender equality and women's empowerment in all spheres of life. The NGP seeks to address the social, economic, political, civic, political, economic and cultural conditions of the people of Ghana, particularly women and men, boys and girls in an appropriate manner and as required by National and International Frameworks.

In order to achieve this goal, the NGP has identified four strategic objectives. The first is to identify committed roles to women's rights and access to justice; the second is to empower and livelihoods of women through economic empowerment; the third is to mainstream gender equality into Ghana's economic justice, and gender roles and relations. The NGP has an implementation plan since 2016, but which lacks good coordination.

In fact, there are several challenges that constrain the effective implementation of policies on gender equality and women empowerment. They include competing government priorities and political will; weak gender mainstreaming coordinating role of the Ministry; conceptual clarification of gender equality in the policy document; lack of adequate budgetary allocation for gender equality and women's empowerment; machinery and weak accountable governance. Regional stakeholders reported confusion as to when the implementation plan would be launched, and the lack of a clear communication strategy to government stakeholders, working with other agencies remains a challenge given that policies and activities frequently do not align with the National Gender Policy.

### Another important challenge to the effectiveness of strategies and plans on gender equality and women's empowerment is inadequate funding from the Government. For example, a main challenge to the implementation of the National Gender Policy (2015) seems to be the lack of reliable and sustainable funding for its implementation. The Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection (MoGCSP) has the mandate to spearhead the achievement of gender equality and women's empowerment; the key challenges are that there is no clear budgetary allocation for the MoGCSP and the Ministry is reliant on donor funds to implement programmes". The same problem of insufficient budget is also reported at local levels. In addition, the National Development Planning Commission (NDPC) and the District Assemblies are unclear about gender issues, and they do not budget or allocate sufficient resources for gender sensitive development planning and budgets". In addition, a requirement of the National Development Plan (2017-2021) is that all government agencies and departments are required to allocate at least 40 per cent of their budget to gender issues, but which is not being respected".

### Institutional arrangements

The Ghana Women's National Council on Women and Development (NCWD), established after the first United Nations Conference on Women in 1975. The NCWD became part of the Office of the President after the Beijing Conference in 1995, operating thereafter in close collaboration with relevant MDAs. A Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection (MoGCSP) was created in 2001, with regional departments and integrated gender desks in other MDAs. The MoGCSP was renamed in 2018 as the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection (MoGCSP).


---
**Page 192**
---

charge of gender equality and women empowerment, as well as social protection and welfare issues. The MoGSCP’s mandate is to promote gender equality and safeguard the welfare and full participation of women in social, political and economic development process. There is also the Domestic Violence and Child Protection Act (2016) which seeks to address violence against women and girls on sexual and gender-based violence, child abuse and child protection, and protection of all vulnerable persons from abuse.”


### Further, the Ministry of Health implemented additional measures, particularly on GBV. Indeed, this Ministry has developed an action plan to establish protocols for better case management for GBV survivors and to ensure collaboration among the health sector, social services, and law enforcement agencies. The MoGSCP is also working with other stakeholders in the country to promote women’s empowerment. Ghana is still facing challenges and significant gaps remain. The designated gender desk officers are not always available or accessible due to their heavy workload. This has made it hard accountable by the MoGSCP since their appointments are discretionary, arrangement that limits the effectiveness of the GBV response.


### Further, the MoGSCP Department of Gender (responsible for coordinating and monitoring them) is unable to ensure the necessary follow-up because of its institutional weakness. As such, the contribution of the MoGSCP towards the implementation of the National Strategy on GBV remains weak. The absence of any multi-sector coordination mechanism to ensure the implementation of the NGSP within all sectors has also been a major challenge. This is due to the lack of resources and the necessary capacities and budget to mainstream gender different sections. Furthermore, competing government priorities, lack of effective monitoring and evaluation systems in the MoGSCP and Department of Gender, as well as the absence of a clear accountability framework are among key challenges to the implementation of Ghana’s national legal frameworks and international commitments.”


### Education

10. Gender parity in education is improving in Ghana, although gaps still exist at secondary and tertiary levels. The gender gap in basic and lower secondary education has made significant progress. Compared to most Sub-Saharan African countries, Ghana is relatively high literacy and net primary school enrollment with more girls attending primary school than boys. Although there are some disparities between urban and rural areas, the gender parity index (GPI) is close at both levels of schooling. The GPI for girls in primary education is 0.98 compared to 1.02 for boys while it is 0.53 for girls, and the literacy rate of men in Ghana outstrips that of women.” The gender gaps for enrollment at secondary level are thought to be higher in rural areas and the northern part of the country.


### Furthermore, there has been a significant increase in female students pursuing Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) majors, which is likely to affect their professional life. Only 22.5 per cent of women pursue STEM-related courses at university level compared with 30% men. The underrepresentation of women in these programmes affect their ability to secure employment in the sector, particularly in the fields traditionally dominated by males. A study on the cases of females offering STEM subjects at the senior secondary school level revealed that girls are less confident about their abilities and their motivation to take STEM-related programs.” Several factors contribute to the imbalances between male and female students pursuing STEM courses. These include the belief held by some that girls are over-burdened by household workload, adolescent pregnancy, and the low importance parents seem to have on the benefits of girls’ education.


---
**Page 193**
---

12. According to the Ghana Living Standard Survey round 8 (GLSS 8), the workload at home for girls is three times more than of boys, and the proportion of girls who are out of school due to domestic chores in 2014 stood at 13.1 compared to 3.2 for boys, on average. Furthermore, Ghanaian parents expect their daughters to be married by age 18 while they expect sons to marry later. This trend culminates in higher investment in boys' education to the disadvantage of their female counterparts at senior secondary school. According to the SDG progress report, while Ghana has been able to provide access to primary and secondary education for both girls and boys, this is not enough. Poor learning outcomes are evidenced by proficiency in learning and pass rates. Indeed, Ghanaian women spend approximately 10 hours a day on domestic chores compared with men's two hours.

### Health

13. Significant progress has been made in improving maternal health. According to the World Bank data, the maternal mortality ratio has decreased from 464 to 328 per 100,000 live births between 2000 and 2015. More efforts need to be done in order to achieve the SDGs target to reduce the global maternal mortality ratio to less than 70 per 100,000 live births. Although fertility remains relatively high in Ghana (4.8 children per woman), it has declined from a peak of 6.2 children per woman in 1995 and is now just under four according to the Ghana Demographic and Health Survey. The number of women who have had at least one child increased from 30 percent in rural areas to an average of 47.7 compared to 3.3 for those in urban areas, northern regions, and southern regions. Women with secondary education or higher are more likely than those without education to have twice as many children than women with more than secondary education (0.5 versus 2.7), and women in poorest households have an average of 5.7 children, compared to 2.8 children among women living above the poverty line. The proportion of births attended by skilled health personnel has increased from 41 percent in 2003 to 69 percent in 2015. The proportion of births attended by a skilled health worker increased from 25 percent in 2000 to 79 percent in 2017, yet wide disparities exist across regions, rural–urban areas and income profiles.

14. As for HIV, women comprise nearly 60 per cent of those living with the virus, with an increasing noted for the new infections among young women aged between 15 to 24 years. Women are more likely to be HIV positive as urban men; and rural women are two times as likely to be HIV positive as rural men. The limited knowledge in terms of prevention and testing is a major challenge. In addition, lack of access to information about family planning methods could explain that women are discharging compared to men. However, more women than men use modern contraception (40 per cent versus 25 per cent) at the same age group. Child marriage remains a challenge in Ghana, despite recent declines resulting from the considerable progress made since 1987. The proportion of girls who were married before age 15 has fallen significantly between 1987 and 2017. According to the 2017 MICS data, only one per cent of women aged 20–24 years were first married before age 15 years; child marriage being prominent in rural areas, in northern regions, and southern regions. The high prevalence of early marriage is associated with higher incidence of child marriage in rural areas lead to higher rates of dropout of girls, resulting in more gender disparity between boys and girls in terms of education. In addition, the proportion of women who are married in Ghana are married, and one-fifth of them are mothers, while the corresponding figures for boys are ten times less. Findings from the GLSS 9 confirm gender disparities about marriage; it is terms of mean age and median age at this marriage. Indeed, the mean age at first marriage, 23.1


---
**Page 194**

years, with females marrying about four years earlier than their male counterparts do, 21.3 and 25.7 years respectively. As for the median age at first marriage, it ranges from 18 years to 20 years, that for females ranges from 19 to 22 years while that of males ranges from 24 to 26 years. Furthermore, girls are also more likely than boys to be married before their 15th birthday (UNICEF, 2017). According to DHS 2016, approximately 27.7 per cent of Ghanaian women had experienced at least one form of domestic violence (physical, emotional, psychological, social and sexual violence)(a). According to the 2014 DHS, more than three in ten women aged 15–49 years reported having been physically or sexually abused by a partner at some point during their lifetime. More than seven per cent of women report that a husband is justified in hitting her if she burns food, and four percent say they have experienced sexual violence from a partner without telling him/her. Two percent of them neglect the children, and 12 percent if she refuses to have sex. Further, 30% of women report having been raped at some point during their lifetime. This is also a concern, with 11 per cent of girls reporting sexual harassment (compared to 6 per cent of boys).

### Employment

Gender gaps are still a reality in the labour market, even though women are economically active. Although Ghana’s economic participation rates are high in Ghana, men represent the majority of the economically active population. According to the GLSS3, among those aged 15 years and older, 71 percent were economically active (participating or not) while for females it was only 60 per cent of this population; males (72.3 percent) were more likely to be economically active than female (89.7 percent). The male-female ratio in the economically active population is 4:1. Among those aged between 15 and 64, and that of women 74.9 per cent according to the GLSS3. The employment gap by gender is evident in almost all the occupational groups with higher proportions of males engaged in various employment activities than females.

### Gender disparities in employment can be illustrated by the high presence of women in low-skilled work

The low female participation rates in the formal sector, the higher likelihood of working as unpaid family workers or in informal enterprises, and the lower proportion of women in managerial positions are some of the reasons for this. Women’s economic participation is mostly in self-employment and the informal sector, such as catering and lodging, food services, textiles, garments, and beauty, while only 3.4 per cent of women work in agriculture. The majority (58%) of women who are economically active are engaged in vulnerable employment: About 77.7 percent of females were engaged in vulnerable employment compared to 60% for males. Women’s participation rate is also low in the formal sector, with only 42.4 per cent of males (own-account workers - 42%), contributing family members – 11.5%). Gender disparities are more pronounced among women in the countries in Africa where gender gaps in income are large, estimated to be 60-90 percent.

### Women are mostly involved in self-employment

In informal businesses in the textile, garment and household services sectors, women represent a significant proportion of employment in Ghana, engaging 3.6 million households (41% of all households in the country) that run micro-enterprises. These enterprises include catering and lodging, food services (making and selling snacks or meals), transport, and personal services such as bartering and hairdressing. In Ghana, evidence shows that the majority of women own their businesses with an average capital investment of $105 USD, and they are operating in less profitable areas than men are. Women face higher constraints than men to access credit for business purposes, and many have been running small one-person enterprises sometimes for years, with no prospect (and intention) to grow. The main challenges that these women face include lack of skills in business management and entrepreneurship, access to

# Agriculture

## Gender Disparities and Agricultural Productivity

### Women's Participation and Challenges

18. Although women are active in the agriculture sector, they still lag behind in terms of income, access to productive resources, and finance. Women participate in rural agricultural labour markets but gender disparity is a major concern. Indeed, nearly two-thirds (38.0 percent) of females were self-employed compared with 51.7 percent of males who were self-employed according the GLEST. However, the proportion of males who were self-employed without employees in the agriculture sector was higher than that for women (62.4% vs. 49.3%). This suggests that it is more likely to be employed in agricultural activities as against their female counterparts who prefer to engage in non-agricultural activities. In rural areas, we found more males in the skilled agricultural labour market.

### Gender-Based Disparities

19. Apart from participation to labour markets, gender-based inequalities in agriculture also apply to access to and control over resources, which affect women's agricultural productivity. These gender inequalities exist in both formal and informal sectors of the economy. Despite a progressive land ownership system, customary land use systems prevail whereby property rights of women are not recognized. Women have less access to productive inputs such as fertilizers than men do. In Ghana and women have temporary usufruct rights that can be lost when they marry or lose their husband. This is because women's tenure security in the agricultural sector is lower compared with male farmers on the total farms than women and 8 times more of the medium-large farms (5 acres and more). Female headed households have less access to land for farming (89% in Northern Ghana vs a 96% national average) and they are also less likely to own livestock (40% compared with 72% of male-headed households or less). Approximately 87% of female-headed households are smallholders compared with 50% of male. About 30% of women farmers in the study area have no access to land, while only about 16% of men lack access to land (i.e. collateral) also results in access that is more limited to financial services.

### Gender-Based Disparities in Productivity and Yields

For gender-based disparities regarding earnings and yields, a study on gender and cocoa in Ghana pointed out that women farmers have lower productivity than male farmers. Women produce 10% less than male-headed, typical households (USD 125) after hired labour and input costs are deducted[3]. As a result of this low productivity, women earn about USD 48 per month compared with USD 67 for female-headed households (37kg/ha). For the cocoa production in Ghana, evidence shown that difference between male and female farmers is not only due to differences in access to productive inputs but their produce in the same way as men[4]. The gender differences in agricultural productivity is not specific to cocoa. Indeed, a study conducted in 2008 concluded to a general disparity of women's productivity compared with men's. This can be explained by the lower quality and quantity of women's farm plots than that of their husbands, for instance a lower investment in improving soil quality or access to fertilizers.

### The Importance of Women in Agriculture

Farming is important given the major role women are playing in the agriculture value chain, from farming to food processing and marketing. However, they face obstacles to access funding, credit, insurance, land tenure security, and capacity in elaborating bankable business plans, limited access to information on how and where to access finance, lack of guarantee for climate change related risks, mind-sets about the agriculture; etc.

considered as for household needs, etc. Access to credit and other financial services is a serious constraint to many actors in the agriculture sector especially the smallholder farmers, who are mostly women, for whom funding from the banks is simply out of reach. Around 90 percent of women-owned businesses lack access to formal credit facilities. The most common sources of finance for women farmers are the predominant customs of informal micro-institutions, but are thought to borrow very small amounts[2].

22. According to the Ghana Statistical Services, women have limited access to bank accounts, compared to men, especially in rural areas. Only 30.8% of women compared to 69.2% of men have an individual saving account. Furthermore, the main source of financing for women in agriculture comes from the traditional informal micro-institutions such as savings clubs and credit unions. According to the Ghanaian credit bureaus such as Banks, less than 43.9% of women have access to formal credit facilities. Regarding the use of financial services, only about 10.5% of women farmers in Ghana have access to agriculture sector is less than 4%. With only 13.7% targeting women lending for land, 27% for agricultural inputs and 68% for livestock[3]. Women are also more likely to be excluded from credit facilities.

In addition to this, women received the largest number of loans reduced with urban disparities of 61.3% to 50.9% respectively. This is due to the lack of collateral and due to the limited capacity in producing high-quality agricultural products. Women are also more likely to be excluded from credit facilities because of accomplishment and knowledge gaps that negatively affect their ability to prepare bankable business plans.

23. Women play a key role in agriculture value chains in Ghana, despite the gender inequalities that exist in the agricultural labour markets, along with access to and control over resources. Women are mainly engaged in small-scale agriculture production (e.g., cocoa, coffee, cashew nuts, yam, cassava, soybean and vegetables). In the production level of the value chains, women dominate small-scale agriculture production[4]. Women also play a key role as processors of agricultural products that can be added or is traditionally a "male-cultivated" product[5]. Moreover, women act as aggregators most often, traveling to the north of the country to buy food products from various producers and re-sell them in bulk to urban consumers. Women are also involved in the marketing process at all levels of the aggregation process but at lower volumes. Women play also the role of small-scale market vendors or unregistered traders who sell agricultural produce on the streets, markets, and other public places due to limited profit margins. Indeed, women are most of the time unable to buy important quantities of agricultural inputs such as fertilizers, seeds, and pesticides because they lack access to credit facilities. Exceptionally, in the case of "market queens", women may control all transactions pertaining to a particular commodity in a market, lead to have comparatively greater access to capital resources, strong connections with traders and other actors in the value chain, and influence supply and demand[6]. Despite their role in agricultural value chains, women are still facing several constraints that limit their participation. These include (i) limited access to credit facilities or capital, including land ownership; (ii) access to credit and financial services, in part due to limitation or lack of capital, (iii) skills and knowledge of agro-technology, (iv) extension services, (v) storage facilities and, (vi) primarily for aggregation, transportation infrastructure[7].

Legal and Policy Framework

26. The government's action revolves around the implementation of the National Gender Policy and the 2017-2023 National Plan for the Promotion of Women, Children and Adolescents (PNDES) to reduce gender inequalities by increasing the number of female business owners from 21% in 2015 to 50% in 2019, by promoting decent jobs opportunities for women and increasing their access to credit. The government has also developed a National Strategy for Women's Empowerment (SNPE) to promote gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls. Burkina Faso has also developed the Economic and Financial Sector Policy (POSEF) for the promotion of economic development, particularly in rural areas. The government has also adopted the national strategy for promoting entrepreneurship among young people and women, supporting specifically micro and small enterprises, and the national strategy for promoting entrepreneurship among young people and women, supporting specifically micro and small enterprises.

27. In addition to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and its optional Protocol, Burkina Faso has ratified the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC), and its Optional Protocols, as well as the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights (Achille Mupito Protocol). Moreover, the country has also ratified the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR) and the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (ICESCR).

28. Burkina Faso has adopted laws and Codes that should guarantee equal rights to both women and men for accessing services and resources. For instance, there are the Law No 034-2012 AN on agrarian and land reform, which aims at ensuring access to land for all citizens; the Law No 058-2016 AN on rural land tenure advocating equitable access to rural land for men and women; the Law on violence against women, and the Law No. 034/2012 AN on agrarian reform and land rights, which aims at ensuring equal access and occupation. Regarding land law, the law 034/2009 on rural land tenure advocates that managed land be open to all citizens, including women and men, and that it should not be a factor of discrimination against women. Thus, the application of prison sentence for rape, introduced explicitly in the penal code in 2015, began to be applied in 2016. Furthermore, the Constitution recognizes the promotion of gender as a factor for realization of the equality of law between men and women.

28. The law allows a girl to be married at 17 against a legal minimum age of 20 for men. No less than 44% of married women were under 18, compared with 3% of men. It is up to each woman the average person to state that a man can beat his wife for one reason or another, while only 4% believe it is justified. ### Institutional Arrangements

29. At the institutional level, the Ministry of Women, National Solidarity, and Family is the institution responsible for promoting gender equality and empowering women, while ensuring the implementation of the national gender policy. The ministry has a secretariat with a deputy director in charge of coordination to coordinate line ministries' activities towards gender equality and women's empowerment. The ministry is carrying out its mission through the support of other institutions such as the National Commission for Women's Entrepreneurship, and the Permanent Secretariat of the National Council for Gender Promotion. The Ministry of Women, National Solidarity, and Family has a secretariat in charge of coordinating the activities of Burkina Faso regarding the Advancement of Women (CNEF), an advisory body consisting of representatives from civil society organizations, government institutions, and other stakeholders with a mandate to support the country's commitments on women's advancement. Furthermore, there are gender-mainstreaming units at the level of line ministries and decentralized structures in charge of implementing projects well mainstreamed while elaborating policies at the sectoral level. At the local level, the ministry is represented through decentralised services in charge of the coordination, monitoring and evaluation.

30. Despite all these institutional arrangements, the initiatives undertaken since the creation of the Ministry in terms of gender mainstreaming have not produced the expected results. There is a lack of mechanisms for the implementation of gender equality policies at national level (the National Assembly) and the regional and municipal councils for the promotion of gender. 2) The gender units provided for or created by line ministries are often underfunded, with limited resources from international donors (World Bank, UNDP, UNFPA, etc.) and the TFP Common Gender Fund. The main reasons for this situation include a lack of political will to implement gender equality policies at national level and institutional capacity and the lack of allocations to drive the implementation process. Civil society organizations of women in civil society suffer from their weak structure and the absence of a national coordinating umbrella structure.
