Education

31. Girls' education in Burkina Faso has been limited due to gender divisions and much-needed improvements in infrastructure. However, several efforts by aid organizations have worked to improve the access that girls have to education in the country. The government in Burkina Faso recognizes the importance of education for women's empowerment and has set a goal of 70 percent literacy among women by 2015. This has been a goal of these organizations. The Pan-African Conference on the Education of Girls as early as 1984, and the National Policy on Education for Girls in 1993, the National Policy on Education and the National Policy of Integrated Development of Children, which outlined a plan for 70 percent enrollment by 2015. have been specifically designed to address issues of education in the country over the past decade. The literacy rate among women aged 15 years or older has increased from all of 0.94, up from 0.7 in 2000. However, from 2008 to 2012, a longer analysis of net participation, the female-to-male ratio was 0.63.

32. According to UNESCO the education system is characterized by geographical disparities both in terms of enrollment rate and its infrastructure coverage. There are also disparities related to gender (55% of girls aged between 7-14 years old do not attend school). The region around Burkina Faso in the North and East is extremely volatile and unrest in the region around Burkina Faso has driven 25,000 refugees into neighboring countries. This has led to a significant increase in the number of children affected by militant extremism[3]. In that environment, students are afraid to go to school, and girls are among the most vulnerable. While approximately 15,000 schools have been closed, leaving 120,000 children without access to formal education, the government works hard to re-open the schools.

Employment

33. Unequal access to education limits women's opportunities in the area of skilled employment while they are also limited by their lack of access to land and other resources for agricultural production, processing and marketing of their agricultural products. In addition to these economic activities, the burden of household chores falls on them as well. The combination of the low level of educational quality and size of these tasks greatly reduces women's free time, which does not allow them to devote themselves entirely to their own development or that of their children. It is estimated that a woman and that of her family, Thus, the number of hours worked per week in rural Burkina Faso is 96 hours while that of men is 58 hours.

34. According to the National Survey of Manpower in the Public and Private Sectors (2015), the participation rate is higher for men than for women: 76.8% for the former and only 60.2% for the seconds. Women generally occupy positions at the bottom of the ladder because of socio-cultural barriers, hence their low level of education and their lack of access to land and other resources for agricultural production.

Health

35. The government of Burkina Faso has adopted a National Policy of Health (Politique nationale de santé) and has implemented activities based on the National Development Plan for Health (Plan national de développement pour la santé). Maternal mortality is considered as one of the major health issues, as it is an important issue and clarifies that the cause of maternal death is HIV/AIDS, malaria, anaemia, and hemorrhagic anomalies. It also presents the practices that have a negative impact on women,

including FGM, early marriage, levirate and demands for a widow to marry her husband’s brother, abortion, difficulties in accessing health facilities, and the quality of health services**.

### Access to land ownership

The agro-fishery private sector employs nearly 90% of the population and accounts for 33.7% of GDP in 2016 (ADB, OECD, UNDP, 2017). Women account for 52% of household farm workers and work more hours than men on their own land or rented land. However, women have less access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, tools, and credit. They also face restrictions in the processing and marketing of farm products. Access to land ownership is still a challenge for women. They are often not recognized as owners by customary law and community management practices. Even when they are land owners, their decision-making power remains limited. Only 14% of female land owners have the opportunity to sell their land, compared to 32% of men, because of customary law and community management practices. Households headed by women spend more time on unpaid domestic work (hours, days). In 95% of cases, while 21.5% of households headed by men in comparable situations use plows, only 60% of those headed by women do so.

### Access to financial services

Two-thirds of those with an account at a bank or other financial institution are men, while only 15% of the population say that men and women should not have the same decision-making power regarding financial services. Women access to agricultural credit is limited because of lack of collateral in the area of skilled employment while they are highly integrated in the informal sector. Women are responsible for all the production, processing and marketing of their agricultural products. In addition to these economic activities, the burden of children and the exclusivity of domestic tasks

The multiplicity and size of these tasks greatly reduce women’s free time, which does not allow them to devote themselves entirely to certain income-generating activities that can raise their standard of living and that of their families. Côte d'Ivoire Gender Policy

41. The government of Côte d’Ivoire adopted the National Policy for Equalities of Chances, Equity and Gender (Politique Nationale de l'Égalité des chances, L'Équité et le Genre) in 2009 to promote gender equality and subsequently implemented various activities. This strategy aims to ensure equal opportunities for women and men in development and decision-making processes. Four priorities are set: governance and public service; education and health; capacity building and access to basic social services (education and health) as well as capacity building and monitoring and evaluation mechanisms.

42. About 60% of women work in the informal economy compared to men, while women earn less, save less and are more likely to fall into poverty. In Côte d’Ivoire, women suffer many inequalities and the informal sector accounts for 85% of employment, with women making up 44%. In rural areas, women have limited access to education and health services. The gender gap in education is large; girls are less likely than boys to complete primary school. While the number of girls’ enrollment in primary education is increasing, the disparity between urban and rural areas is large. Girls drop out more often from school because they must help with household chores or care for younger siblings. Further advocacy activities are needed to make parents understand the importance of girl’s education. Moreover, many girls drop-out of school due to pregnancy and early marriage. The government has launched a program to provide free sanitary pads for girls.

43. Since the end of the socio-political crisis, Côte d’Ivoire has reformulated many taxes and adopted several new public policies to address gender inequalities. The 2018 Constitution provides that the state must ensure equal opportunities between men and women in all areas of life. It may not discriminate against the basis of gender. It also provides that the State shall promote parity between men and women in all areas of life, including in the exercise of public functions, as well as responsibilities in public institutions and administrations, as well as at the corporate level. National Development Plans (PND) are to be reviewed every five years with a view to ensuring gender equality in a society where good governance is guaranteed. In terms of social cohesion, the participation of women in local and national institutions is promoted; and in terms of the "standpoint of justice" the protection of women’s rights is ensured by law. The National Policy for Equalities of Chances, Equity and Gender has adopted policies and action plans to promote gender equality. It is important to assure the implementation of this policy through a monitoring mechanism. In 2018, the National Commission against gender based violence was established in 2012, and it is now in the implementation phase of the action plan for the fight against gender-based violence. The National Policy for Equalities of Chances, Equity and Gender was adopted in 2009 by the Council of Ministers as a way to evaluate and utilize the capacities and talents of women across the country. The National Policy on Equal Opportunities, Equity and Gender was adopted in 2009 by the Council of Ministers as a way to evaluate and utilize the capacities and talents of women across the country.

44. In 2018, Côte d’Ivoire launched a program to implement the Resolution 1325 of the Security Council on Peace, Women and Security as well as the National Policy for Equalities of Chances, Equity and Gender (Politique Nationale de l'Égalité des chances, L'Équité et le Genre) in 2009. This policy aims to ensure equal opportunities between men and women in all areas of life and to promote gender equality in the development and decision-making processes. Four priorities are set: governance and public service; education and health; capacity building and access to basic social services (education and health), and capacity building and monitoring and evaluation mechanisms. The

challenges for the future are to ensure the implementation of the 30% quota for elections and the review of the National Gender Policy adopted in 2009. The National Action Plan to Combat Gender-Based Violence began in July 2012. SNLVBG prioritized the prevention and protection of sexual violence over other various kinds of gender based violence. The strategy aims at preventing and combating gender-based violence, the protection of orphans due to HIV/AIDS and the reintegrating of victims into their families and communities. It is working under a legislative framework to encourage women's participation in the economy. Although the legal, regulatory and policy frameworks in Côte d'Ivoire is large in line with international best practice regarding the protection of women's rights, awareness and enforcement of these rights remains low.

Gender related laws and regulations:

46. The Côte d’Ivoire government has adopted various laws and regulations in order to reduce the incidence of violence against women.

Constitutional Law of 08 November 2015

The law no. 2016-885 of 8 November 2016 establishing the Constitution of the Republic of Côte d’Ivoire which guarantees and protects women’s rights to equality in access to education, working conditions and equal pay with men in public life and in the labour market. The country has also adopted law no. 348-88 of 20 June 2014 on the promotion and protection of the rights of women and girls in Côte d’Ivoire. This law is based on the Convention No. 9 and Decree No. 2017-121 on its application. Moreover, the government also adopted the International Convention for the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) as well as the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol). The Government has yet to ratify the Optional Protocol to CEDAW.

Women’s rights to property and land use in the Civil Code, Labour Code and Family Code

The following laws are in force:

• Law No. 83-000 of 2 August 1983 amending and completing the provisions of Law No. 64-375 on marriage of 7 October 1964.

• Law No. 93-001 of 23 December 1993 amending and completing Law No. 64-376 of 7 October 1964 in divorce and legal separation, amended and completed by Law No. 83-001 of 2 August 1983.

• Law n° 97-400 of 11 July 1997 as amended by Law n° 2015-532 of 20 July 2015 on the Labour Code

• Law n° 99-477 of 02 August 1983 as amended by Ordonnance n° 2012-03 of 11 January 2012 on the Social Security Code.

• Law No. 85-006 of 19 September 1985, amended by the law of 29 July 2004 on rural land tenure

48. The first strategy in the National Development Plan (Plan national de développement) for 2013-2015 is to live in harmony in a secure society where good governance is ensured. This strategy includes several components: consolidation of peace and social cohesion; army, gendarmerie, police, and prison reform; strengthening of justice institutions; development of the economy, communication, and development of national static systems. In regards to social cohesion, women's participation in national and local institutions is being promoted. From the standpoint of justice, the fight against impunity and corruption is a priority. The disarmament, demobilization, and reintegration (DDR) process for ex-combatants in Côte d'Ivoire (Disarmement, Demobilization, and Reintegration) has been implemented following the end of the civil war. DDR is a complex process that involves the identification of all combatants who are ex-combatants or supported armed forces. Generally, the term DDR tends to refer to male ex-combatants, although women have also participated in the conflict. The DDR process targets those who were engaged in actual combat. However, it is estimated that about 450 female ex-combatants could be targeted under DDR. Although the conflict has ended and there are no more armed groups, many ex-combatants continue to carry weapons, and many assaults using these arms still persist. In areas where saw intense combat, sexual violence occurs in a context of impunity. The victims of such crimes are often unable to report their complaints; the victims are unable to take action because of their fear. Consequently, the assailants are not punished and repeat the same offenses.

Access to justice

49. In Côte d'Ivoire, since the cost of judiciary procedures is high, those who do not have economic power, including both men and women, do not have access to the courts. However, since women


---
**Page 205**
---

are vulnerable and have a low literacy rate, they have much greater difficulty in accessing justice than men. The protection of victims is a big concern as well. Many victims do not know about their rights, few resort to legal procedures. Also, few lawyers exist, and they tend to be based in Abidjan. In addition, the judicial system is often slow and inefficient, which can be detrimental for the victims. For example, the trial should take place behind closed doors, and the victims should not be at the same room as the assailant. Nevertheless, trials are not closed, and during the testimony of the victim, the accused is present in the courtroom. In addition, many women who suffer from GBV need a medical certificate as evidence of the violence they suffered. However, it costs 50,000 CFA francs to obtain this certificate, which is prohibitive for most victims. As such, many victims do not go to a medical facility to have this expensive medical certificate issued and, moreover, bringing the case to justice is often impossible because the accused is not brought before the court. Many cases of GBV are not brought to justice, which substantiates the idea that acts of violence are not punished. This phenomenon stimulates the violence and creates a vicious circle of impunity and violence. In the meantime, the Ministry of Solidarity, Family, Women and Children has set up the Association des Femmes juristes de Côte d'Ivoire with assistance from donors who are noteworthy example. These initiatives aim to provide necessary information and support to victims of GBV.

### Institutional Arrangements

50. The Ministry of Solidarity, Family, Women and Children is the institution responsible for the promotion of gender equality and women's empowerment. The Ministry has a Directorate of Equality and Gender (DGE) which is in charge of: (i) promoting policies and programmes to respect equality and equity between women and men; (ii) implementing the national policy on equal opportunities, which includes the implementation of the National Action Plan for Equal Opportunities with the Ministry of Justice and Human Rights; (iv) monitoring the actions of the National Programme for Gender Equality (PNGE); (v) promoting gender mainstreaming in all areas (e.g. gain mothers, widows and women victims of domestic violence); (vi) ensuring systematic integration of gender equality into the content of national policies, plans, programmes and projects; The National Committee to Combat Violence against Women and Children (CNLVE) is active and produces much appreciated results. The Women's Training Centre (CETF) is also an important institution in this field.

51. Furthermore, there are gender units within Technical Ministries, following the ministerial decree under the Ministry of Solidarity, Family, Women and Children. Those units are responsible for ensuring that gender equality is mainstreamed into all policies and programmes. These units have contributed to the production of various national gender promotion documents. An independent evaluation carried out by the National Institute of Statistics (INSD) has shown that the presence in the technical ministries as an approach to promoting gender mainstreaming in the sectors. There can be no doubt about the importance of this sector for the field of gender promotion, women empowerment, and its fight against gender-based violence.

52. Despite the existence of institutional mechanisms towards gender mainstreaming and gender equality, there are still many weaknesses that need to be addressed. These weaknesses include a lack of system of accountability to the different gender structures for gender promotion and the limited impact of the gender units in the sectorial ministries. A Gender Observatory is one of the solutions proposed by the Ministry of Solidarity, Family, Women and Children. This tool consists of the collection of policies, plans, programmes, projects and activities of public or private organisations. Moreover, the creation of a national committee for gender equality would be another solution to ensure the competence, especially in the case of overlapping missions. In addition, the framework for collaboration between the Ministry of Solidarity, Family, Women and Children and other ministries in charge of gender equality. In the Ministry in charge of gender, the gender aspect is drawn in the mission statement and in the structure of the ministry. The same applies to the fields of social solidarity and social cohesion, the family, children and war victims. Another weakness is also related


---
**Page 206**
---

To the lack of synergy at the field level between the interventions of different ministries, or those supported by different partners working towards gender equality.

**Education**

53. In 2017, the enrollment parity index (girls to boys) was 0.98 in primary, 0.87 in secondary, and 0.81 in upper secondary education. The low enrollment of girls is more acute for young participants: about 27 percent of working-age women are not active in the labor market, compared to 19 percent of men. School completion rates for girls was lower than boys in 2017 due to higher dropouts on account of early marriage and pregnancy. In 2016, 35 percent of married women aged between 20-24 years were pregnant. According to the latest 2018 household survey, working-age women are much less likely to be employed in formal sectors than men (69 percent vs. 78 percent). They also have significantly lower wages in salaried employment.

54. The proportion of female teachers is also low at 13.7%. The government has set a policy to increase the number of girls attending school, but this is not always possible for young women living in urban areas; so many girls must leave home to attend school. This situation hinders girls in rural areas from completing their education and entering the labor market. In addition, there are no schools where they get pregnant and drop out of school. In these cases, the parents would never want their other girls go to secondary school. Given this situation, the government has laid out a policy to promote the construction of high schools in urban areas so that young women can continue studying during school and to assure a favorable environment in which girls can study and live. Also, at least one new school is planned for each district. In addition, the government recognizes that they should be built near residential areas, and around 40 high junior secondary schools are to be constructed across the country.

**Health**

55. Access to prenatal and maternal care and contraceptive methods is a major public health challenge. In 2015, only 57% of births were attended by skilled birth personnel; 89% of women received medical care during pregnancy, but only 63% of pregnant women aged between 15-49 years used any contraceptive method (GGGR 2015). The maternal death rate is high in Côte d'Ivoire, and represents a major public health challenge. In 2015, the maternal mortality ratio was 786 per 100,000 live births. HIV/AIDS is prevalent in young women and men ages 50 and older. It is expected that the government's efforts to reduce the prevalence of HIV/AIDS will continue to be effective as it had been decreasing since 1998. However, this downward trend reversed during the period of internal conflict, and currently, there are even cases of FGM in Abidjan, an area where FGM was not previously practiced. The number of deaths from malaria has decreased significantly over the past 25 years, from 754 maternal deaths per 100,000 live births in 1990 to 643 in 2015 (World Bank 2015). It remains a major public health challenge. The number of cases of malaria is related to pregnancy or childbirth in Côte d'Ivoire, mostly due to preventable or treatable medical problems.

56. The government of Côte d’Ivoire aims at making the country a place where “no woman dies when giving birth and every person is born healthy and lives healthy sexual and reproductive lives.” This objective is part of the National Strategy for the Promotion of Sexual and Reproductive Health, which includes all services. Specifically, matters related to women’s health have been included in the National Policy of Population and Family Planning. The government has also set up a National Council on FGM to address the prevention of HIV/AIDS. The government holds a High-Level Council for the Fight against HIV/AIDS (Conseil National de Lutte contre VIH/SIDA), which was last held in September 2018. This council is composed of representatives from all sectors and is designed to be a forum for vulnerable groups. It is important to pay sufficient attention to nutrition before becoming pregnant as it is the most critical period for the development of the fetus. In Côte d’Ivoire, 35% are underweight, and 59% lack sufficient iron. Most cases of obesity are found in urban areas. Against


---
**Page 207**
---

This backdrop, the Ministry of Agriculture supports female cooperatives and distributes food and seeds in the regions that face malnutrition. In addition, the government plans to support mothers with HIV as breastfeeding can pass the HIV virus to the child.

### Agriculture

57. The women crop-subsector occupies 85% of the agricultural labour force, 90% of whom are women. In addition to food crops, women are also involved in small cattle breeding and processing and marketing of by-products, and they represent 2/3 of the agricultural labour force.Agricultural production is mainly based on subsistence farming. Women grow staple food crops such as cassava, maize, bananas and other vegetables. On the other hand, men grow cash crops like cocoa, coffee, palm oil and rubber. In addition to these two different roles, women grow crops for self-support, such as cassava, maize, bananas and other vegetables. On the other hand, men grow cash crops like cocoa, coffee, palm oil and rubber. In addition to these two different roles, women grow crops for self-support, such as cassava, maize, bananas and other vegetables.

58. The law on ownership of land is discriminatory against women and does not allow them access to land for both men and women. Law No. 97-013 December 24, 1997 on rural land ownership (Article 6 stipulates that any individual entitled is entitled to own land. This law establishes equal access to land for men and women of human nationality. In reality, women can only acquire land in urban areas. In rural areas, local customs do not allow women to inherit or buy land. Women are therefore excluded from the right to inherit or buy long-term. In addition, the law of succession permits equal rights for both sexes, as customary norms and practices continue to exclude women from inheritance. The government has adopted a policy to promote land ownership by women and conducted advocacy activities. As a result, some women have acquired land in urban areas through inheritance or purchase. However, they cannot engage in large-scale agriculture such as plantations. Since women do not own any land, when the operators of these plantations are usually men, women cannot benefit from the occupation. Moreover, they cannot object, even if it is against their will and prejudicial to them. Mostly farmers (in rural areas), women gain access to land through the customary law system, which allows them to inherit or buy land. Women who grow food crops in particular have easier access to land for off-season crops, unlike for permanent crops. Regardless of the mode of acquisition, women's access to land is less than 10% of the national average.

### Employment

59. The informal employment rate in Côte d'Ivoire, according to the Integrated Regional Survey on Employment and the Informal Sector (ER-ESI), was 77% in 2017, reflecting a small decline from 96.6 percent in 2014. In general, low-productivity occupations are disproportionately occupied by women. Women's participation in agriculture is particularly high. Women are highly vulnerable to adverse market and weather conditions and a drop in their income has an immediate impact on the family. In addition to working as agricultural workers, especially in the private sector, many women work in agriculture, informal sectors, and service sectors. Younger women tend to work as secretaries. Thus, the government plans to increase the number of women in management positions (16,870 female managers), 37% for middle-level positions (74,960 female managers) and 36.1% for executive level positions (25,000 female executives). Women's participation is particularly high in the public sector, which is outside of these rankings50. An in-depth analysis of the mechanisms that lead to women's underrepresentation in management positions shows that many women have delayed women and girls' gains into formal education, which in some cases limits their


---
**Page 208**
---

Access to credit

60. With limited access to land and no resources, women face difficulties in accessing credit. Many micro credit institutions start out offering loans that are unviable to women, but when the micro-credit institutions become more established they can offer much stronger loans. The government has introduced credit programs that maintain loan conditions favorable for women, and this is one of major concerns for women's access to credit.

Representation in the decision-making process

61. Stereotypes against women, general societal attitude among women, low literacy rate, and inadequate education have been pointed out as reasons why women's participation in the decision- making process has been limited. The law No 2013-533 of January 2013 on marriage and family law changed the role of women within the family (Law No. 2013-533). The new law states that the spouse, who provides for the education of children, is responsible to the household expenses according to their respective abilities, manage the family jointly. It also provides that the wife has the right to work and earn money in addition to her duties within the family. The reform also gives each of the spouses the right to exercise the profession of their choice. Côte d'Ivoire has taken several actions to promote gender equality in public institutions. However, women are no longer considered a secondary aspect, but is integrated into public policies and sectoral programs. Several actions have been carried out to promote gender equality in public institutions. However, there still exist some synergies between them. Under the impulsion of UN agencies, several actions being carried out to promote gender in public institutions, but this is still very timid, even if certain ministries (Women's Affairs and Family) have taken action.

Traditions and social norms

62. There are various ethnic groups in Côte d'Ivoire, each with a different language, culture and customs, and the social status of women is different in each group. For example, Akan maternal and patrilineal societies, which are found mainly in the south of the country. However, generally, the social and economic position of women in Côte d'Ivoire is low, and their access to social services and public policies is limited. Women face a high risk of sexual violence during pregnancy and throughout their education. The sexual abuse of which they are victims is frequently accompanied by physical violence. In addition, women are often the victims of harassment and violence throughout their education. The perpetrators are usually men or other employees of the school, soldiers, drivers, shopkeepers or other students. According to a 2016 study for the Ministry of Education, 4 out of 10 students, especially girls, have experienced sexual abuse at school. This is often accompanied by violence sometimes committed by boys (UNICEF 2016/07). The government has clearly stated that this practice is unacceptable and has taken several actions to combat it, including the introduction of the practice of FGM by law. The government also conducts advocacy activities such as a special week against gender-based violence in schools. In addition, the government has launched a national awareness campaign on health and hygiene. In 2014, Côte d'Ivoire developed a National Strategy Document on the fight against gender-based violence (SDSNVB) through the Ministry of Social Affairs, Family, Women's Affairs and Children's Rights, in collaboration with UNICEF, UNFPA, ActionAid, NGOs and civil society. A Gender-Based Violence Information Management System (GBVIMS) has been set up as a tool to monitor the implementation of the cross-cutting theme related to GBV data

collection. As of April 1, 2019, Côte d'Ivoire has fifty-nine (59) platforms for the fight against GBV in different localities.

## Mail Gender Profile

### According to the Gender Inequality Index (GII), Mail is one of the ten African countries where the disparities between the sexes are the highest. The GII decreased significantly over the 2000-2010 period, but no significant improvement has been noted since 2010. This index offers a composite measure of gender inequality in three areas: empowerment, the labor market and reproductive health.

### In Mail, women have less access to resources than men. Women represent more than half (51%) of the population lives in rural areas where the national electrification rate is only 28%. The country has made progress on the MDGs, but the 2013 National Energy Consumption in 2016 established 77% of biomass use by women, the more vulnerable group. This situation makes it difficult for women to access energy services and to have a major component to improve women's access to appropriate technologies that are less costly and less wood-energy use.

### This national policy sets the country’s vision and strategic directions in terms of priorities and objectives related to gender equality. The implementation of this policy, including laws and back donors at the national level must be aligned in order to optimize the results and changes in the lives of women and girls. The National Gender Policy is based on three pillars:

#### Pillar 1: A democratic society that guarantees the development of women and men through the full exercise of their rights and freedoms, and which ensures equal opportunities for all citizens.

#### Pillar 2: An economy that provides women with access to resources, with a view to making Mail an emerging 5 country strong in its growth and proud of its values. This pillar is based on three components:

- Strengthening the role of women in economic activities.
- Improving the quality of men and women life, and globally the future of the planet: In this case, Mail is currently facing significant environmental problems such as desertification, floods, and rapid population growth.
- Promoting the development of women's entrepreneurship.

#### Pillar 3: A society that guarantees equal opportunities for all men and women in rural areas (Mail, BOAD).

### At the international and regional levels, the country has ratified several conventions. For instance:

- Mail has ratified the CEDAW, the 2003 Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Elimination of一切形式的歧视，the 1985 Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women, and the 1976 Optional Protocols to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women.
- Mail is a party to the African Court of Justice and Human Rights. However, it has yet to enact legislation at the national level. Indeed, the African Court on Human and Peoples’ Rights found the Malian Government in violation of its obligations under Article 14 of the Convention on the Elimination of一切形式的歧视，which does not require consent for marriage, and discriminating against women in inheritance statutes in the rural areas.
- The National Gender Policy (PNG) is a strategic document that sets out the priorities of the Ministry of Women’s Affairs and Family Development. It aims to promote gender equality through the implementation of the following actions: promotion of women's entrepreneurship; strengthening the role of women in economic activities, etc.

### Mail has also adopted several laws and regulations aimed at promoting gender equality:

- The Law No. 2015-036 on the Promotion of Women’s Empowerment (LPEM) sets out a series of measures to promote women's empowerment.
- The Law No. 2014-089 on the Promotion of Women’s Entrepreneurship (LPME) aims at promoting entrepreneurship among women and providing them with access to resources, including financial services.
- The Law No. 2015-036 instituting measures for promoting women's empowerment and access to functions normative and elective (out of at least 30% representation of the other sex in appointments and on the boards of public and private entities).
- The principle of the primacy of universalism is enshrined in the Constitution, which guarantees equal rights and opportunities for all. The Constitution grants duly ratified and published international treaties and agreements binding force greater than that of national laws.

### Poverty

65. The poverty rate is estimated at 43.8% in 2015, compared to 44.9% in 2017 and 47.2% in 2015 according to the results of the EMOP. During the first decade of the 2000s, a reduction in poverty had been observed (from 63.8% in 2000 to 59.5% in 2007). This trend was reversed after 2007 using the data from INSTAT. According to the World Bank, the results obtained in terms of poverty reduction over this period are not significant (from 43.8% in 1996 to 43.5% in 2011). This is due to a redistribution between households. **This redistribution could also be explained by the growth of the economy estimated at an average rate of 7.5% per year over this period, which has allowed the population in Mali where, between 1995 and 2015, the incomes of the poorest 40% increased by 60 to 80 percentage points more than the average** However, the country experienced an increase in poverty during the economic crisis of 2013 and the drought of 2011. According to World Bank data, the percentage of households with income below the poverty line has increased from 45% in 2010 to 56% in 2017.

66. This regional distribution shows that monetary poverty disproportionately affects rural populations. Indeed, if poverty has recently decreased in Bamako and in other urban centers, it has increased in rural areas. Analysis of NDAT data on poverty from 2001 to 2017 shows that the poorest regions are those where the population is most concentrated (Bamako, Sikasso, Koulikoro). These are poor, followed by Ségou and Kouloura. The 2018 poverty incidence map confirms this geographical concentration of poverty in Bamako (54%), Sikasso (63%), Koulikoro (57%) compared to Bamako (10.6%) or other cities (29.8%). This precarious living conditions and the extent of poverty translate into poor human development. Indeed, Mali ranks among the last countries in terms of HDI with a score of 0.427 out of 1.000. However, the evolution of the HDI since 2000 shows no real improvement (from 0.395 to 0.427). The HDI has been stable between 2000 and 2010 and is followed by a slowdown from 2011. The decline in the HDI could be explained by the weak economic growth and the recession that marked the country from this year.

### Government representation


---
**Page 212**
---

**68.** The empowerment is measured by the proportion of seats held by women and the proportion of the population with at least secondary education started by gender. This female parliamentary representation decreased from 18 women between 1997 and 2002 to 15 women from 2002 to 2007 out of a total of 64 seats in the National Assembly. In 2013, there were only 10 female members deputies for 147 seats in the National Assembly. In Mali, as of February 2021, 27.3% of seats in parliament were held by women[89]. Inequalities are also observed at the level of elective functions, where women hold less than 5% of local government positions and only 4.6% of ministerial positions, despite Law No. 2015-052 instituting measures to promote gender in these functions with a minimum quota of 30%. In the 2019 legislative elections, which were held under the new framework of the government formed in May 2019, there were 09 women ministers out of 38 (23.68%), while no woman was elected to the presidency or vice-presidency of the National Assembly. At the communal level, women represent less than 2% of mayors and less than 9% of municipal councillors. The low visibility of women at the level of local governance, the judiciary, and also at the level of civil society organizations is another phenomenon in Mali[89].

**Institutional arrangements**

**69.** The Ministry for the Advancement of Women, Children and the Family was established by Decree No. 2017-345 of January 19th, 1997. Its responsibilities include: (i) formulating and implementing measures to ensure the wellbeing of women, children and the family; (ii) preparing and implementing policies for the protection of the rights of women, children and families; (iii) ensuring that women and children meet their specific needs; (iv) promoting the rights of women and children; (v) promoting the family. The government institutional framework for managing gender issues in Mali is coordinated by the National Committee on Gender Equality (CNEM), which includes the National Women's Development Directorate (NWAD), the National Child and Family Promotion Directorate (NCFPR), and the National Directorate for the Advancement of Women, Children and the Family (DNADCWCF) and the Regional Directorate for the Advancement of Women, Children and the Family (DRADCWF) and includes the National Committee on Gender Equality (CNEM). The government also has a national centre called the Children's Centre. This institutional framework, established by the government, is reinforced by the presence of a strong female civil society committed to supporting government actions (CMRAN, Réseau des Femmes de l'Association pour la Promotion de la Jeunesse et de la Culture). Women's organizations, including the Pan-African Women's Organization (PAWRO), the Network of African Women's Organizations (RENAFEM), the Association for the Advancement of Women in Mali (AMAF) and the West African Women's Association (WAWA) also support the activities of MNAG.

**70.** While there have been notable gains in overall enrolment levels and steps made to decrease gender disparities, there are still considerable gaps between literacy levels and enrolment rates between males and females, especially in rural areas. There are gender disparities about Education, particularly at the secondary level where girls are less likely than boys to be enrolled or complete their education. In basic education, these are reduced to 25% for girls at the end of basic education, compared to 41% for boys. The same is true in upper secondary education and vocational education; and in higher education. The school dropouts, which are frequent in the second cycle of basic education, are also linked to the adolescent crisis in addition to other social factors.

**71.** The significant withdrawal of girls from the education system is the consequence of, among other things, child marriage, rural exodus, parental poverty or the remodelling of school infrastructures

# Employment

Three types of inequalities between men and women exist in the labor market: In Mali, four out of ten women (40.1%) aged 15-64 were employed in 2017, just over half (51.2%) of women aged 15-24 and 47.7% of women 15-34 year olds were also employed. According to the results of the integrated regional survey conducted by the National Institute for Statistics and Economic Studies in Agriculture, Livestock, Hunting and support activities branch, however, the percentage of urban women aged 15–64 who are economically active is only 28%, compared with 39% for men. Among 15-34 year olds and 74.4% for 15-40 year olds in urban areas, women make up 50% of total employment in the catering sector, 62% in food processing and 66% in marketing. This preponderance of women in the non-agricultural sectors is confirmed by the differences in income compared to men: women earn an average monthly income of CFAF 138,400 for agricultural work, compared to CFAF 259,125 for non-agricultural work. Women also have the lowest percentages of employees working women, compared with Sikasso, Koulikoro and Mopti regions. In terms of employment in the public sector and the public sector, unlike the informal sector, which indicates a low level of qualification and a certain precariousness of their jobs. Gender inequalities are also evidenced by the differences in remuneration between men and women. According to the results of the integrated regional survey, the average monthly income of women (CFAF 40,275) is 2 times lower than that of men (CFAF 81,369). In addition, women earn an average monthly income of CFAF 138,400 for agricultural work compared to CFAF 259,125 for non-agricultural work. Women also have the lowest percentages of employees working women, compared with Sikasso, Koulikoro and Mopti regions.

# Health

Efforts have been made in the area of reproductive health given the decline recorded by its two measurement indicators: the maternal mortality rate and the adolescent birth rate. According to World Bank data, the maternal mortality rate is 368 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2015, compared to 465 and 582 in 2006 and 2001 respectively. There is also a drop in the

adolescent birth rate, from 189 births per 1,000 women aged 15-19 in 2000, to 181 in 2010 and 169 in 2017.

Gender Based Violence

75. Gender-based violence (GBV) constitutes a major risk for women and girls in Mali. According to ESSMA/VII 13% of women aged 15-49 have experienced sexual violence. There is an upward trend since the last survey, which was conducted at the same age group in 2006. The most frequent forms of gender-based violence are: domestic violence (78%), physical violence (58%), and emotional violence in 2006. Through the GBV information management system, the number of reported cases has increased from 13% to 49%. The main perpetrators of sexual violence are: husband (45%), ex-husband (19%), Koulouba (15%), Bamako (15%), Ségué (14%), while Tombouctou and Kidal with 7% each. In the region of Sikasso, the most frequent forms of gender-based violence are rape and discrimination; the most widespread in Mali, with more than one out ten women concerned by the DHH-SI. The rate has increased over the past five years, from 83% (MICS I) to 95% (MICS III). In the region of Kidal, the prevalence is 70%, while it is only 42% in Bamako and 61% before the age of 14 years old. The prevalence is high in the regions of Koulikoro and Sikasso (80%), while it is low in the regions of Mopti and Gao (53%). In the region of Kidal, the prevalence remains marginal in northern regions such as Kidal and Gao, where the prevalence is at most 1%. Early Marriage

76. Furthermore, early marriage is one of the most widespread discriminatory practices against girls in Mali. Although the legal age for marriage is set at 18 for girls (for boys it is 20), the results of the MICS survey (2015) show that 41% of women aged 15-49 have married before the age of 15. Among women who are currently married, 37% were married at a young age and 68% were married for those under 15 increasing from 14% to 16% between 2010 and 2015 according to MICS. This practice is observed in both rural and urban areas; the region of Sikasso has the highest average (29%), while it is only 8% for Bamako (for women aged 16 years). Mali is among the West and Central African countries with the highest rates of child marriage, knowing that the rate of this region is 50%. The early marriage of girls is a consequence of poverty, which forces families to marry off their daughters at an early age. This practice also reflects the fact that the poor economic life exposes them to the risks of dropping out of school, and there is a strong correlation between child marriage and low levels of education. In addition, it is observed in areas where girls have less secondary education. Reducing child marriage would have significant economic benefits for Mali, including increased earnings from women's work (10% increase) and reduced fertility rates (-7%). This discrimination is also that of the division of domestic tasks between girls and boys, especially in rural areas. Young girls spend 6 hours a day collecting water and 0.2 days collecting wood, compared to only one hour for young boys. The early marriage of girls also deprives them of education and other financial services. Despite the improvement in the number of women over 15 with a bank or mobile account (from 3% to 7%), it is still very low compared to men (20%). This represents an increase of 20 percentage points. In the area of agricultural finance, it is important to emphasize that women with land have more access than men and are better able to use their resources. They also have other productive resources such as land to develop certain specifications such as rice. In 2017, for example, female owners of rice plots were 14.71% in the Office Rice Mopti zone and 8% in the Office Riz Ségué zone.

# Agriculture

77. Mali’s economy is based on agriculture. It employs 80 per cent of the active population and contributes about 40 per cent GDP and 30 per cent of export revenues. Livestock production is the third largest contributor to export earnings, after cotton and gold; it is the largest livestock producer in West Africa. Mali has a large land area (about 12 million hectares), fertile soils, and significant water resources. In addition to significant non-perennial surface waters, the Niger and Senegal rivers provide perennial water supplies for irrigation.

78. The government of Mali recognizes that its agricultural sector is underdeveloped and offers an estimated irrigable potential of more than 2.2 million hectares. The government allocates more than 15 per cent of its national budget to agriculture and significant progress has been made in recent years, including the development of irrigation schemes.

79. Mali’s vast potential for agricultural and rural development has barely been tapped. Just 7 million ha are under cultivation (about 60 per cent of which is rainfed), with a total production capacity of less than 300,000 t of the 2.2 million ha of irrigable land that is being irrigated or semi-irrigated.

In the absence of adequate management, a high proportion of the available surface water is not benefiting agricultural development. The main constraints to irrigation are: (i) inadequate infrastructure; (ii) institutional and administrative constraints; (iii) lack of access to inputs such as seeds, processing and marketing infrastructure; (iv) limited access to markets for producers; (v) weak rural credit systems; (vi) the absence of a strong private sector and community-based institutions; (vii) insufficient incentives for private sector engagement; (viii) lack of secure land tenure, in particular access to land for women; (ix) underproduced populations; and (x) the marginalization of women in rural areas, which is reflected by their low representation in the country.

80. Mali has adopted a Law n° 2017-001 on agricultural land, which indicates the modalities of access to agricultural land and prioritizes conflict prevention. It pays particular attention to women and young people, allocating them 15% of the State’s land development. It has also enabled women to participate in decision-making bodies for the first time by giving them a seat among their interests in decision-making bodies. For example, to operationalize this law, Village Land Committees have been created at the level of each village and are responsible for land allocation and contributing to the prevention of land disputes.

This is all the more important since land disputes are one of the main causes of inter-ethnic and inter-communal violence especially in central and southern Mali. Another positive discrimination measure relates to the increase in the representation of women in land commissions, rural women in particular. One of the innovations of this law is that it provides for a provision on customary rights, which must be respected by all those with whom the customary authorities must deal**16**. Also: The Law recognizes the right of rural communities and indigenous peoples to their traditional lands and resources, as well as the customs and traditions existing in these communities. Such a provision could constitute a limit to tenure security for women who are not recognized as heads of household or landowners in certain customs and traditions.

However, despite the legal provisions, women continue to be disadvantaged compared to men. According to EAC-17, in most regions, land managed by women is less than 20% of total cultivated land. In addition, according to the same report, women heads of household own land that is about twice as small as male heads of households of the same age group and are represented by male heads of household under 30 years of age, against 1.45 for women of this age group.

# Senegal Gender Profile

## Legal and policy framework

The National Action Plan for Gender Equality in Senegal was implemented between 2005 and 2015, concentrating on increasing women's status in society, improving their economic independence, and raising awareness about the issues that are prevalent to Senegalese society. In 1999, the Criminal Code was revised to make tougher penalties for crimes against women. This revision allows for the punishment of invasions of privacy, sexual harassment, and domestic violence. Legal reform in Senegal has included a law on equality in elected public bodies and a stipulation that at least one-third of candidates must be women.

New policies provide free caesarean operations in public hospitals and extend social security grants to half of the population. The government's rural development programme is to lighten burdens on women working in rural areas. Senegal is committed to giving meaning to the empowerment of women, ensuring that women’s participation in society is not measured by their ability to bear children.

Ratification of the CEDAW in 1983 and the additional protocol to the African Charter on the rights of women in Africa in 2005 have been important steps towards achieving gender equality between sexes. Article 15 of the Constitution gives women and men the same rights in matters relating to marriage, divorce, and inheritance.

Access to land is familialized, and the management is the responsibility of the head of the family, always a man. A law adopted on 14 May 2010 establishes “absolute parity” in the selection of candidates for public office, with women representing at least one-third of all candidates listed.

Senegal has gained international recognition for its efforts towards political participation of women through the adoption of the Gender Party Law (2010). As a result of this law, which demands party on electoral lists, the proportion of seats held by women in national parliament has increased


---
**Page 218**
---

significantly from 19.2% in 2007 to 43% in the 2017 elections. This marks a major step forward in the struggle for gender equality and the promotion of women's rights and makes Senegal a leader in women's political participation in Africa. On 30 July 2017, about 6 million Senegalese citizens voted to elect their representatives to the National Assembly. The results were as follows: Women were elected compared to 64 women (39%) in 2012. Women represent 21% of ministers in the National Government (8 women out of 39 members).Women represent 49% of the world's population and 52% of its leaders, but only 23% of parliamentarians worldwide.

The election results show that Senegal is making progress on gender equality. However, it still has a long way to go in terms of women's political participation. Women are underrepresented in decision-making roles and their priorities and views are not always taken into account. For example, women tend to be more sensitive to issues of development and social inclusion than men.

Women's political participation is not only essential for the democracy and the legitimacy of a political system—it is also a strategic investment in the future of society. When women and men work together, and men and women can better reflect on and respond to the diverse needs of a society. Therefore, women's political participations bring benefits for the whole society. In fact, countries with high levels of gender equality have more stable governments, higher quality of governance, the quality of educational systems, infrastructure investment, and perceptions of corruption. However, despite these benefits, regarding governance and women political participation key challenges remain in Senegal:

* It remains unclear to only engage men in gender party and; more generally, in the struggle for gender equality.
* The Gender Party Law is quite unprolific among male Parliamentarian and not followed;
* The parliamentarians do not have the same understanding of the parity law and particularly the parity concept.

83. There are several institutions in charge of monitoring gender equality in Senegal. In particular the National Commission for the Promotion of Women's Rights (established by Decree No 2009-1047 of 15 September 2008), which monitors all violations of women's rights. However, this body cannot receive complaints from citizens. The National Observatory on Gender Equality (established by Decree No 2011-819 of 7 March 2011), which is tasked with monitoring, evaluating and making proposals for promoting parity between men and women in public policy. Other initiatives for the advancement of women's rights are also being implemented at national level. The government has adopted a strategy in accordance with the National Strategy for Gender Equality and Equity (CEDAW). In 2013, the government launched a gender equality action plan to implement this strategy. However, the financing mechanisms put in place to reduce inequalities. In the agricultural sector, incentives have been introduced to encourage women's participation in agriculture. The proportion of female farmers has risen from less than 5% in 2008 to at least 20%. These measures consist in allocating to women a quota of at least 15% of the installations to be carried out from surface water, 20% of the installations to be made from groundwater and 30% of the installations to be made by small-scale irrigation. To ensure better integration of gender in sectorial interventions, gender cells have been created in key ministries and at the level of the National Office for Water Management (ONDA). The government has also launched a pilot gender-sensitive budgeting exercise has been initiated since 2015, and to which 18 ministries have committed. This exercise is based on the recommendations made by the World Bank in the 2019 draft budget[6]. Women may uphold the norms that harm them because the social costs of doing otherwise would be unacceptably high. Even though a social norm may be harmful, it may give women a sense of security and belonging. This is particularly true when there are no alternatives to exchange for economic support (Sen and Oelkin, 2006). The social norms approach to elimination has been developed by the Gender Justice Project (GJP), an international research project launched in 1988 in Senegal and implemented by Tostan, a non-governmental organization (Dip


---
**Page 219**
---

Education

84. Senegal has made enormous strides in its education system, with primary school enrollment rates increasing nearly 50% since 1994; in that time, Senegal has almost reached gender parity in terms of primary school enrollment, with 77.3 percent girls enrolled compared to about 80% of boys. **Gender gaps** are still present at the secondary and tertiary levels, however, with girls lagging behind boys in completion rates in Senegal. According to UNESCO, primary education completion rates rose from 33 percent for girls in 1995 to 62 percent by 2017; for boys, this rate increased from 48 percent to 70 percent during the same period. However, gender gaps in secondary and tertiary education persist, with girls' completion rates in secondary education and enrollment rates in tertiary education lower than boys' rates. The Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) data show that only 15.3 percent of women aged 25–49 have a secondary education completion rate for secondary education was only 13 percent, compared to about 21 percent for boys. According to the World Bank, in 2017, the literacy rate among women aged 15 and older in Senegal (68.2%) is still lower than that of men (74.9%), although gender parity has been achieved in favor of girls in primary education, where for every 100 boys enrolled in grade one, there are about 103 girls. The proportion of women who are school learners is high. The scope of literacy, especially among women rural Senegal, is also symptomatic of the poor access to education. According to UNESCO, Senegal's literacy rate for the population aged 15 and older was 48% in 2017, with a gender gap of 36 percentage points (boys: 69.2 percent) and Dakar (8.19 percent) have the best literacy rates. In contrast, the regions of Matam (21.4%) and Kolda (15.2%) have the lowest literacy rate.

Key gender-based constraints in the education sector include: Social beliefs about appropriate life choices for girls that encourage early marriage and childbearing and discourage continued education; Social norms that place a high value on boys' education, while girls are expected to stay at home or be sent to marry those who then deploy the boys to log on the streets. Social beliefs about the appropriateness of child labor (boys and girls) to assist at home; Lack of adequate infrastructure (unreliable electricity supply, lack of water, poor sanitation facilities, etc.) in schools for girls; Educational policies that discriminate against pregnant girls attending school. Social beliefs as well as poor infrastructure that inhibit many women teachers from taking positions in rural schools.

Health

86. The 2005 Law in Relation to Reproductive Health recognizes reproductive health as a "fundamental and universal right guaranteed to all individuals without discrimination based on age, sex, marital status or social class." However, the law does not address the significant legal restrictions on young people's access to contraceptives and other basic services, such as pregnancy and STI testing, apart from a requirement that one be 15 or older to consent to HIV testing. The law also prohibits abortion for women under age 20 without parental consent and financial penalties. However, many young women resort to illegal abortion services, which often put their health at risk.


---
**Page 220**
---

Gender-Based Violence

87. Gender-based violence (GBV) remains a serious problem in Senegal. According to the 2005 DHS, “96% of adult women believe that men have a right to beat their wives, ranging from 37% in urban areas to 73% in rural areas. Among wealthier and more educated women, the percentage that believes this is lower (84%). In addition, 21% of women report having been beaten by their husbands at some point in their lives. Some women have been abused, and there are some serious institutional and social constraints surrounding medical and police producers. For example, the law apparently requires that to file a claim of rape, a woman must be accompanied by her husband or father. This is not always possible, and therefore, few cases are initiated. That certificate has a fee of 10,000 CFA; in some cases, an NGO might pick up the cost for a woman, but an advocacy effort is needed to address these costs and this requirement.”

Employment

88. In rural areas, the distribution of employment in different economic sectors reveals women’s involvement in agriculture, livestock farming and the environment where they represent 70% of the workforce. Women are more engaged in informal activities than men due to their lower level of inclusion and salary security. Results from three ANSD surveys show that women have lower activity and employment rates than men. Women represent only 22.8% of workers in the formal sector, with a higher proportion (30%) among those who work for themselves or are self-employed compared to 21.9% for men; for a difference that remains statistically significant even when integrating income from agricultural activities. This is due to the fact that women have less access to credit and the fact that women are less involved in household financial and budgetary decisions. Women are generally paid lower salaries than men, with an average monthly salary of 102,587 CFA for a man compared to 697,432 FCFA / month for a woman; the same is true for the category “middle managers and senior technicians” where the monthly remuneration is 684,493 FCFA for men against only 100,500 CFA for women. The lack of access to credit, combined with limited resources, increase the vulnerability of households headed by women. The rate of food insecurity in households headed by a woman is higher than that of households headed by a man according to World Bank (2018) data on women in Senegal still face gender-based discriminatory legal restrictions and discrimination in access to credit. In addition, the law does not provide for equal pay for work of equivalent value nor laws consuming discrimination based on gender in hiring, job promotions or dismissal. Moreover, discriminatory practices are also found in the labor market. The 2018 World Bank report notes that the constitution does not formally recognize, nor does it prohibit discrimination against women that may be practiced by employers. In addition, the unemployment rate for women is double that of men. While performance on the United Nations Development Programme’s (UNDP) Gender Equality Index shows progress in Senegal, this progress is rural areas due to issues with access to land meaningful employment and technology. Most of what needs to job creation will have to come from the private sector**.

89. Despite these efforts to promote gender disparities continue to exist, especially in the area of Agriculture and Rural Development. Access, control and securing factors of production, such as land, remains problematic for women. Indeed, the percentage of women who own their plots is only 13.8% compared: to 86.2% for men; 23.2% of women are renting land compared to 70.7% for men; 54.7% of women borrow land compared to 93.3% for men, and 14.3% of women do tenant

# Gender equality and women’s empowerment

## Achieving gender equity and women’s empowerment in Senegal

### Key barriers common to all target countries

1. The country assessments above indicate a series of common factors that must be taken into account in the programme's design and implementation to ensure that it is effective in building women smallholder farmers' resilience to climate change. Many are important barriers to women's economic empowerment.

   * **Gender division of labour:** Cultural beliefs and norms govern daily life in the rural areas. Women are responsible for most domestic tasks, including childcare and household chores, which is unpaid, and certain norms limit their engagement in economic activities outside the home. Only a small percentage of women have paying jobs or their own small businesses. They lack access to land and tenure security.
   * **Access to financing mechanisms:** Women face significant barriers when it comes to accessing credit and other financial services. This can be due to a lack of collateral, limited access to information about available resources, and gender biases in lending practices.
   * **Access to factors of production and extension services:** Women often have limited access to land, water, seeds, tools, and other inputs necessary for agricultural production. They also face barriers when it comes to accessing extension services that can help them improve their farming techniques and increase their yields.
   * **Effects of climate change:** Climate change is having a significant impact on agriculture in Senegal, particularly in rural areas where women are often the primary producers. This includes increased frequency and intensity of droughts, floods, and other extreme weather events, which can lead to reduced crop yields and income for smallholder farmers.
   * **Access to markets:** Women face barriers when it comes to accessing markets for their agricultural products. This can include a lack of access to transportation, limited knowledge about marketing strategies, and discrimination in the marketplace.

In rural areas, the distribution of employment in different economic sectors reveals women's involvement in agriculture, livestock farming and the environment where they represent 70% of the workforce.


---
**Page 222**
---

financial resources to invest in machinery, male family labour, technologies and crops that bring higher returns and in sustainable manner.

- Property rights and control over assets. Due to cultural beliefs and practices, only a small minority of women have access to land or other productive assets such as livestock and agricultural inputs. The lack of assets in their names is an important impediment to developing viable business and obtaining loans, as they have nothing to offer as collateral. This remains a major challenge for women’s financial inclusion.

- Lack or no awareness of financing opportunities and new instruments like green finance. Women are often unaware that there are financing options available from the banks with the financial institutions and men control resources. This is mainly due to lack of financial education and training, which is usually provided by the banks themselves. There is a need for microfinance, which could strengthen their resilience to climate shocks depending on the intensity and magnitude.

- Lack or access to knowledge particularly reliable climate information’s: when available Climate Information Services are critical for the creation of relevant, science-based information to inform decision-making processes. Women in rural areas who have limited education do not have access to climate information for decision-making processes in agriculture.

- Lack of education and training. Women with low levels of education, literacy skills than men and mortality rates are high. They also lack access to knowledge and information to develop projects for the banks and for MFIs.

- Based on experience, women are less likely to bank and MFIs consider smallholder agriculture as a high-risk sector and women are not attractive to banking. In situations where they do have access to credit, it is often at higher interest rates than men.

- Many women are subjected to various forms of gender-based violence: domestic violence, dowry demands, harassment and sexual abuse.

- Access to technologies: Evidence consistently suggests that male household heads and farmers adopt new agricultural production technologies faster than female heads and farmers across regions. This is due to a number of factors including the fact that men have greater access range of agricultural and rural technologies than male heads and plot-managers. The most commonly cited reasons for this are: (1) women have less time and labor constraints, relatively less access to funds and credit; limited information on new technology and training; more limited capacity and opportunity for participation in innovation and decision-making processes; and less access to extension services and services. These are influenced by weak design or implementation of legislation that protect rights and promote gender equality as well as deeply rooted social biases and cultural norms, limiting equal access and opportunities for women and men [2].

9. In order to address the gender gap in access to technology SDG4/EFF will ensure that women can participate equally with men in decision-making processes. Some other key recommendations follow be the following:

- Strengthening capacity of women and men farmers as innovators, evaluations of technologies and their role in innovation processes.

- Building measurable targets and strengthening the monitoring and evaluation to ensure that (1) women have equal access to agricultural extension services, information on new technologies and opportunities; (2) women and men can access and use these technologies; and (3) women and men benefit from these technologies.

93. Furthermore, women face multiple barriers when seeking remediation and justice. To ensure women have equal access to the Grievance Redress Mechanism and benefit from it equally, IFAD is committed to promoting a gender-sensitive approach in grievance redress. This involves, among other things:

- Ensuring gender diversity in grievance mechanism staff and providing them with gender sensitive communications training
- Involving gender specialists from all PFMUs within the banks and the baseline projects
- Ensuring that women are included as beneficiaries of the interventions, including women and marginalized groups, taking into account potential lower literacy levels
- Providing for independent, gender-sensitive investigations of violations and committing to addressing them in a timely manner through appropriate grievance resolution processes
- Protecting all people who register complaints from reprisals

Monitoring the grievance mechanism to ensure that access and remediation outcomes are provided on an equal basis.

94. If AD is recognized for addressing gender inequalities in rural areas. A recent report by our Independent Office of Evaluation showed that IFAD has been successful in addressing the root causes of gender inequality in rural areas, including women's empowerment. The next step towards this initiative will be implemented. The programme will build on this solid experience. It starts with a rigorous analysis of the context and the identification of key challenges and opportunities to address these includes, amongst others, multiple and complementary activities promoting women's economic empowerment, increasing access to credit for women, reducing women's workloads, support to women's organizations, working with men as critical as they often are the gatekeepers of customary practices that limit women's access to resources or public spaces, highly participatory approaches and culture sensitivity.

# Prevention of gender-based violence

GBV is an umbrella term for any harmful act that is perpetrated against a person’s will and is based on socially ascribed (i.e., gender) differences between males and females. It includes acts that inflict physical, sexual or mental harm or suffering; threats of such actions; coercion and other deprivations of freedom. GBV can be prevented through: (i) the use of Early Warning Indicators on sexual violence, (ii) the use of the early warning indicators to inform communities about their rights and responsibilities in relation to GBV, (iii) policy frameworks related to sexual violence and harassment in each country to inform communities of their rights and sensitize traditional leaders and communities (especially women’s groups) to refer victims for medical care and other services, (iv) ensures that the selected banks take into account specific measures to prevent and fight GBV.

The following table provides a summary of the key indicators against which to strengthen or not strengthen GBV prevention and response measures. However, the Gender Action Plan below also includes actions that will be minimally implemented at the country level.

The role of community leaders will be essential in the prevention of GBV, especially women leaders who could more easily gain the trust of potential survivors. The protection of victims, wherever possible, should be a priority for banks and other financial institutions. Banks, policy leaders, the media, the authorities and civil society have a significant role to play and can strongly influence the implementation of measures against GBV in their communities. It is also important that there are channels to make the complaint mechanism accessible and to collect the grievances of GBV victims within the framework of the program.
